The Complexity of Learning Temporal Properties
Benjamin Bordais, Daniel Neider, Rajarshi Roy

Abstract
We consider the problem of learning temporal logic formulas from examples of system behavior. Learning temporal properties has crystallized as an effective mean to explain complex temporal behaviors. Several efficient algorithms have been designed for learning temporal formulas. However, the theoretical understanding of the complexity of the learning decision problems remains largely unexplored. To address this, we study the complexity of the passive learning problems of three prominent temporal logics, Linear Temporal Logic (LTL), Computation Tree Logic (CTL) and Alternating-time Temporal Logic (ATL) and several of their fragments. We show that learning formulas using an unbounded amount of occurrences of binary operators is NP-complete for all of these logics. On the other hand, when investigating the complexity of learning formulas with bounded amount of occurrences of binary operators, we exhibit discrepancies between the complexity of learning LTL, CTL and ATL formulas (with a varying number of agents).



1
Introduction

Temporal logics are the de-facto standard for expressing temporal properties for software and cyber-physical systems.
Originally introduced in the context of program verification
[
28
,
14
]
, temporal logics are now well-established in numerous areas, including reinforcement learning
[
37
,
22
,
9
]
, motion planning
[
15
,
11
]
, process mining
[
12
]
, and countless others.
The popularity of temporal logics can be attributed to their unique blend of mathematical rigor and resemblance to natural language.

Until recently, formulating properties in temporal logics has been a manual task, requiring human intuition and expertise
[
6
,
36
]
.
To circumvent this step, in the past ten years, there have been numerous works to automatically learn (i.e., generate) properties in temporal logic.
Among them, a substantial number of works
[
26
,
10
,
31
,
23
,
38
]
target Linear Temporal Logic (LTL)
[
28
]
.
There is now a growing interest
[
35
,
30
]
in learning formulas in Computation Tree Logic (CTL)
[
14
]
and Alternating-time Temporal Logic (ATL)
[
1
]
due to their ability to express branching-time properties of multi-agent systems.

While existing approaches for learning temporal properties demonstrate impressive empirical performance, their computational complexity remains largely unexplored.
The only noteworthy related works are
[
16
]
and the follow-up work
[
24
]
.
They present
\mathsf{NP}
-completeness results for learning formulas in LTL and several of its fragments.

In this work, we extend the existing results to encompass a wider range of LTL operators.
Moreover, we extend the study to learning CTL and ATL formulas.

To elaborate on our contributions, we describe the precise problem that we consider, the fundamental
passive learning
problem
[
17
]
.
Its decision version asks the following question: given two sets
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{N}
of positive and negative examples of system behavior and a size bound
B
, does there exist a formula of size at most
B
satisfied by the positive examples and violated by the negative examples.

Our instantiation of the above problem varies slightly depending on the considered logic. Indeed, LTL-formulas express linear-time properties, CTL-formulas express branching-time properties, and ATL-formulas express properties involving on multi-agent systems.
Accordingly, the input examples for learning LTL, CTL and ATL are linear structures (equivalently infinite words), Kripke structures and concurrent game structures, respectively.
We refer to Section
2
for formal definitions and other prerequisites,

We summarize our contributions in Table
1
.

Our first result, illustrated in the left column, shows that without any restriction on the use of binary operators, the learning problem for any logic is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete, regardless of the binary operators allowed. The
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness results are (heavily) inspired by the proofs by
[
24
]
and (mostly) use reductions from the hitting set problem—one of Karp’s 21
\mathsf{NP}
-complete problem. The details of the proofs are given in Section
3
.

Unbounded

Bounded use of binary operators

use of binary

\lX\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}

\lX\notin\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}

operators

\{\lF,\lG\}\subseteq\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}

\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}=\{\lF\},\{\lG\}

\mathsf{LTL}

\mathsf{NP}

\mathsf{CTL}

\mathsf{NP}
-c

\mathsf{ATL}(2)

\mathsf{NP}
-c

\mathsf{P}
-c

Table 1:
Summary of the complexity results for learning LTL, CTL and ATL.
\mathsf{ATL}(k)
corresponds to learning ATL with
k
agents, while
\mathsf{ATL}
refers to learning ATL with the set of agents as input.
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
refers to the set of unary operators allowed.

In the search for logic fragments with lower complexities, we turn to formulas using only a bounded amount of binary operators, and unary operators in a set
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\{\neg,\lX,\lF,\lG\}
, depicted in the middle column. Note that the bound on the number of binary operators is fixed beforehand (i.e. it is part of the learning problem itself, not of the input). In this case, the complexity of the learning problems varies between different logics and unary operators. Importantly, we exhibit fragments where the learning problem is decidable in polynomial time. This is handled in Section
4
.

Note that this work largely extends a preliminary version
[
8
]
.

Related Works.

The closest related works are
[
16
]
and
[
24
]
.
Both works consider learning problems in several fragments of LTL, especially involving boolean operators such as
\lor
and
\land
, and temporal operators such as
\lX
,
\lF
and
\lG
and prove their
\mathsf{NP}
-completeness.
We extend part of their work by categorizing fragments based on the arity of the operators and studying which type of operators contribute to the hardness.
Moreover, there are several differences in the parameters considered for the learning problem.
For instance, the above works consider the size upper bound
B
to be in binary, while we assume
B
given in unary.
Considering size bound in unary is often justified since one may want to output a unary-sized formula anyway.
We discuss more thoroughly such differences in Section
3.1.1
.
Nonetheless, in addition to LTL, we widen the scope of the complexity results to CTL and ATL.

In the past, complexity analysis of passive learning has been studied for formalisms other than temporal logics.
For instance,
[
18
]
and
[
2
]
proved
\mathsf{NP}
-completeness of the passive learning problems of deterministic finite automata (DFAs) and regular expressions (REs).

When considering temporal logics, most related works focus on devising efficient algorithms for learning temporal logic.
Several works learn LTL (or its important fragments) by either exploiting constraint solving
[
26
,
10
,
33
]
or efficient enumerative search
[
31
,
38
]
.
Some recent works rely on neuro-symbolic approaches to learn LTL formulas from noisy data
[
23
,
39
]
.
For CTL, many works resort to handcrafted templates
[
13
,
40
]
for simple enumerative search, while others learn formulas of aribtrary structure through constraint solving
[
35
,
30
]
.

There are also works on learning other logics such as Signal Temporal Logic
[
7
,
25
]
, Metric Temporal Logic
[
32
]
, Past LTL
[
3
]
, Property Specification Language
[
34
]
, etc.

2
Definitions

Complexity classes

In this paper, we are going to show several completeness results. As can be seen in Table
1
, the complexity classes that we will consider are
\mathsf{L}
(logspace),
\mathsf{NL}
(non-deterministic logspace),
\mathsf{P}
(polynomial time), and
\mathsf{NP}
(non-deterministic polynomial time). Note that all the reductions that we will define are logspace reductions.

Some notations

We let
\mathbb{N}
denote the set of all integers and
\mathbb{N}_{1}
denote the set of all positive integers. Furthermore, for all
i\leq j\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
[i,\ldots,j]\subseteq\mathbb{N}
denote the set of integers
\{i,i+1,\ldots,j\}
.

Given any non-empty set
Q
, we let
Q^{*},Q^{+}
and
Q^{\omega}
denote the sets of finite, non-empty finite and infinite sequences of elements in
Q
, respectively. For all
\rho\in Q^{+}
, we denote by
|\rho|\in\mathbb{N}
the length of
\rho
, i.e. its number of elements.

Furthermore, for all
\bullet\in\{+,\omega\}
,
\rho\in Q^{\bullet}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, if
\rho
has at least
i
elements, we let:

•

\rho[i]\in Q
denote the
i
-th element in
\rho
, in particular
\rho[1]\in Q
denotes the first element of
\rho
;

•

\rho[:i]\in Q^{+}
denote the non-empty finite sequence
\rho_{1}\cdots\rho_{i}\in Q^{+}
;

•

\rho[i:]\in Q^{\bullet}
denote the non-empty sequence
\rho_{i}\cdot\rho_{i+1}\cdots\in Q^{\bullet}
, in particular we have
\rho[1:]=\rho
.

For the remainder of this section, we fix a non-empty set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
.

2.1
\mathsf{LTL}
: syntax and semantics

Before introducing
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas
[
29
]
, let us first introduce the objects on which these formulas will be interpreted: infinite words.

Infinite and ultimately periodic words.

Given a set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
, an infinite word is an element of the set
(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})^{\omega}
. Furthermore, we will be particularly interested in ultimately periodic words, and in size-1 ultimately periodic words. They are formally defined below.

Definition 1
.

Consider a set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
. An ultimately periodic word
w\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})^{\omega}
is such that
w=u\cdot v^{\omega}
for some finite words
u\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})^{*},v\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})^{+}
. In that case, we set the size
|w|
of
w
to be equal to
|w|:=|u|+|v|
. Then, for all sets
S
of ultimately periodic words, we set
|S|:=\sum_{w\in S}|w|
.

An ultimately periodic word
w
is of size-1 if
|w|=1
. That is,
w=\alpha^{\omega}
for some
\alpha\in 2^{\mathsf{Prop}}
.

Set of operators

The
\mathsf{LTL}
,
\mathsf{CTL}
and
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas that we will consider in the following will use the following temporal operators:
\lX
(neXt),
\lF
(Future),
\lG
(Globally),
\lU
(Until),
\lR
(Release),
\lW
(Weak until),
\lM
(Mighty release).

We let
\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}:=\{\neg,\lX,\lF,\lG\}
and
\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}:=\{\lU,\lR,\lW,\lM\}
denote the sets of unary and binary operators respectively. We also let
\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
denote the set of all logical binary operators, i.e. the classical operators along with their negations:
\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}:=\{\lor,\wedge,\Rightarrow,\Leftarrow,\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor},\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge},\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow},\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow},\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\}
.

Syntax.

For all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
, we denote by
\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
the set of
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas defined inductively as follows:

\displaystyle\varphi\Coloneqq p\mid\neg\varphi\mid*_{1}\varphi\mid\varphi*_{2}\varphi

where
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
,
*_{1}\>\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
and
*_{2}\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\cup\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
.

We define the size
|\varphi|
of an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula
\varphi
to be its number of sub-formulas. That set of sub-formulas
\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi)
is defined inductively as follows:

•

\mathsf{SubF}(p):=\{p\}
for all
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
;

•

\mathsf{SubF}(\bullet\varphi):=\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi)\cup\{\bullet\varphi\}
for all unary operators
\bullet\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
;

•

\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi_{1}\bullet\varphi_{2}):=\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi_{1})\cup\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi_{2})\cup\{\varphi_{1}\bullet\varphi_{2}\}
for all binary operators
\bullet\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}\cup\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
.

We also denote by
\mathsf{Prop}(\varphi)
the set
\mathsf{Prop}\cap\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi)
. Furthermore, we say that a set of propositions
Y\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}

occurs
in a formula
\varphi
if
Y\cap\mathsf{Prop}(\varphi)\neq\emptyset
. (This notation will also be used with
\mathsf{CTL}
and
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas.)

Finally, we let
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{Bin}}\in\mathbb{N}
denote the number of usage of binary operators in
\varphi
, i.e.:

|\varphi|_{\mathsf{Bin}}:=|\mathsf{SubBin}(\varphi)|

with

\mathsf{SubBin}(\varphi):=\{\varphi_{1}\bullet\varphi_{2}\in\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi)\mid\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi),\bullet\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}\cup\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}\}

Semantics.

We define the semantics of
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas. That is, given an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula
\varphi
and an infinite word
w\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})^{\omega}
, we define when
w\models\varphi
, i.e. when
\varphi
accepts
w
, (otherwise it rejects it). In order to give a semantics to all binary logical operators, when
w\models\varphi
is true, it is seen as the boolean value
\mathsf{True}
, and when
w\models\varphi
is false, it is seen as the boolean value
\mathsf{False}
. Then, for all
w\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})^{\omega}
, we have (for
*\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
):

\displaystyle w\models p

\displaystyle\text{ iif }p\in w[0],

\displaystyle w\models\neg\varphi

\displaystyle\text{ iif }w\not\models\varphi;

\displaystyle w\models\varphi_{1}*\varphi_{2}

\displaystyle\text{ iif }(w\models\varphi_{1})*(w\models\varphi_{2})=\mathsf{True};

\displaystyle w\models\lX\varphi

\displaystyle\text{ iif }w[2:]\models\varphi;

\displaystyle w\models\lF\varphi

\displaystyle\text{ iif }\exists i\in\mathbb{N}_{1},\;w[i:]\models\varphi;

\displaystyle w\models\lG\varphi

\displaystyle\text{ iif }\forall i\in\mathbb{N}_{1},\;w[i:]\models\varphi;

\displaystyle w\models\varphi_{1}\lU\varphi_{2}

\displaystyle\text{ iif }\exists i\in\mathbb{N}_{1},\;w[i:]\models\varphi_{2}\text{ and }\forall 1\leq j\leq i-1,\;w[j:]\models\varphi_{1};

\displaystyle w\models\varphi_{1}\lR\varphi_{2}

\displaystyle\text{ iif }w\models\neg(\neg\varphi_{1}\lU\neg\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle w\models\varphi_{1}\lW\varphi_{2}

\displaystyle\text{ iif }w\models(\varphi_{1}\lU\varphi_{2})\vee\lG\varphi_{1};

\displaystyle w\models\varphi_{1}\lM\varphi_{2}

\displaystyle\text{ iif }w\models(\varphi_{1}\lR\varphi_{2})\wedge\lF\varphi_{1}

Given two
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}
, we write
\varphi\implies\varphi^{\prime}
when, for all ultimately periodic words
w
, we have that if
w\models\varphi
, then
v\models\varphi^{\prime}
. We write
\varphi\equiv\varphi^{\prime}
if
\varphi\implies\varphi^{\prime}
and
\varphi^{\prime}\implies\varphi
.

Given any set
S\subseteq(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})^{\omega}
of infinite words, we say that an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula
\varphi
accepts
S
if it accepts all words in
S
, and we say that
\varphi
rejects
S
if it rejects all words in
S
. Furthermore, such an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula
\varphi
distinguishes two sets of infinite words
S\subseteq(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})^{\omega}
and
S^{\prime}\subseteq(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})^{\omega}
if it accepts
S
and rejects
S^{\prime}
, or if it accepts
S^{\prime}
and rejects
S
.

2.2
\mathsf{ATL}
and
\mathsf{CTL}
: syntax and semantics

Let us first introduce the notion of concurrent game structures (CGS), on which
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas are evaluated. We then introduce Kripke structures, a special kind of concurrent game structure, on which
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas are evaluated.

Definition 2
.

A concurrent game structure (CGS for short) is a the tuple
C=\langle Q,I,k,\mathsf{Prop},\pi,d,\delta\rangle
where,

•

Q
is the finite set of states;

•

I\subseteq Q
is the set of initial states;

•

k\in\mathbb{N}
denotes the number of agents, we denote by
\mathsf{Ag}:=[1,\ldots,k]
the set of
k
agents;

•

\pi:Q\mapsto 2^{\mathsf{Prop}}
maps each state
s\in Q
to the set of propositions that hold in
s
;

•

d:Q\times\mathsf{Ag}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}^{+}
maps each state and agent to the number of actions available to that agent at that state;

•

\delta:Q_{\mathsf{Act}}\rightarrow Q
is the function mapping every state and tuple of one action per agent to the next state, where
Q_{\mathsf{Act}}:=\{(q,\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})\mid q\in Q,\;\forall a\in\mathsf{Ag},\;\alpha_{a}\in[1,\ldots,d(q,a)]\}
.

For all states
q\in Q
and coalitions of agents
A\subseteq\mathsf{Ag}
, we let
\mathsf{Act}_{A}(q):=\{\alpha=(\alpha_{a})_{a\in A}\mid\forall a\in\mathsf{Ag},\;\alpha_{a}\in[1,\ldots,d(q,a)]\}
. Then, for all tuple
\alpha=(\alpha_{a})_{a\in A}\in\mathsf{Act}_{A}(q)
of one action per agent in
A
, we let:

\mathsf{Succ}(q,\alpha):=\{q^{\prime}\in Q\mid\exists\alpha^{\prime}=(\alpha^{\prime}_{a})_{a\in\mathsf{Ag}\setminus A}\in\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{Ag}\setminus A}(q),\;\delta(q,(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}))=q^{\prime}\}

Finally, the size
|C|
of the concurrent structure
C
is equal to:
|C|=|Q_{\mathsf{Act}}|+|\mathsf{Prop}|
.

Unless otherwise stated, a concurrent game structure
C
will always refer to the tuple
\langle Q,I,k,\mathsf{Prop},\pi,d,\delta\rangle
.

A Kripke structure is then a concurrent game structure where there is only one agent.

Definition 3
.

A
Kripke structure
is a concurrent game structure
C
where
k=1
and
d
and
\delta
are replaced by subsets of successor states
\emptyset\neq\mathsf{Succ}(q)\subseteq Q
for all states
q\in Q
.

Unless otherwise state, a Krikpe structure
C
will will always refer to the tuple
\langle Q,I,k,\mathsf{Prop},\pi,\mathsf{Succ}\rangle
.

In a concurrent game structure, a strategy for an agent is a function that prescribes to the agent what to do as a function of the history of the game, i.e. of the finite sequence of states seen so far. Furthermore, given a coalition of agents and a tuple of one strategy per agent in the coalition, we define the set of infinite sequences of states that can occur with this tuple of strategies, from any state. This is formally defined below.

Definition 4
.

Consider a concurrent game structure
C
and a agent
a\in[1,\ldots,k]
. A
strategy
for agent
a
is a function
s_{a}:Q^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{1}
such that, for all
\rho=\rho_{0}\dots\rho_{n}\in Q^{+}
, we have
s_{a}(\rho)\leq d(\rho_{n},a)
. We denote by
\mathsf{S}_{a}
the set of strategies available to Agent
a\in[1,\ldots,k]
.

Given any coalition (or subset) of agents
A\subseteq[1,\ldots,k]
, a
strategy profile
for the coalition
A
is a tuple
s=(s_{a})_{a\in A}
of one strategy per agent in
A
. We denote by
\mathsf{S}_{A}
the set of strategy profiles for the coalition
A
. Given any such strategy profile
s
, for all states
q\in Q
, we let
\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q,s)\subseteq Q^{\omega}
denote the set of infinite paths
\rho
that are compatible with the strategy profile
s
from
q
, i.e.:

\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q,s):=\{\rho\in Q^{\omega}\mid\rho[1]=q,\;\forall i\in\mathbb{N}_{1},\rho[i+1]\in\mathsf{Succ}(\rho[i],(s_{a}(\rho[:i]))_{a\in A})\}

In a Kripke structure, we simply consider the set of all infinite paths
\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q)
that can occur from a specific state
q\in Q
, regardless of strategies:

\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q):=\{\rho\in Q^{\omega}\mid\rho[1]=q,\;\forall i\in\mathbb{N}_{1},\rho[i+1]\in\mathsf{Succ}(\rho[i])\}

Syntax.

To define the syntax of
\mathsf{ATL}
and
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas, we introduce two types of formulas: state formulas and path formulas. Intuitively, state formulas express properties of states, where the strategic quantifier occurs, whereas path formulas express temporal properties of paths. For ease of notation, we denote state formulas and path formulas with the Greek letter
\phi
and the Greek letter
\psi
, respectively. Consider some
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
. Then, we denote by
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
the set of
\mathsf{ATL}
-state formulas defined by the grammar:

\displaystyle\phi\Coloneqq p\mid\neg\phi\mid\phi*\phi\mid\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\psi

where
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
,
*\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
,
A\subseteq\mathsf{Ag}:=\{1,\ldots,k\}
is a subset of agents, and
\psi
is an
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
-path formula. Note that, for
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas, we have
k=1
. Hence, there are only two possible subsets:
\emptyset
and
\mathsf{Ag}
itself. Usually, in the
\mathsf{CTL}
syntax,
\langle\langle\emptyset\rangle\rangle\psi
is denoted
\forall\psi
whereas
\langle\langle\mathsf{Ag}\rangle\rangle\psi
is denoted
\exists\psi
.

Next,
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
-path formulas are given by the grammar

\displaystyle\psi\Coloneqq*_{1}\phi\mid\phi*_{2}\phi

where
*_{1}\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\}
and
*_{2}\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}
. We denote by
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}
the set of all
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}
-formulas, with
\mathsf{CTL}
referring to
\mathsf{ATL}^{1}
. The set of sub-formulas
\mathsf{SubF}(\phi)
of a formula
\phi
is then defined inductively as follows:

•

\mathsf{SubF}(p):=\{p\}
for all
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
;

•

\mathsf{SubF}(\neg\phi):=\{\neg\phi\}\cup\mathsf{SubF}(\phi)
;

•

\mathsf{SubF}(\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle(\bullet\phi)):=\{\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle(\bullet\phi)\}\cup\mathsf{SubF}(\phi)
for all
\bullet\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
and
A\subseteq\mathsf{Ag}
;

•

\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2}):=\{\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2}\}\cup\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{1})\cup\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})
for all
\bullet\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
;

•

\mathsf{SubF}(\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle(\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2})):=\{\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle(\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2})\}\cup\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{1})\cup\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})
for all
\bullet\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
and
A\subseteq\mathsf{Ag}
.

The size
|\phi|
of a
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula is defined as its number of sub-formulas:
|\phi|:=|\mathsf{SubF}(\phi)|
. Finally, we also let
\mathsf{NbBin}(\phi)\in\mathbb{N}
denote the number of usage of binary operators in
\phi
, i.e.:

|\phi|_{\mathsf{Bin}}:=|\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi)|

with

\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi):=\{\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2}\in\mathsf{SubF}(\phi)\mid\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathsf{SubF}(\phi),\bullet\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}\cup\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}\}

Semantics.

As mentioned above, we interpret

\mathsf{ATL}^{k}
-formulas over CGS with the set of agents
\mathsf{Ag}:=\{1,\ldots,k\}
using the standard definitions
[
1
]
. Given a state
s
and a state formula
\phi
, we define when
\phi
holds in state
s
, denoted using
s\models\phi
, inductively as follows:

\displaystyle s\models p

\displaystyle\text{ iif }p\in\pi(s),

\displaystyle s\models\neg\phi

\displaystyle\text{ iif }s\not\models\phi,

\displaystyle s\models\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}

\displaystyle\text{ iif }(s\models\phi_{1})*(s\models\phi_{2})=\mathsf{True},

\displaystyle s\models\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\psi

\displaystyle\text{ iif }\exists s\in\mathsf{S}_{A},\;\forall\pi\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q,s),\;\pi\models\psi

where
*\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
. In a Kripke structure, this last line cam be rewritten as follows (where the first line corresponds to
A=\emptyset
and the second to
A=\mathsf{Ag}
):

\displaystyle s\models\exists\psi

\displaystyle\text{ iif }\exists\pi\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q),\;\pi\models\psi

\displaystyle s\models\forall\psi

\displaystyle\text{ iif }\forall\pi\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q),\;\pi\models\psi

Furthermore, given a path
\pi
and a path formula
\psi
, we define when
\psi
holds for the path
\pi
, also denoted using
\pi\models\phi
, inductively as follows:

\displaystyle\pi\models\lX\phi

\displaystyle\text{ iif }\pi[2:]\models\phi;

\displaystyle\pi\models\lF\phi

\displaystyle\text{ iif }\exists i\in\mathbb{N}_{1},\;\pi[i:]\models\phi;

\displaystyle\pi\models\lG\phi

\displaystyle\text{ iif }\forall i\in\mathbb{N}_{1},\;\pi[i:]\models\phi;

\displaystyle\pi\models\phi_{1}\lU\phi_{2}

\displaystyle\text{ iif }\exists i\in\mathbb{N}_{1},\;\pi[i:]\models\phi_{2}\text{ and }\forall 1\leq j\leq i-1,\;\pi[j:]\models\phi_{1};

\displaystyle\pi\models\phi_{1}\lR\phi_{2}

\displaystyle\text{ iif }\pi\models\neg(\neg\phi_{1}\lU\neg\phi_{2})

\displaystyle\pi\models\phi_{1}\lW\phi_{2}

\displaystyle\text{ iif }\pi\models(\phi_{1}\lU\phi_{2})\vee\lG\phi_{1};

\displaystyle\pi\models\phi_{1}\lM\phi_{2}

\displaystyle\text{ iif }\pi\models(\phi_{1}\lR\phi_{2})\wedge\lF\phi_{1}

We now say that an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
holds on a CGS
C
, denoted by
C\models\phi
, if
s\models\phi
for all initial states
s\in I
of
C
.

We use the notations
\implies
and
\equiv
as for
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas.

2.3
Decision problems

We define the
\mathsf{LTL}
,
\mathsf{CTL}
and
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}
learning problems below (for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
), where a model for
\mathsf{LTL}
is an ultimately periodic word, a model for
\mathsf{CTL}
is a Kripke structure and a model for
\mathsf{ATL}^{1}
is a concurrent game structure on the set of agents
\mathsf{Ag}:=\{1,\ldots,k\}
.

Definition 5
.

Let
\mathsf{L}\in\{\mathsf{LTL},\mathsf{CTL},\mathsf{ATL}^{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}\}
and consider some sets of operators
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, we denote by
\mathsf{L}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
the following decision problem:

•

Input:
(\mathsf{Prop},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N},B)
where
\mathsf{Prop}
is a set of propositions,
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N}
are two finite sets of models for
\mathsf{L}
, and
B\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

Output: yes iff there exists an
\mathsf{L}
-formula
\varphi\in\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
such that
|\varphi|\leq B
,
|\varphi|_{\mathsf{Bin}}\leq n
and
\varphi
separates
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{N}
, i.e. such that:

–

for all
X\in\mathcal{P}
, we have
X\models\varphi
;

–

for all
X\in\mathcal{N}
, we have
X\not\models\varphi
.

The size of the input is equal to
|\mathsf{Prop}|+|\mathcal{P}|+|\mathcal{N}|+B
(i.e.
B
is written in unary).

As mentioned in the introduction), since the model checking problems for
\mathsf{LTL}
,
\mathsf{CTL}
and
\mathsf{ATL}
can be decided in polynomial time
[
1
]
, the problems
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}
,
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}
and
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}
are all in
\mathsf{NP}
, using a straightforward guess-and-check subroutine.

Proposition 6
.

For all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, the decision problems
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
,
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
, and

\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},k)
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
are in
\mathsf{NP}
.

3
Learning with non-unary binary operators is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard

In this section, we study the complexity of the learning decision problems in the case where the number of occurrence of binary operators is unbounded. We first show that, in this setting,
\mathsf{LTL}
learning is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard. We then show that
\mathsf{CTL}
learning is at least as hard as
\mathsf{LTL}
learning, which, in turn, implies that
\mathsf{CTL}
learning with unbounded occurrence of binary operators is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard. This holds as well for
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}
learning, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

3.1
\mathsf{LTL}
learning

Let us consider
\mathsf{LTL}
learning. Before we proceed to our contributions, let us discuss the very important related work
[
24
]
.

3.1.1
What is done in
[
24
]

In
[
24
]
, the authors study
\mathsf{LTL}
learning. However, the setting that they consider differs in several ways with the setting that we consider in this paper. We list the main differences below.

•

The letters of the words considered in
[
24
]
are propositions (i.e. elements of
\mathsf{Prop}
) whereas the letters that we consider are subsets of propositions (i.e. elements of
2^{\mathsf{Prop}}
).

•

The words that we consider are infinite whereas the words considered in
[
24
]
are finite.

•

Crucially for complexity questions, the alphabet (as it is referred to in
[
24
]
, i.e. the set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
) that we consider is part of the input, it is not fixed beforehand.

•

The bound
B
that we consider is written in unary instead of binary.

We discuss below the motivations behind our choices, and the implications they have on the complexity of the learning problems.

Letters.

Using subsets of proposition as letters is not unusual, this is done for instance in the seminal book
[
4
]
. Furthermore, in this paper, we focus on comparing
\mathsf{LTL}
,
\mathsf{CTL}
and
\mathsf{ATL}
learning. In that regard, we aim at having settings for all of these logics as close as possible. Hence, we use subsets of propositions as letters, since the states of Kripke structures are usually labeled with subsets of propositions (and the same goes for concurrent game structures). Note that this choice has significant impact on the complexity of the learning problem considered. Indeed, in our setting, letters are subsets of propositions. In combination with having the set of propositions as part of the input — which we discuss below — we show, in particular, that
\mathsf{LTL}
learning with only operator
\lor
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard. On the other hand, in the setting of
[
24
]
, the authors show that with only the operator
\lor
, the
\mathsf{LTL}
learning problem can de decided in polynomial time (Proposition 7).

Word length.

In this paper, we consider infinite words. For representation issues, we focus on ultimately periodic ones. As above, this is more closely related to
\mathsf{CTL}
and
\mathsf{ATL}
semantics. It has little influence on complexity questions, although some differences may arise. For instance, as stated in
[
24
, Proposition 8]
, an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula of the shape
\varphi:=\lX^{k}\varphi^{\prime}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
is always false when evaluated on words of size at most
k-1
, which is irrelevant for us since all words have infinite size.

Alphabet.

Let us now consider this more complicated issue: is it better to have the alphabet (or set of propositions) fixed a priori or to have it part of the input? As is mentioned in
[
24
]
, it is much more usual to have the alphabet fixed a priori, as in the classical examples of automata learning (
[
17
]
). However, we believe that in a learning setting, it makes sense not to know a priori the propositions occurring in the model. That way, the set of propositions could be learned by looking at the models, positive or negative, that we need to separate
1
1

1

Note that in our setting the set of propositions is given as an explicit part of the input. It could alternatively be given implicitly in the input as in the can read in the models.
.

This choice is important in terms of complexity. Indeed, consider the very technical results of
[
24
]
, in Section 7 and especially in Section 8, which deal with
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness results (and hard-to-approximate results) with and without the next operator
\lX
. These results are especially hard to prove because the set of propositions is fixed a priori (and is of size 3). On the other hand, our proofs of
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness for
\mathsf{LTL}
learning are significantly easier than, for instance, the proof of Theorem 9 from
[
24
]
. However, note that the distinction between having the set of propositions part of the input or not becomes less relevant when we restrict ourselves to formulas (for
\mathsf{LTL}
,
\mathsf{CTL}
and
\mathsf{ATL}
learning) with bounded occurrence of binary operators, since in that case our
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness proofs hold even for formula that do not use at all binary operators, and therefor use exactly one proposition.

Bound.

Finally, there is the issue of the representation of the integer
B
that bounds the size of the formulas that we consider. In this paper, we consider the case where it is given in unary. Indeed, although we consider only decision problems where we only answer whether or not there exists a formula, it would also be interesting to explicitly synthesize formulas separating negative and positive instances. If the bound were written in binary, explicitly writing the formula could be exponential in the size of the input. Additionally, having the bound written in unary allows us to have completeness results, and not only hardness ones.

Results from
[
24
]

Let us now briefly discuss the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness results of
[
24
]
. Letting
\mathsf{Op}
denote the operators allowed in
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas, they show that the following
\mathsf{LTL}
learning problems are
\mathsf{NP}
-hard:

•

With alphabet part of the input:

–

When
\mathsf{Op}=\{\lF,\lor\}
(Theorem 2)

–

When
\mathsf{Op}=\{\lF,\wedge\}
(Theorem 8), and the learning problem is hard to approximate;

•

With alphabet not part of the input:

–

When
\{\lX,\wedge,\lor\}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}\subseteq\{\wedge,\lor,\lX,\lF,\lG\}
(Theorem 6, Proposition 11, Proposition 12);

–

When
\{\lF,\wedge\}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}\subseteq\{\wedge,\lor,\lF,\lG,\neg\}
(Theorem 9);

–

When
\{\lG,\lor\}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}\subseteq\{\wedge,\lor,\lF,\lG,\neg\}
(Theorem 10).

Note that, in a previous version of this paper, independently of
[
24
]
, we have shown that the
\mathsf{LTL}
learning problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for
\{\lor\}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}\subseteq\{\wedge,\lor,\Rightarrow,\Leftrightarrow,\lX,\lF,\lG,\lU,\lR,\lW,\lM\}
2
2

2

We present this way our result to mimic
[
24
]
, however in this paper we distinguish between the logical and temporal operators that we consider.
(with the setting used in this paper). The reduction was established from the satisfiability problem
\mathsf{SAT}
. This made the proof quite convoluted as, from a positive instance of the learning problem, we had to be able to extract to a satisfying valuation of the variables. On the other hand, in
[
24
]
, the authors often use the hitting set problem (one of Karp’s 21
\mathsf{NP}
-complete problem) for their reductions. We define it below.

Definition 7

(Hitting set problem)
.

We denote by
\mathsf{Hit}
the following decision problem:

•

Input:
(l,C,k)
where
l\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
,
C=C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}
are non-empty subsets of
[1,\dots,l]
such that
\cup_{1\leq i\leq n}C_{i}=[1,\ldots,l]
and
1\leq k\leq l
.

•

Output: yes iff there is a subset
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
of size at most
k
such that, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we have
H\cap C_{i}\neq\emptyset
.

In the following, unless otherwise stated, when we use
(l,C,k)
as an instance of the hitting set problem,
C
refers to
C=C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}
, for some
n\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
.

Theorem 8

(
[
21
]
)
.

The hitting set problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Observation 9
.

The above theorem holds even if
k
is given in unary. This comes from the fact that
k\leq l
and
\cup_{1\leq i\leq n}C_{i}=[1,\ldots,l]
.

This problem is much better suited for establishing the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of the learning problem decision that we consider. Indeed, it is sufficient to be able to exhibit a set of integers, not a valuation on variables. In particular, it makes it easier to handle various logical operators. In this paper, all the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness proofs that we exhibit in this paper but one are established from the hitting set problem
3
3

3

Except for the proof that
\mathsf{CTL}
and
\mathsf{ATL}
learning with any non-unary binary operator is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, which is proved via a reduction to the
\mathsf{LTL}
case
. Note that some of the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness proofs for the
\mathsf{LTL}
learning case are very close to some proof from
[
24
]
. We discuss it in details below.

3.2
Our results

The goal of this subsection is to show the theorem below:

Theorem 10
.

Consider some
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
, and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
and assume that
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\cup\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\neq\emptyset
. Then, for all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
, the decision problem
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Overall, there are fourteen different binary operators that we will handle, the ten logical operators
\{\lor,\Rightarrow,\Leftarrow,\wedge,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow},\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow},\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor},\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge},\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\}
and the four temporal operators
\{\lU,\lR,\lW,\lM\}
. We give below a bird’s eye view of how the proof of Theorem
10
is structured.

•

We start with the operators
\lor,\Rightarrow,\Leftarrow
, i.e. we assume that
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\cap\{\lor,\Rightarrow,\Leftarrow\}\neq\emptyset
, and we show that for all
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
and
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
, the decision problem
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard. This is stated in Corollary
17
. The reduction for this case is actually a straightforward adaptation of the proof of
[
24
, Theorem 2]
(that additionally makes use of the fact that our letters are subsets of propositions). In fact, the operators
\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow},\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow},\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}
are handled at the same time. The reduction for these operators is obtained from the previous one by reversing the positive and negative sets of words.

•

We then handle the operators
\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor},\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}
(Corollary
23
). The reduction used for the previous item cannot be used as is, because when the operator
\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}
(or the operator
\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}
) is used successively, the formula obtained is semantically equivalent to an alternation of conjunction and disjunction. An illustrating example is given in Example
18
. To circumvent this difficulty, we define a reduction that is both slightly more subtle and an adaptation of the previous one.

•

Before considering the last two logical operators
\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}
, we handle the temporal operators
\lW,\lM
(Corollary
27
). This is actually quite straightforward. Indeed, the two previous reductions only use size-1 infinite words (i.e. a subset of propositions is repeated indefinitely). On such words, the temporal operators
\lW,\lM
actually behave like
\lor,\wedge
respectively. Hence, we can use the reduction of the first item.

•

We then handle the final two logical operators
\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}
(Corollary
36
). These operators behave quite differently from all other operators. In fact, in this case the reduction is not established from the hitting set problem, but from an
\mathsf{NP}
-complete problem dealing with modulo-2 calculus (see Definition
32
), although it still uses only size-1 infinite words. Contrary to the other reductions, here we explicitly assume that the only non-binary operators considered are
\Leftrightarrow
and
\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}
.

•

Finally, we handle the last two operators: the temporal operators
\lU
and
\lR
(Corollary
42
). Contrary to the temporal operators
\lW
and
\lM
, on size-1 words,
\lU
and
\lR
are equivalent to unary binary operators. Hence, the reduction that we consider does not use only size-1 infinite words. It is once again established from the hitting set problem, however the construction is more involved than the reductions of the two first items.

Overall, note that we provide in this subsection a reduction for all possible binary operators to properly justify the statement: “if the occurrence of any binary operator is unbounded, the
\mathsf{LTL}
learning decision problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard”. However, binary operators are not all equivalently relevant, and it may be that some binary operator are not relevant at all when considered alone. This seems to be particularly the case for the operators
\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}
.

3.2.1
Two useful lemmas

Before we handle all possible operators as described above, we first state and prove two lemmas that we will use extensively in this subsection.

First, we state a lemma that establishes a condition on the set of propositions occurring in an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula that distinguishes a pair of infinite words.

Lemma 11
.

Consider a subset of propositions
Y\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}
and two infinite words
w_{1},w_{2}\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})^{\omega}
. Assume that, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
and
x\in\mathsf{Prop}\setminus Y
, we have
x\in w_{1}[i]
if and only if
x\in w_{2}[i]
. Then, if the formula
\varphi
distinguishes the words
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
, the set
Y
occurs in
\varphi
.

Proof.

Let us prove this property
\mathcal{P}(\varphi)
on
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas
\varphi
by induction. Consider such an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula
\varphi
:

•

Assume that
\varphi=x
for some
x\in\mathsf{Prop}
. Then, if
\varphi
distinguishes
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
, it must be that
x\in Y
, by assumption.

•

Assume that
\varphi=\neg\varphi^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}(\varphi^{\prime})
holds. Then, if
\varphi
distinguishes
w_{1}
and
w_{1}
, so does
\varphi^{\prime}
. Hence,
\mathcal{P}(\varphi)
follows.

•

Assume that
\varphi=\varphi_{1}*\varphi_{2}
for some
*\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
and that
\mathcal{P}(\varphi_{1})
and
\mathcal{P}(\varphi_{2})
hold. Then, if neither
\varphi_{1}
nor
\varphi_{2}
distinguish
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
, neither does
\varphi
. Hence, assuming that
\varphi
distinguishes
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
, then
Y
occurs in
\varphi_{1}
or
\varphi_{2}
, and it therefore also occurs in
\varphi
. Hence,
\mathcal{P}(\varphi)
holds.

•

Assume that
\varphi=\bullet\varphi^{\prime}
for some
\bullet\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}\setminus\{\neg\}
and
\mathcal{P}(\varphi^{\prime})
holds. Assume that for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
,
\varphi^{\prime}
does not distinguish
w_{1}[i:]
and
w_{2}[i:]
. Then,
\varphi^{\prime}
does not distinguish
w_{1}[2:]
and
w_{2}[2:]
, and
\lX\varphi^{\prime}
does not distinguish
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
. Furthermore, there is some
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
such that
\varphi^{\prime}
accepts
w_{1}[i:]
iff there is some
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
such that
\varphi^{\prime}
accepts
w_{2}[i:]
. That is,
\lF\varphi^{\prime}
does not distinguish
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
. This is similar for
\lG\varphi^{\prime}
. Hence, if
\varphi
distinguishes
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
,
Y
occurs in
\varphi^{\prime}
, and also in
\varphi
.

•

The case of binary temporal operators is similar.

∎

In addition, we show the straightforward relation between the number of propositions occurring in an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula and the size of that
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula.

Lemma 12
.

For all
Y\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}
, we let
\mathsf{Occ}(Y),\mathsf{NbSubF}(Y)
denote the properties on
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas such that an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula
\varphi
satisfies:

•

\mathsf{Occ}(Y)
if all variables in
Y
occur in
\varphi
, i.e.
Y\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}(\varphi)
;

•

\mathsf{NbSubF}(Y)
if there are at least
2|Y|-1
different sub-formulas of
\varphi
where
Y
occurs.

Then, an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula
\varphi
on
\mathsf{Prop}
that satisfies
\mathsf{Occ}(Y)
also satisfies
\mathsf{NbSubF}(Y)
.

Proof.

Let us show this lemma by induction on
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas
\varphi
:

•

Assume that
\varphi=x
. Then,
\varphi
satisfies
\mathsf{Occ}(Y)
only for
Y=\emptyset
and
Y=\{x\}
, and it also satisfies
\mathsf{NbSubF}(\emptyset)
and
\mathsf{NbSubF}(\{x\})
;

•

for all
\bullet\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
, assume that
\varphi=\bullet\varphi^{\prime}
. Consider any
Y\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}
. If
\varphi
satisfies
\mathsf{Occ}(Y)
, then so does
\varphi^{\prime}
. Hence,
\varphi^{\prime}
satisfies
\mathsf{NbSubF}(Y)
, and therefore so does
\varphi
;

•

for all
\bullet\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}\cup\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
, assume that
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\bullet\varphi_{2}
. Consider any
Y\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}
and assume that
\varphi
satisfies
\mathsf{Occ}(Y)
. Let
Y_{1}:=\mathsf{Prop}(\varphi_{1})\cap Y\subseteq Y
denote the set of different propositions in
Y
occurring in
\varphi_{1}
(they may also occur in
\varphi_{2}
). Let also
Y_{2}^{\prime}:=(\mathsf{Prop}(\varphi_{2})\setminus\mathsf{Prop}(\varphi_{1}))\cap Y\subseteq Y
denote the set of different propositions in
Y
occurring in
\varphi_{2}
and not in
\varphi_{1}
. We have
Y=Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}^{\prime}
. Furthermore:

–

by our induction hypothesis on
\varphi_{1}
, there are at least
k_{1}:=2|Y_{1}|-1
sub-formulas in
\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi_{1})\subseteq\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi)
where
Y_{1}
occurs;

–

by our induction hypothesis on
\varphi_{2}
, there are also at least
k_{2}^{\prime}:=2|Y_{2}^{\prime}|-1
sub-formulas in
\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi_{2})\subseteq\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi)
where
Y^{\prime}_{2}
occurs. By definition of
Y^{\prime}_{2}
, it follows that all these sub-formulas are not sub-formulas of
\varphi_{1}
;

–

Finally, the sub-formula
\varphi
itself is a sub-formula of
\varphi
that is neither a sub-formula of
\varphi_{1}
nor of
\varphi_{2}
and where
Y
occurs.

Therefore, there are at least
k_{1}+k_{2}^{\prime}+1=2|Y_{1}|-1+2|Y^{\prime}_{2}|-1+1\geq 2|Y|-1
different sub-formulas of
\varphi
where
Y
occurs. That is,
\varphi
satisfies
\mathsf{NbSubF}(Y)
.

∎

3.2.2
Proof of Theorem
10
: when
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\cap\{\lor,\Rightarrow,\Leftarrow,\wedge,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow},\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow}\}\neq\emptyset

We present a first reduction that we will consider for the operators
\lor,\Rightarrow,\Leftarrow
, along with the dual reduction for the operators
\wedge,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow},\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow}
. This is obtained via a slight modification of the reduction presented in
[
24
]
to establish Theorem 2. We make use of the fact that the letters we consider are subsets of propositions instead of being a single proposition.

Definition 13
.

Consider an instance
(l,C,k)
of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
. We define:

•

\mathsf{Prop}:=\{a_{j},b_{j}\mid 1\leq j\leq l\}
to be the set of propositions;

•

\mathsf{Set}:=\{\alpha_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
where for all
1\leq i\leq n
: we let
\alpha_{i}:=\{c_{i}^{1},\ldots,c_{i}^{l}\}^{\omega}\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})
with, for all
1\leq j\leq l
:

\displaystyle c_{i}^{j}:=\begin{cases}a_{j}&\text{ if }j\in C_{i}\\
b_{j}&\text{ if }j\notin C_{i}\\
\end{cases}

•

\mathsf{EmptySet}:=\{\beta\}
with
\beta:=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{l}\}^{\omega}\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})
;

•

B=2k-1
.

Then, we define the inputs
\mathsf{In}^{\lor}_{(l,C,k)}:=(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{Set},\mathsf{EmptySet},B)
and
\mathsf{In}^{\wedge}_{(l,C,k)}:=(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{EmptySet},\mathsf{Set},B)
.

The positive and negative words that we have defined above satisfy the observation below.

Observation 14
.

For all
w\in\mathsf{EmptySet}\cup\mathsf{Set}
, we have:

\forall 1\leq j\leq l,\;a_{j}\models w\Leftrightarrow\neg b_{j}\models w

Let us describe on an example below what this reduction amounts to on a specific instance of the hitting set problem.

Example 15
.

Assume that
l=4
,
C=C_{1},C_{2}
with
C_{1}:=\{1,3\}
and
C_{2}:=\{1,2,4\}
and
k=1
. Then, we have:
\mathsf{Prop}=\{a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2},a_{3},b_{3},a_{4},b_{4}\}
,
\alpha_{1}=(\{a_{1},b_{2},a_{3},b_{4}\})^{\omega}
,
\alpha_{2}=(\{a_{1},a_{2},b_{3},a_{4}\})^{\omega}
and
\beta=(\{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4}\})^{\omega}
. Finally,
B=1
.

The above definition satisfies the lemma below.

Lemma 16
.

Consider an instance
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
and sets of operators
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
, and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{}}
. If
\mathsf{In}^{\lor}_{(l,C,k)}
or
\mathsf{In}^{\wedge}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of the decision problem
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
, then
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
.

On the other hand, if
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
and
\{\lor,\Rightarrow,\Leftarrow\}\cap\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\neq\emptyset
(resp.
\{\wedge,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow},\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow}\}\cap\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\neq\emptyset
), then
\mathsf{In}^{\lor}_{(l,C,k)}
(resp.
\mathsf{In}^{\wedge}_{(l,C,k)}
) is a positive instance of the decision problem
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathsf{In}^{\lor}_{(l,C,k)}
or
\mathsf{In}^{\wedge}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
. Consider an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula
\varphi
of size at most
B=2k-1
that distinguishes the sets of infinite words
\mathsf{Set}
and
\mathsf{EmptySet}
. Let
H=\{i\in[1,\ldots,l]\mid\{a_{i},b_{i}\}\cap\mathsf{Prop}(\varphi)\neq\emptyset\}
denote the set of integers
i
for which at least one of the corresponding variables
a_{i}
or
b_{i}
occurs in
\varphi
. By Lemma
12
, it must be that
|H|\leq k
since
|\varphi|\leq 2k-1
. Let us show that
H
is a hitting set. Let
1\leq i\leq n
. The formula
\varphi
distinguishes the infinite words
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta
.
Furthermore, for all
j\in[1,\ldots,l]\setminus C_{i}
, we have
b_{j}\in\alpha_{i}[1]
and
b_{j}\in\beta[1]
(also, recall Observation
14
). Hence, by Lemma
11
, it must be that
C_{i}\cap H\neq\emptyset
. Since this holds for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we obtain that
H
is indeed a hitting set. Note that the arguments that we have given here hold regardless of the operators used in the formula
\varphi
. Hence,
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
.

Assume now that
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
. Consider a hitting set
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
of size at most
k
. We denote
H:=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{r}\}
with
|H|=r\leq k
. We define
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas indexed by the operator that we consider.

•

\varphi_{\lor}:=a_{j_{1}}\lor a_{j_{2}}\lor\ldots\lor a_{j_{r}}

•

\varphi_{\Rightarrow}:=b_{j_{1}}\Rightarrow(b_{j_{2}}\Rightarrow(\ldots\Rightarrow\;a_{j_{r}}))

•

\varphi_{\Leftarrow}:=((a_{j_{1}}\Leftarrow b_{j_{2}})\Leftarrow\ldots)\Leftarrow b_{j_{r}}

Recall that for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{B}
, we have
x_{1}\Rightarrow x_{2}=\neg x_{1}\lor x_{2}
and
x_{1}\Leftarrow x_{2}=x_{1}\lor\neg x_{2}
, hence, by Observation
14
, for all
w\in\mathsf{EmptySet}\cup\mathsf{Set}
, we have
w\models\varphi_{\lor}
iff
w\models\varphi_{\Rightarrow}
iff
w\models\varphi_{\Leftarrow}
.

We also define the
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas below.

•

\varphi_{\wedge}:=b_{j_{1}}\wedge b_{j_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge b_{j_{r}}

•

\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow}}:=a_{j_{1}}\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow}\;(a_{j_{2}}\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow}\;(\ldots\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow}\;b_{j_{r}}))

•

\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow}}:=((b_{j_{1}}\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow}\;a_{j_{2}})\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow}\;\ldots)\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow}\;a_{j_{r}}

Recall that for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{B}
, we have
x_{1}\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow}\;x_{2}=x_{1}\wedge\neg x_{2}
and
x_{1}\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow}\;x_{2}=\neg x_{1}\wedge x_{2}
. Hence, by Observation
14
, for all
w\in\mathsf{EmptySet}\cup\mathsf{Set}
, we have
w\models\varphi_{\wedge}
iff
w\models\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow}}
iff
w\models\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow}}
and
w\models\varphi_{\wedge}
iff
w\not\models\varphi_{\lor}
.

Clearly, we have
|\varphi_{\lor}|=|\varphi_{\Rightarrow}|=|\varphi_{\Leftarrow}|=|\varphi_{\wedge}|=|\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow}}|=|\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow}}|=2r-1\leq B
.

Furthermore, consider any
1\leq i\leq n
. Let
j\in H\cap C_{i}\neq\emptyset
. We have
\alpha_{i}\models a_{j}
, hence
\alpha_{i}\models\varphi_{\lor}
. This holds for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Furthermore, we also have
\beta\not\models\varphi_{\lor}
. Therefore,
\varphi_{\lor}
accepts
\mathsf{Set}
and rejects
\mathsf{EmptySet}
. It is also the case for
\varphi_{\Rightarrow}
and
\varphi_{\Leftarrow}
. It is the opposite for the formulas
\varphi_{\wedge},\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow}},\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow}}
(i.e. they accept
\mathsf{EmptySet}
and reject
\mathsf{Set}
). Hence, if
\{\lor,\Rightarrow,\Leftarrow\}\cap\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\neq\emptyset
then
\mathsf{In}^{\lor}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
and if
\{\wedge,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow},\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow}\}\cap\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\neq\emptyset
, then
\mathsf{In}^{\wedge}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
.
∎

We obtain the corollary below.

Corollary 17
.

Consider a set
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
of binary logical operators and assume that
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\cap\{\lor,\Rightarrow,\Leftarrow,\wedge,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow},\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow}\}\neq\emptyset
. For all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
, the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Lemmas
16
and the fact that the instances
\mathsf{In}^{\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}}_{(l,C,k)}
and
\mathsf{In}^{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}_{(l,C,k)}
can be computed in logarithmic space from
(l,C,k)
.
∎

3.2.3
Proof of Theorem
10
: when
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\cap\{\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor},\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\}\neq\emptyset
.

The case of the operators
\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}
and
\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}
is slightly different. Indeed, contrary to the above operators, when successively using one of these operators, we obtain (semantically) an alternation of conjunctions and disjunctions. We describe it on an example below.

Example 18
.

Consider six variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5},x_{6}
. Assume that we want to use them in a single
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula. If we can use the
\lor
operator, we can consider the formula
x_{1}\lor x_{2}\lor x_{3}\lor x_{4}\lor x_{5}\lor x_{6}
. If we can use the
\Rightarrow
operator, we can consider the formula
x_{1}\Rightarrow(x_{2}\Rightarrow(x_{3}\Rightarrow(x_{4}\Rightarrow(x_{5}\Rightarrow x_{6}))))
. Note that, up to some negation on the variables, this amounts semantically to only using the
\lor
operator. However, assume now that we can only use the
\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}
operator. For instance, consider:

\varphi:=x_{1}\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;(x_{2}\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;(x_{3}\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;(x_{4}\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;(x_{5}\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;x_{6}))))

It is semantically equivalent to:

\varphi\equiv\neg x_{1}\lor(x_{2}\wedge(\neg x_{3}\lor(x_{4}\wedge(\neg x_{5}\lor\neg x_{6})))

Here, we have both operators
\lor
and
\wedge
.

To circumvent this difficulty, we are going to change the reduction by adding propositions that will always hold on the words of interest. We can then place these propositions where
x_{2}
and
x_{4}
were in the above formula. That way, on the infinite words where these propositions hold, we obtain a disjunction, as in the formula above.

We start with the reduction for the
\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}
operator.

Definition 19
.

Consider an instance
(l,C,k)
of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
. If
k\geq l
,
(l,C,k)
is obviously a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
, and we define
\mathsf{In}^{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}_{(l,C,k)}
to be an arbitrary positive instance of the
\mathsf{LTL}
learning decision problem. Otherwise, we define:

•

\mathsf{Prop}:=\{a_{i},b_{i}\mid 1\leq j\leq l\}\cup\{x_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
to be the set of propositions;

•

\mathcal{P}:=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n},\beta\}
where for all
1\leq i\leq n
: we let
\alpha_{i}:=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},c_{i}^{1},\ldots,c_{i}^{l}\}^{\omega}\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})
with, for all
1\leq j\leq l
:

\displaystyle c_{i}^{j}:=\begin{cases}a_{j}&\text{ if }j\in C_{i}\\
b_{j}&\text{ if }j\notin C_{i}\\
\end{cases}

and
\beta:=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k-1},b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}\}^{\omega}
;

•

\mathcal{N}:=\{\alpha,\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\}
with
\alpha:=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},b_{1},\dots,b_{l}\}^{\omega}\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})
and, for all
1\leq i\leq k-1
, we have
\beta_{i}:=(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k-1},b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}\}\setminus\{x_{i}\})^{\omega}
and
\beta_{k}:=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}\}^{\omega}
;

•

B=4k-1
.

Then, we define the input
\mathsf{In}^{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}_{(l,C,k)}:=(\mathsf{Prop},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N},B)
of the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}
decision problem.

Similarly to the previous reduction, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 20
.

Consider a set
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
of binary logical operators and assume that
\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
. Then, for all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
,
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
if and only if
\mathsf{In}^{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of the the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem.

This proof of this lemma is quite close to the proof of Lemma
16
.

Proof.

If
k\geq l
, the equivalence is straightforward. We assume in the following that
k\leq l
.

Assume that
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
. Consider a hitting set
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
of size at most
k
. Consider any set
H^{\prime}\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
of size exactly
k
such that
H\subseteq H^{\prime}
. We let
H^{\prime}:=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\}
. We let:

•

\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}:=b_{j_{1}}\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;(x_{1}\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;(b_{j_{2}}\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;(\ldots x_{k-1}\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;(b_{j_{k}}\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;x_{k}))))

•

\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}^{\mathsf{expl}}:=\neg b_{j_{1}}\lor(x_{1}\wedge(\neg b_{j_{2}}\lor(\ldots x_{k-1}\wedge(\neg b_{j_{k}}\vee\neg x_{k}))))

The formula
\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}^{\mathsf{expl}}
is written to make more explicit what
\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}
is equal to. Indeed, recall that for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{B}
, we have
x_{1}\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;x_{2}=\neg x_{1}\lor\neg x_{2}
, hence, for all
w\in\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{N}
, we have
w\models\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}
iff
w\models\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}^{\mathsf{expl}}
. Furthermore, we have
|\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}|=4k-3=B
.

Let
\gamma\in\{\alpha,\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
. We have
\gamma\models x_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge x_{k}
. Hence,
\gamma\models\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}
if and only if
\gamma\models\neg b_{j_{1}}\lor\ldots\lor\neg b_{j_{k}}
. Therefore,
\alpha\not\models\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}
. However, for any
1\leq i\leq n
, there is
j\in H\cap C_{i}\neq\emptyset
such that
\alpha_{i}\models\neg b_{j}
, and thus
\alpha_{i}\models\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}
. In addition, consider any
\delta\in\{\beta,\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\}
. For all
j\in[1,\ldots,l]
, we have
\delta\models b_{j}
. Hence,
\delta\models\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}
if and only if
\delta\models x_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge x_{k-1}\wedge\neg x_{k}
. Hence,
\beta\models\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}
whereas, for all
1\leq i\leq k
, we have
\beta_{i}\not\models\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}
. Overall, the formula
\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}
accepts
\mathcal{P}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}
. Hence, the decision problem
\mathsf{In}^{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
.

Assume now the decision problem
\mathsf{In}^{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
. Consider an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula
\varphi
of size at most
B=4k-1
that distinguishes the sets of infinite words
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{N}
. Let
H=\{i\in[1,\ldots,l]\mid\{a_{i},b_{i}\}\cap\mathsf{Prop}(\varphi)\neq\emptyset\}
denote the set of integers
i
for which at least one of the corresponding variables
a_{i}
or
b_{i}
occurs in
\varphi
. Let us show that this set is of size at most
k
and intersects all sets
C_{i}
.

By Lemma
12
, we have
\mathsf{Prop}(\varphi)\leq 2k
. Furthermore, by Lemma
11
, since for all
1\leq i\leq k
, the formula
\varphi
distinguishes the words
\beta
and
\beta_{i}
, it follows that
x_{i}\in\mathsf{Prop}(\varphi)
. Hence,
|H|\leq 2k-k=k
.

Furthermore, let
1\leq i\leq n
. The formula
\varphi
distinguishes the infinite words
\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha
. In addition, for all
j\in[1,\ldots,l]\setminus C_{i}
, we have
b_{j}\in\alpha_{i}[1]\cap\alpha[1]
,
a_{j}\notin\alpha_{i}[1]\cup\alpha[1]
and
\alpha_{i}[1]\cap\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}=\alpha[1]\cap\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}
. Hence, by Lemma
11
and by Definition of
\alpha_{i}
, it must be that
C_{i}\cap H\neq\emptyset
. Since this holds for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we obtain that
H
is indeed a hitting set. Hence,
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
.
∎

Contrary to the reductions we defined in Definition
13
, the reduction for the operator
\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}
is not obtained from the reduction for
\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}
by reversing the positive and negative sets of words, though it is quite similar. We give it below.

Definition 21
.

Consider an instance
(l,C,k)
of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
. If
k\geq l
,
(l,C,k)
is obviously a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
, and we define
\mathsf{In}^{\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}}_{(l,C,k)}
to be an arbitrary positive instance of the
\mathsf{LTL}
learning decision problem. Otherwise, we define:

•

\mathsf{Prop}:=\{a_{i},b_{i}\mid 1\leq j\leq l\}\cup\{x_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
to be the set of propositions;

•

\mathcal{P}:=\{\alpha,\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\}
with
\alpha:=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{l}\}^{\omega}\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})
and, for all
1\leq i\leq k-1
, we have
\beta_{i}:=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{l},x_{i},x_{k}\}^{\omega}
and
\beta_{k}=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{l}\}^{\omega}
;

•

\mathcal{N}:=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n},\beta\}
where for all
1\leq i\leq n
: we let
\alpha_{i}:=\{c_{i}^{1},\ldots,c_{i}^{l}\}^{\omega}\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})
with, for all
1\leq j\leq l
:

\displaystyle c_{i}^{j}:=\begin{cases}a_{j}&\text{ if }j\in C_{i}\\
b_{j}&\text{ if }j\notin C_{i}\\
\end{cases}

and
\beta:=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{l},x_{k}\}^{\omega}
;

•

B=4k-1
.

Then, we define the inputs
\mathsf{In}^{\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}}_{(l,C,k)}:=(\mathsf{Prop},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N},B)
of the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}
decision problem.

Similarly to the previous reduction, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 22
.

Consider a set
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
of binary logical operators and assume that
\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
. Then, for all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
,
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
if and only if
\mathsf{In}^{\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of the the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem.

The proof of this lemma is very close to the proof of Lemma
20
. Hence, we only give the formula using only the
\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}
operator built from a hitting set that we consider.

Proof sketch.

If
k\geq l
, the equivalence is straightforward. Let us now assume that
k\leq l
.

Assume that
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
. Consider a hitting set
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
of size at most
k
. Consider any set
H^{\prime}\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
of size exactly
k
such that
H\subseteq H^{\prime}
. We let
H^{\prime}:=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\}
and:

•

\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}}:=a_{j_{1}}\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}\;(x_{1}\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}\;(a_{j_{2}}\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}\;(\ldots x_{k-1}\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}\;(a_{j_{k}}\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}\;x_{k}))))

•

\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}}^{\mathsf{expl}}:=\neg a_{j_{1}}\wedge(x_{1}\lor(\neg a_{j_{2}}\wedge(\ldots x_{k-1}\lor(\neg a_{j_{k}}\wedge\neg x_{k}))))

The formula
\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}}^{\mathsf{expl}}
is written to make more explicit what
\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}}
is equal to. Indeed, recall that for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{B}
, we have
x_{1}\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}\;x_{2}=\neg x_{1}\wedge\neg x_{2}
, hence, for all
w\in\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{N}
, we have
w\models\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}}
iff
w\models\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}}^{\mathsf{expl}}
. One can then check that the formula
\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}}
accepts
\mathcal{P}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}
.
∎

We deduce the corollary below.

Corollary 23
.

Consider a set
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
of binary logical operators and assume that
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\cap\{\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge},\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}\}\neq\emptyset
. For all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
, the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Lemmas
20
and
22
and the fact that the instances
\mathsf{In}^{\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}}_{(l,C,k)}
and
\mathsf{In}^{\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}}_{(l,C,k)}
can be computed in logarithmic space from
(l,C,k)
.
∎

3.2.4
Proof of Theorem
10
: with the temporal operators
\lW
and
\lM

The last two logical binary operators
\Leftrightarrow
and
\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}
will be handled by considering a reduction that is completely different from what we have presented so far. However, to prove the correctness of that reduction we first show a way to handle (i.e. to remove them from the formulas that we consider) the temporal operators. This can be done because, in all the reductions that we have presented so far, and with the reduction for the operators
\Leftrightarrow
and
\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}
that will follow, all the infinite words that we consider are size-1 infinite words. We define a notion of equivalence on
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas over these infinite words.

Definition 24
.

Consider a set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
and two
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
. We write
\varphi_{1}\equiv_{1}\varphi_{2}
when, for all size-1 infinite words
w\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})^{\omega}
, we have
w\models\varphi_{1}
if and only if
w\models\varphi_{2}
.

Then, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 25
.

Consider three
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas
\varphi,\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in\mathsf{LTL}
. We have:

1.

for all
\bullet\in\{\lX,\lF,\lG\}
, we have
\varphi\equiv_{1}\bullet\varphi
;

2.

for all
\bullet\in\{\lU,\lR\}
, we have
\varphi_{2}\equiv_{1}\varphi_{1}\bullet\varphi_{2}
;

3.

\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2}\equiv_{1}\varphi_{1}\lW\varphi_{2}
;

4.

\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}\equiv_{1}\varphi_{1}\lM\varphi_{2}
.

Proof.

Consider any
\alpha\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}
. Note that for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, we have
\alpha^{\omega}[i:]=\alpha^{\omega}
. Therefore:

1.

•

\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}[2:]\models\varphi\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}\models\lX\varphi
;

•

\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi\text{ iff }\exists i\in\mathbb{N}_{1},\;\alpha^{\omega}[i:]\models\varphi\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}\models\lF\varphi
;

•

\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi\text{ iff }\forall i\in\mathbb{N}_{1},\;\alpha^{\omega}[i:]\models\varphi\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}\models\lG\varphi;

2.

If
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{2}
, then
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{1}\lU\varphi_{2}
. Furthermore, if
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{1}\lU\varphi_{2}
, then there is some
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
such that
\alpha^{\omega}[i:]\models\varphi_{2}
. Hence,
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{2}
. In fact,
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{1}\lU\varphi_{2}\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{2}
.

In addition, we have:

\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{1}\lR\varphi_{2}\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}\not\models\neg\varphi_{1}\lU\neg\varphi_{2}\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}\not\models\neg\varphi_{2}\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{2}

3.

\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{1}\lW\varphi_{2}\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}\models(\varphi_{1}\lU\varphi_{2})\lor\lG\varphi_{1}\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{2}\lor\varphi_{1}
;

4.

\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{1}\lM\varphi_{2}\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}\models(\varphi_{1}\lR\varphi_{2})\wedge\lF\varphi_{1}\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{2}\wedge\varphi_{2}
.

∎

In particular, this lemma tells us that, on size-1 infinite words, the
\lW
temporal operator behaves like the
\lor
logical operator and the
\lM
temporal operator behaves like the
\wedge
logical operator. Hence, we can reuse the reduction from Definition
13
to establish that the
\mathsf{LTL}
learning problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard with at least one of the two temporal operators
\lW
or
\lM
, as stated in the lemma below.

Lemma 26
.

Consider a set
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}
of binary temporal operators and assume that
\lW\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}
(resp.
\lM\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}
). Then, for all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
,
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
if and only if
\mathsf{In}^{\lor}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of the the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem (resp.
\mathsf{In}^{\wedge}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of the the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
).

Proof.

The bottom to top implication is already given by Lemma
16
. Furthermore, let us assume that
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
. Then, from a hitting set
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
of size at most
k
with
H:=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{r}\}
, we consider the
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas:
\varphi_{\lW}:=a_{j_{1}}\lW(a_{j_{2}}\lW(\ldots\lW a_{j_{r}}))
and
\varphi_{\lM}:=b_{j_{1}}\lM(b_{j_{2}}\lM(\ldots\lM b_{j_{r}}))
. We have
|\varphi_{\lW}|=|\varphi_{\lM}|=2r-1\leq 2k-1
.
In addition, by Lemma
25
,
\varphi_{\lW}\equiv_{1}a_{j_{1}}\lor\ldots\lor a_{j_{r}}=\varphi_{\lor}
and
\varphi_{\lM}:=b_{j_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge b_{j_{r}}=\varphi_{\wedge}
, with
\varphi_{\lor}
and
\varphi_{\wedge}
from the proof of Lemma
16
, where we have shown that
\varphi_{\lor}
accepts
\mathsf{Set}
and rejects
\mathsf{EmptySet}
and
\varphi_{\wedge}
accepts
\mathsf{EmptySet}
and rejects
\mathsf{Set}
. The lemma follows.
∎

We deduce the corollary below.

Corollary 27
.

Consider a set
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
of binary temporal operators with
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\cap\{\lW,\lM\}\neq\emptyset
. For all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
, the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Lemma
26
and the fact that the instances
\mathsf{In}^{\lor}_{(l,C,k)}
and
\mathsf{In}^{\wedge}_{(l,C,k)}
can be computed in logarithmic space from
(l,C,k)
.
∎

3.2.5
Proof of Theorem
10
: when
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\cap\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\}\neq\emptyset
.

We now consider the case of the operators
\Leftrightarrow
and
\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}
. To handle this case, we are going to restrict ourselves to
\Leftrightarrow
-formula, defined below, i.e.
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas that only use operators
\{\neg,\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\}
.

Definition 28
.

We say that an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula is a
\Leftrightarrow
-formula if it belongs to the fragment
\mathsf{LTL}(\{\neg\},\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\},\emptyset)
.

We exhibit a function that removes all the operators that we do not want to consider here.

Lemma 29
.

There exists a function
\mathsf{tr}:\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}},\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\},\{\lU,\lR\})\rightarrow\mathsf{LTL}(\{\neg\},\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\},\emptyset)
such that, for all
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas
\varphi\in\mathsf{LTL}
:

1.

\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{tr}(\varphi))\subseteq\mathsf{tr}[\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi)]:=\{\mathsf{tr}(\varphi^{\prime})\mid\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi)\}
, hence
|\mathsf{tr}(\varphi)|\leq|\varphi|
;

2.

\varphi\equiv_{1}\mathsf{tr}(\varphi)
.

Proof.

We define
\mathsf{tr}:\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}},\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\},\{\lU,\lR\})\rightarrow\mathsf{LTL}(\{\neg\},\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\},\emptyset)
by induction on
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas. Consider a formula
\varphi\in\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}},\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\},\{\lU,\lR\})
:

•

Assume that
\varphi=x
for any
x\in\mathsf{Prop}
. We set
\mathsf{tr}(\varphi):=\varphi
which satisfies conditions 1.-2.;

•

Assume that
\varphi=\neg\varphi^{\prime}
and that
\mathsf{tr}
is defined on all sub-formulas of
\varphi^{\prime}
and satisfies conditions 1.-2. on
\varphi^{\prime}
. We set
\mathsf{tr}(\varphi):=\lnot\mathsf{tr}(\varphi^{\prime})
, which ensures that
\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{tr}(\varphi))=\{\lnot\mathsf{tr}(\varphi^{\prime})\}\cup\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{tr}(\varphi^{\prime}))\subseteq\mathsf{tr}[\{\lnot\varphi^{\prime}\}]\cup\mathsf{tr}[\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi^{\prime})]=\mathsf{tr}[\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi)]
. Furthermore, for all
\alpha\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}
, we have
\alpha^{\omega}\models\mathsf{tr}(\varphi)\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}\not\models\mathsf{tr}(\varphi^{\prime})\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}\not\models\varphi^{\prime}\text{ iff }\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi
. Thus,
\varphi\equiv_{1}\mathsf{tr}(\varphi)
;

•

For all
\bullet\in\{\lX,\lF,\lG\}
, assume that
\varphi=\bullet\varphi^{\prime}
, that
\mathsf{tr}
is defined on all sub-formulas of
\varphi^{\prime}
and satisfies conditions 1.-2. on
\varphi^{\prime}
. Then, we set
\mathsf{tr}(\varphi):=\mathsf{tr}(\varphi^{\prime})
, which ensures that
\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{tr}(\varphi))=\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{tr}(\varphi^{\prime}))\subseteq\mathsf{tr}[\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi^{\prime})]\subseteq\mathsf{tr}[\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi)]
. Furthermore,
\varphi\equiv_{1}\mathsf{tr}(\varphi)
by Lemma
25
and since
\varphi^{\prime}\equiv_{1}\mathsf{tr}(\varphi^{\prime})
.

•

For any
\bullet\in\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow},\lU,\lR\}
, assume that
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\bullet\varphi_{2}
and that
\mathsf{tr}
is defined on all sub-formulas of
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
and satisfies conditions 1.-2. on
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
.

–

If
\bullet\in\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\}
, we set
\mathsf{tr}(\varphi):=\mathsf{tr}(\varphi_{1})\bullet\mathsf{tr}(\varphi_{2})
, which ensures that
\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{tr}(\varphi))=\{\mathsf{tr}(\varphi)\}\cup\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{tr}(\varphi_{1}))\cup\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{tr}(\varphi_{2}))\subseteq\mathsf{tr}[\{\varphi\}]\cup\mathsf{tr}[\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi_{1})]\cup\mathsf{tr}[\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi_{2})]\subseteq\mathsf{tr}[\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi)]
. Furthermore, since
\varphi_{1}\equiv_{1}\mathsf{tr}(\varphi_{1})
and
\varphi_{2}\equiv_{1}\mathsf{tr}(\varphi_{2})
, we have
\varphi\equiv_{1}\mathsf{tr}(\varphi)
;

–

If
\bullet\in\{\lU,\lR\}
, we set
\mathsf{tr}(\varphi):=\mathsf{tr}(\varphi_{2})
, which ensures that
\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{tr}(\varphi))=\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{tr}(\varphi_{2}))\subseteq\mathsf{tr}[\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi_{2})]\subseteq\mathsf{tr}[\mathsf{SubF}(\varphi)]
. Furthermore,
\varphi\equiv_{1}\mathsf{tr}(\varphi)
by Lemma
25
and since
\varphi_{1}\equiv_{1}\mathsf{tr}(\varphi_{1})
and
\varphi_{2}\equiv_{1}\mathsf{tr}(\varphi_{2})

∎

Now, in order to gain an intuition on the reduction that we will consider (and especially the problem from which we make that reduction), let us give the central property satisfied by
\Leftrightarrow
-formulas.

Lemma 30
.

Consider a set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
. Given an
\Leftrightarrow
-formula
\varphi
, we let
\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi)\in\mathbb{N}
denote the number of occurrences of the
\neg
and
\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}
operators in
\varphi
. Furthermore, given any subset
\alpha\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}
, we also denote by
\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi)\in\mathbb{N}
the number of occurrences of propositions in
\mathsf{Prop}\setminus\alpha
occurring in
\mathsf{Prop}
(hence, some propositions may be counted several times if they appear more than once in
\varphi
). Both of these numbers are defined inductively on the tree structure of the
\Leftrightarrow
-formula
\varphi
, without the notion of sub-formulas. Then, for all
\alpha\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}
, we have:

\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi\text{ if and only if }\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi)\text{ and }\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi)\text{ have the same parity}

Stated algebraically, we have
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi\text{ if and only if }\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi)+\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi)\mod 2=0
.

Proof.
