We show by induction on
\varphi
the property
\mathcal{P}(\varphi)
:
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi
iff

\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi)+\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi)\text{ is even}
.

•

If
\varphi=x
for some
x\in\mathsf{Prop}
, then
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi
if and only if
x\in\alpha
, i.e.
\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi)=\emptyset
. Since
\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi)=0
in any case,
\mathcal{P}(\varphi)
follows.

•

Assume that
\varphi=\neg\varphi^{\prime}
and that
\mathcal{P}(\varphi^{\prime})
holds. Then,
\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi)=\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi^{\prime})+1
and
\mathsf{Prop}(\varphi)=\mathsf{Prop}(\varphi^{\prime})
, thus
\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi^{\prime})=\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi)
. Hence,
\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi^{\prime})
have the same parity if and only if
\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi)
and
\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi)
do not, and
\mathcal{P}(\varphi)
follows.

•

Assume that
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\bullet\varphi_{2}
for some
\bullet\in\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\}
and assume that both
\mathcal{P}(\varphi_{1})
and
\mathcal{P}(\varphi_{2})
hold. We have
\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi)=\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi_{1})+\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi_{2})
. Then:

–

If
\bullet=\Leftrightarrow
, we have
\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi)=\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi_{1})+\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi_{2})
. Furthermore,
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi
iff we have
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{1}
and
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{2}
or
\alpha^{\omega}\not\models\varphi_{2}
and
\alpha^{\omega}\not\models\varphi_{2}
. That is, by
\mathcal{P}(\varphi_{1})
and
\mathcal{P}(\varphi_{2})
, we have
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi
iff

\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi_{1})+\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi_{1})\mod 2=\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi_{2})+\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi_{2})\mod 2

which is equivalent to

\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi_{1})+\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi_{2})+\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi_{1})+\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi_{2})=0\mod 2

That is:

\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi)+\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi)=0\mod 2

–

If
\bullet=\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}
, we have
\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi)=\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi_{1})+\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi_{2})+1
. Furthermore,
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi
iff we have
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{1}
and
\alpha^{\omega}\not\models\varphi_{2}
or
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi_{2}
and
\alpha^{\omega}\not\models\varphi_{2}
. That is, by
\mathcal{P}(\varphi_{1})
and
\mathcal{P}(\varphi_{2})
, we have
\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi
iff

\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi_{1})+\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi_{1})\mod 2=\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi_{2})+\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi_{2})+1\mod 2

which is equivalent to

\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi_{1})+\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi_{2})+1+\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi_{1})+\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi_{2})=0\mod 2

That is:

\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi)+\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi)=0\mod 2

The property
\mathcal{P}(\varphi)
follows.

∎

We state below, as a corollary, the lemma above in a form that is easier to use for us.

Corollary 31
.

Consider a set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
. Given an
\Leftrightarrow
-formula
\varphi
and any subset
\alpha\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}
, we let
\mathsf{TrueNbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi)\in\mathbb{N}
denote the number of propositions in
\mathsf{Prop}\setminus\alpha
that occur oddly many times in
\varphi
. Then, for all
\alpha\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}
, we have:

\alpha^{\omega}\models\varphi\text{ if and only if }\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi)\text{ and }\mathsf{TrueNbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi)\text{ have the same parity}

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Lemma
30
and the fact that
\mathsf{TrueNbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi)
and
\mathsf{NbPr}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\varphi)
have the same parity.
∎

This corollary suggests that the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\{\neg\},\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\},\emptyset,\infty)
learning problem is linked to modulo 2-calculus. In fact, there exists an
\mathsf{NP}
-hard decision problem dealing with modulo 2-calculus, which we define below. The definition of this problem, and the proof that it is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete can be found in
[
5
]
.

Definition 32

(Coset Weight)
.

We denote by
\mathsf{CW}
the following decision problem:

•

Input:
(A,k,y)
where
A
is
n\times l
-matrix on
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
,
k\leq l
is an integer and
y
is an
n
-vector in
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
.

•

Output: yes iff there is an
l
-vector
x
in
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
with at most
k
components of value 1 such that
A\cdot x=y
in
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
.

In terms of representation, the integer
k
may be given in unary.

Since we are going to manipulate matrices, we introduce below the relevant notations.

Definition 33
.

Given
n,l\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, an
n\times l
-matrix
A
has
n
rows and
l
columns. Furthermore, for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq l
,
A_{i,j}
refers to the coefficient of the matrix
A
at the intersection of the
i
-th row and
j
-th column.

Let us now define the reduction that we consider.

Definition 34
.

Consider an instance
(A,k,y)
of the coset weight problem
\mathsf{CW}
. If
y=0
(i.e. it is the null vector), then
(A,k,y)
is trivially a positive instance of
\mathsf{CW}
, hence in that case
\mathsf{In}^{\Leftrightarrow}_{(A,k,y)}
is defined as any positive instance of the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\{\neg\},\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\},\emptyset,\infty)
decision problem. Otherwise, we define:

•

\mathsf{Prop}:=\{a_{j}\mid 1\leq j\leq l\}
to be the set of propositions;

•

For all
1\leq i\leq n
, we set
\alpha_{i}:=\{a_{j}\mid 1\leq j\leq l,\;A_{i,j}=0\}^{\omega}
;

•

\mathcal{P}:=\{\beta\}\cup\{\alpha_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n,\;y[i]=0\}
with
\beta:=\mathsf{Prop}^{\omega}
;

•

\mathcal{N}:=\{\alpha_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n,\;y[i]=1\}
;

•

B=2k
.

Then, we define the input
\mathsf{In}^{\Leftrightarrow}_{(A,k,y)}:=(\mathsf{Prop},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N},B)
of the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\{\neg\},\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\},\emptyset,\infty)
decision problem.

The definition above satisfies the lemma below.

Lemma 35
.

Consider a set
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
of logical binary operators and assume that
\emptyset\neq\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\}
. Then, for all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\{\lU,\lW\}
,
(A,k,y)
is a positive instance of the coset weight problem
\mathsf{CW}
if and only if
\mathsf{In}^{\Leftrightarrow}_{(A,k,y)}
is a positive instance of the the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem.

Proof.

If
y=0
, the equivalence is straightforward. Let us now assume in the following that
y\neq 0
. In that case, we have
\mathcal{P}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{N}\neq\emptyset
.

Assume that
(A,k,y)
is a positive instance of the coset weight problem
\mathsf{CW}
and consider an
l
-vector
x
in
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
with at most
k
components of value 1 such that
Ax=y
. Since
y\neq 0
,
x
has at least one component of value 1. We let
H:=\{a_{j}\mid 1\leq j\leq l,\;x[j]=1\}
. We write
H=\{a_{j_{1}},\ldots,a_{j_{r}}\}
with
1\leq r\leq k
. We then consider the formulas:

•

\varphi_{\Leftrightarrow}:=a_{j_{1}}\Leftrightarrow a_{j_{2}}\Leftrightarrow\ldots\Leftrightarrow a_{j_{r}}

•

We let
\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}}^{\mathsf{even}}:=a_{j_{1}}\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\;a_{j_{2}}\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\ldots\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\;a_{j_{r}}
. We also define:

\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}}:=\begin{cases}\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}}^{\mathsf{even}}&\text{ if }r\text{ is even}\\
\neg\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}}^{\mathsf{even}}&\text{ if }r\text{ is odd}\end{cases}

Both of these formulas have size at most
2r\leq 2k=B
.

By definition, we have both
\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi_{\Leftrightarrow})
and
\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}})
even. Therefore, by Corollary
31
, both
\varphi_{\Leftrightarrow}
and
\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}}
accept the word
\beta
. Consider now some
1\leq i\leq n
.

•

Assume that
y[i]=0
. Then, they are evenly many indices
j\in[1,\ldots,l]
such that
x[j]=1=A_{i,j}
. That is,
|H\setminus\alpha_{i}|
is even. Hence, by Corollary
31
,
\varphi_{\Leftrightarrow},\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}}
accept
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
.

•

Assume that
y[i]=1
. Then, they are oddly many indices
j\in[1,\ldots,l]
such that
x[j]=1=A_{i,j}
. That is,
|H\setminus\alpha_{i}|
is odd. Hence, by Corollary
31
,
\varphi_{\Leftrightarrow},\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}}
reject
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{N}
.

Hence,
\varphi_{\Leftrightarrow},\varphi_{\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}}
accept
\mathcal{P}
and reject
\mathcal{N}
. Thus,
\mathsf{In}^{\Leftrightarrow}_{(A,k,y)}
is a positive instance of the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem.

Assume now that
\mathsf{In}^{\Leftrightarrow}_{(A,k,y)}
is a positive instance of the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem. Consider an
\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
-formula
\varphi
of size at most
B
that accepts
\mathcal{P}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}
. By Lemma
29
, there is a
\Leftrightarrow
-formula
\varphi^{\prime}
of size at most
B
that is equivalent to the formula
\varphi
on size-1 infinite words. By Corollary
31
, since
\varphi^{\prime}
accepts
\beta
, it follows that
\mathsf{Neg}(\varphi^{\prime})
is even. We let
H:=\{i\in[1,\ldots,l]\mid a_{i}\text{ occurs oddly many times in }\varphi^{\prime}\}
. By Lemma
12
, we have
H\leq k
. We define the
l
-vector
x
in
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
by, for all
1\leq j\leq l
,
x[j]:=1
if and only if
j\in H
. Consider now any
1\leq i\leq n
.

•

If
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
, we have
|H\setminus\alpha_{i}[1]|
even, by Corollary
31
. In addition, for all
j\in H\setminus\alpha_{i}[1]
, we have
x[j]=1=A_{i,j}
whereas, for all
j\notin H\setminus\alpha_{i}
, we have
x[j]=0
or
A_{i,j}=0
. Therefore,
Ax[i]=0=y[i]
.

•

If
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{N}
, we have
|H\setminus\alpha_{i}|
odd, by Corollary
31
. Therefore,
Ax[i]=1=y[i]
.

Hence,
(A,k,y)
is a positive instance of the coset weight problem
\mathsf{CW}
.
∎

We deduce the corollary below.

Corollary 36
.

Consider a set
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
of binary logical operators with
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\cap\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\}\neq\emptyset
. For all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
, the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Proof.

If we have
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\cap\{\lor,\Rightarrow,\Leftarrow,\wedge,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow},\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow},\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor},\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\}\neq\emptyset
, Corollary
17
or Corollary
23
gives that the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard. Furthermore, if
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\cap\{\lW,\lM\}\neq\emptyset
, then Corollary
27
gives that the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard. If it is not the case, then we have both
\emptyset\neq\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\{\Leftrightarrow,\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}\}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\{\lU,\lW\}
. The result then follows from Lemma
35
, the fact that the Coset Weight decision problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard and the fact that the instance
\mathsf{In}^{\Leftrightarrow}_{(l,C,k)}
can be computed in logarithmic space from
(l,C,k)
.
∎

3.2.6
Proof of Theorem
10
: with the temporal operators
\lU
and
\lR

We conclude with the temporal operators
\lU
and
\lR
. We will consider a reduction from the hitting set problem again. However, as can be seen in Lemma
25
, with these operators, a reduction with size-1 infinite words will not work since they simplify too much on them.

We define below a way to build infinite words or
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas from subsets of integers.

Definition 37
.

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
. We consider a set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}^{l}:=\{a_{i,j},b_{i,j}\mid 1\leq i\leq j\leq l\}
. Consider any set subset
C\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
. For all
1\leq i\leq l
, we let
\mathsf{S}_{i}(C):=\{(k,j)\mid 1\leq k\leq i,\;i\leq j,j\in C\}
. Then, we let:

w^{\lR}(C):=w_{1}(C)\cdot\ldots\cdot w_{l}(C)\cdot\emptyset^{\omega}\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}^{l}})^{\omega}

and

w^{\lU}(C):=w_{1}(C)\cdot\ldots\cdot w_{l}(C)\cdot(\mathsf{Prop}^{l})^{\omega}\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}^{l}})^{\omega}

where, for all
1\leq i\leq l
, we have:

w_{i}(C):=\{a_{k,j}\mid(k,j)\in\mathsf{S}_{i}(C)\}\cup\{b_{k,j}\mid(k,j)\notin\mathsf{S}_{i}(C)\}

Furthermore, consider any
H=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
with
i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}<\ldots<i_{r}
. We let
i_{0}:=1
and we define:

•

\varphi_{\lR}(H,r):=a_{i_{r-1},i_{r}}
, and
\varphi_{\lU}(H,r):=b_{i_{r-1},i_{r}}
;

•

for all
2\leq p\leq r
,
\varphi_{\lR}(H,p-1):=\varphi_{\lR}(H,p)\;\lR\;a_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}
, and
\varphi_{\lU}(H,p-1):=\varphi_{\lU}(H,p)\;\lR\;b_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}
.

We let
\varphi_{\lR}(H):=\varphi_{\lR}(H,1)
and
\varphi_{\lU}(H):=\varphi_{\lU}(H,1)
. That way, we have:

\varphi_{\lR}(H)=((a_{i_{r-1},i_{r}}\;\lR\;a_{i_{r-2},i_{r-1}})\;\lR\;\ldots\;)\lR\;a_{1,i_{1}}

and

\varphi_{\lU}(H)=((b_{i_{r-1},i_{r}}\;\lU\;b_{i_{r-2},i_{r-1}})\;\lU\;\ldots\;)\lU\;b_{1,i_{1}}

We first establish below what the
\lR
-formula satisfies.

Lemma 38
.

Consider any
H=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
with
i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}<\ldots<i_{r}
and some
C\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
. We have the following equivalence:

H\subseteq C\text{ if and only if }w^{\lR}(C)\models\varphi_{\lR}(H)

Proof.

For all
r\geq p\geq 1
, we let
H_{p}:=\{i_{p},\ldots,i_{r}\}\subseteq H
. Let us show by induction on
r\geq p\geq 1
the property
\mathcal{P}(p)
:

For all
1\leq i\leq i_{p-1}-1
:
w^{\lR}(C)[i:]\not\models\varphi_{\lR}(H,p)
and
w^{\lR}(C)[i_{p-1}:]\models\varphi_{\lR}(H,p)
iff
H_{p}\subseteq C

We start with the base case
\mathcal{P}(r)
. For all
1\leq i\leq i_{r-1}-1
, we have
a_{i_{r-1},i_{r}}\notin w^{\lR}(C)[i]
. Furthermore, we have
a_{i_{r-1},i_{r}}\in w^{\lR}(C)[i_{r-1}]
if and only if
i_{r}\in C
. Since
\varphi_{\lR}(H,r)=a_{i_{r-1},i_{r}}
,
\mathcal{P}(r)
follows.

Let us now assume that
\mathcal{P}(p)
holds for some
2\leq p\leq r
. We have:

\varphi_{\lR}(H,p-1)=\varphi_{\lR}(H,p)\;\lR\;a_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}

For all
1\leq i\leq i_{p-2}-1
, we have
a_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}\notin w^{\lR}(C)[i]
, thus
w^{\lR}(C)[i:]\not\models a_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}
. In addition, by
\mathcal{P}(p)
, for all
1\leq i\leq i_{p-1}-1
:
w^{\lR}(C)[i:]\not\models\varphi_{\lR}(H,p)
(in particular, this holds for
i=i_{p-2}
). It follows that, for all
1\leq i\leq i_{p-2}-1
, we have
w^{\lR}(C)[i:]\not\models\varphi_{\lR}(H,p-1)
.

•

Assume that
H_{p-1}\subseteq C
. Then, we also have
H_{p}\subseteq C
, thus, by
\mathcal{P}(p)
, we have
w^{\lR}(C)[i_{p-1}:]\models\varphi_{\lR}(H,p)
. Furthermore, for all
i_{p-2}\leq i\leq i_{p-1}
, we have
w^{\lR}(C)[i]\models a_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}
since
i_{p-1}\in C
. It follows that:

w^{\lR}(C)[i_{p-2}:]\models\varphi_{\lR}(H,p-1)

•

Assume that
H_{p-1}\not\subseteq C
. Then, there are two cases, since
H_{p-1}=H_{p}\cup\{i_{p-1}\}
.

–

Assume that
H_{p}\not\subseteq C
. By
\mathcal{P}(p)
, for all
1\leq i\leq i_{p-1}
, we have
w^{\lR}(C)[i:]\not\models\varphi_{\lR}(H,p)
. Furthermore,
w^{\lR}(C)[i_{p-1}+1:]\not\models a_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}
. Therefore,
w^{\lR}(C)[i_{p-2}:]\not\models\varphi_{\lR}(H,p-1)
.

–

Assume that
i_{p-1}\notin C
. Then, for all
1\leq i\leq l
, we have
w^{\lR}(C)[i:]\not\models a_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}
. Furthermore, by
\mathcal{P}(p)
, for all
1\leq i\leq i_{p-1}-1
:
w^{\lR}(C)[i:]\not\models\varphi_{\lR}(H,p)
. It follows that
w^{\lR}(C)[i_{p-2}:]\not\models\varphi_{\lR}(H,p-1)
.

Hence, the property
\mathcal{P}(p-1)
holds. Therefore,
\mathcal{P}(p)
holds for all
1\leq p\leq r
. The lemma is then given by
\mathcal{P}(1)
, since
H_{1}=H
,
w^{\lR}(C)[1:]=w^{\lR}(C)
and
\varphi_{\lR}(H,1)=\varphi_{\lR}(H)
.
∎

Let us now consider the case of
\lU
-formulas.

Lemma 39
.

Consider any
H=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
with
i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}<\ldots<i_{r}
and some
C\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
. We have the following equivalence:

H\subseteq C\text{ if and only if }w^{\lU}(C)\not\models\varphi_{\lU}(H)

Proof.

Let us show by induction on
r\geq p\geq 1
the property
\mathcal{P}(p)
: for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, we have
w^{\lU}(C)[k:]\models\varphi_{\lU}(H,p)
if and only if
w^{\lR}(C)[k:]\not\models\varphi_{\lR}(H,p)
.

Let us start with the base case. We have
\varphi_{\lU}(H,r)=b_{i_{r-1},i_{r}}
and
\varphi_{\lR}(H,r)=a_{i_{r-1},i_{r}}
. Furthermore, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, we have
a_{i_{r-1},i_{r}}\in w^{\lU}(C)[k]
if and only if
b_{i_{r-1},i_{r}}\notin w^{\lR}(C)[k]
. Thus,
\mathcal{P}(r)
follows.

Assume now that
\mathcal{P}(p)
holds for some
2\leq p\leq r
. We have:

\varphi_{\lU}(H,p-1)=\varphi_{\lU}(H,p)\;\lU\;b_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}

and

\varphi_{\lR}(H,p-1)=\varphi_{\lR}(H,p)\;\lR\;a_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}

Thus, by definition of the operator
\lR
, we have
\varphi_{\lR}(H,p-1)
equivalent to
\neg(\neg\varphi_{\lR}(H,p)\lU\neg a_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}})
. In addition, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, we have
b_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}\in w^{\lU}(C)[i]
iff
a_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}\in w^{\lR}(C)[i]
. Thus, by
\mathcal{P}(p)
, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, we have:

\displaystyle w^{\lU}(C)[k:]\models\varphi_{\lU}(H,p-1)

\displaystyle\text{ iff }\exists j\geq k,\;b_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}\in w^{\lU}(C)[j]\text{ and }\forall k\leq i<j,\;w^{\lU}(C)[i:]\models\varphi_{\lU}(H,p)

\displaystyle\text{ iff }\exists j\geq k,\;a_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}\notin w^{\lR}(C)[j]\text{ and }\forall k\leq i<j,\;w^{\lR}(C)[i:]\not\models\varphi_{\lR}(H,p)

\displaystyle\text{ iff }w^{\lR}(C)[k:]\models\neg\varphi_{\lR}(H,p)\lU\neg a_{i_{p-2},i_{p-1}}

\displaystyle\text{ iff }w^{\lR}(C)[k:]\models\neg\varphi_{\lR}(H,p-1)

Thus, the property
\mathcal{P}(p-1)
holds. In fact, it holds for all
1\leq p\leq r
. This lemma is then a direct consequence of the property
\mathcal{P}(1)
and Lemma
39
.
∎

We can now define the reduction that we consider.

Definition 40
.

Consider an instance
(l,C,k)
of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
. We define:

•

\mathsf{Prop}:=\mathsf{Prop}^{l}=\{a_{i,j},b_{i,j}\mid 1\leq i\leq j\leq l\}
as set of propositions;

•

\mathcal{P}^{\lU}:=\{v_{1}^{\lU},\ldots,v_{n}^{\lU}\}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\lR}:=\{v^{\lR}\}
;

•

\mathcal{N}^{\lU}:=\{v^{\lU}\}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\lR}:=\{v_{1}^{\lR},\ldots,v_{n}^{\lR}\}
;

•

B=2k-1
.

with, for
\bullet\in\{\lR,\lU\}
, we let
v^{\bullet}:=w^{\bullet}([1,\ldots,l])\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})
and for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we let
v_{i}^{\bullet}:=w^{\bullet}([1,\ldots,l]\setminus C_{i})\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})
.

Then, we define the inputs
\mathsf{In}^{\lR}_{(l,C,k)}:=(\mathsf{Prop},\mathcal{P}^{\lR},\mathcal{N}^{\lR},B)
and
\mathsf{In}^{\lU}_{(l,C,k)}:=(\mathsf{Prop},\mathcal{P}^{\lU},\mathcal{N}^{\lU},B)
of the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem.

This definition satisfies the lemma below.

Lemma 41
.

Let
\bullet\in\{\lR,\lU\}
. Consider a set
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}
of operators and assume that
\bullet\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\neq\emptyset
. Then, for all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
,
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
if and only if
\mathsf{In}^{\bullet}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of the the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem.

Proof.

Assume that
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
. Consider a hitting set
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
. We consider the formulas
\varphi_{\lR}:=\varphi_{\lR}(H)
and
\varphi_{\lU}:=\varphi_{\lU}(H)
. Clearly, these formulas have size
2k-1=B
. In addition, Lemmas
38
and
39
gives that
v^{\lU}\not\models\varphi_{\lU}
and
v^{\lR}\models\varphi_{\lR}
. Consider now some
1\leq i\leq n
. Since
H\cap C_{i}\neq\emptyset
, it follows that
H\not\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]\setminus C_{i}
, hence, by Lemmas
38
and
39
, we have
v_{i}^{\lU}\models\varphi_{\lU}
and
v_{i}^{\lR}\not\models\varphi_{\lR}
. Therefore, for
\bullet\in\{\lR,\lU\}
, we have
\mathsf{In}^{\bullet}_{(l,C,k)}
a positive instance of the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem.

Assume now that
\mathsf{In}^{\bullet}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem. Consider an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula
\varphi
of size at most
B=2k-1
that accepts
\mathcal{P}^{\bullet}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}
. We let
H:=\{\alpha\in[1,\ldots,l]\mid\exists 1\leq i\leq l,\;\{a_{i,\alpha},b_{i,\alpha}\}\cap\mathsf{Prop}(\varphi)\neq\emptyset\}
. By Lemma
12
, we have
|H|\leq k
. Let us show that it is a hitting set. Consider some
1\leq p\leq n
.
Given tow sets
A
and
B
, we let
A\Delta B
denote the symmetric difference:
A\;\Delta\;B:=A\setminus B\cup B\setminus A
. Then, we have that:

\forall 1\leq i\leq l,\;v_{p}^{\bullet}[i]\;\Delta\;v^{\bullet}[i]=\{a_{k,j},b_{k,j}\mid 1\leq k\leq i,\;i\leq j,j\in C_{p}\}

and

\forall i\geq l,\;v_{p}^{\bullet}[i]=v^{\bullet}[i]

Hence, by Lemma
11
, it follows that
\mathsf{Prop}(\varphi)
must contain at least a variable
a_{k,j}
or
b_{k,j}
with
j\in C_{p}
. That is,
C_{p}\cap H\neq\emptyset
. Since this holds for all
1\leq p\leq n
, it follows that
H
is a hitting set and
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
.
∎

We obtain the corollary below.

Corollary 42
.

Consider a set
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
of binary temporal operators with
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\cap\{\lU,\lR\}\neq\emptyset
. For all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
, the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
decision problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Lemma
41
and the fact that the instances
\mathsf{In}^{\lU}_{(l,C,k)}
and
\mathsf{In}^{\lR}_{(l,C,k)}
can be computed in logarithmic space from
(l,C,k)
.
∎

The proof of Theorem
10
follows.

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of Corollaries
17
,
23
,
36
,
27
and
42
.
∎

3.3
\mathsf{CTL}
learning is at least as hard as
\mathsf{LTL}
learning

Let us now turn to
\mathsf{CTL}
learning. Our goal is to show that
\mathsf{CTL}
learning is at least as hard as
\mathsf{LTL}
learning, under logarithmic space reductions, regardless of the operators allowed. This is formally stated in the theorem below.

Theorem 43
.

For all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, the decision problem
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
is at least as hard as the decision problem
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
, under logarithmic space reduction.

Therefore, it is also the case of the decision problems
\mathsf{ATL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}^{k}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
, for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
.

The proof of this theorem consists in translating ultimately periodic words into Kripke structures, translating
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas into
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas, and vice versa, and relating all these translations together.

Let first translate ultimately periodic words into Kripke structure of the same size.

Definition 44
.

Consider a set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
and an ultimately periodic word
w=u\cdot v^{\omega}\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})^{\omega}
. We
k:=|u|
and
n:=|v|
. Then, we define the Kripke structure
K_{w}=\langle Q,I,\mathsf{Prop},\pi,\mathsf{Succ}\rangle
where:

•

Q:=\{q^{u}_{1},\ldots,q^{u}_{k},q^{v}_{1},\ldots,q^{v}_{n}\}
;

•

I:=\{q_{1}^{u}\}
;

•

for all
1\leq i\leq k-1
, we have
\mathsf{Succ}(q^{u}_{i}):=\{q^{u}_{i+1}\}
,
\mathsf{Succ}(q^{u}_{k}):=\{q^{v}_{1}\}
and for all
1\leq i\leq n-1
, we have
\mathsf{Succ}(q^{v}_{i}):=\{q^{v}_{i+1}\}
,
\mathsf{Succ}(q^{v}_{n}):=\{q^{v}_{1}\}
;

•

for all
1\leq i\leq k
,
\pi(q^{u}_{i}):=u[i]\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}
and for all
1\leq i\leq n
,
\pi(q^{v}_{i}):=v[i]\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}
.

We then define below how to translate
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas into
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas and vice versa, while staying in the same fragment.

Definition 45
.

Consider a set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
and
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
. Then, we let
\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}:\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})\rightarrow\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
be such that it removes all
\exists
or
\forall
quantifiers from a
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula, while keeping the same operators. More formally:

•

\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(p)=p
for all
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
;

•

\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\neg\phi)=\neg\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi)
;

•

\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\diamond(\bullet\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi))=\bullet\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi))
for all
\bullet\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\}
and
\diamond\in\{\exists,\forall\}
;

•

\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2})=\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi_{1})\bullet\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi_{2})
for all
\bullet\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
;

•

\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\diamond(\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2}))=\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi_{1})\bullet\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi_{2})
for all
\bullet\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}
and
\diamond\in\{\exists,\forall\}
.

We also define the function
\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}:\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})\rightarrow\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
that adds an
\exists
quantifier before every temporal operator of an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula, thus transforming it into a
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula. More formally:

•

\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(p)=p
for all
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
;

•

\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\neg\varphi)=\neg\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\varphi)
;

•

\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\bullet\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\varphi)=\exists(\bullet\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\varphi)))
for all
\bullet\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\}
;

•

\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\varphi_{1}\bullet\varphi_{2})=\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\varphi_{1})\bullet\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{O}_{U},\mathsf{O}_{B})}(\varphi_{2})
for all
\bullet\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
;

•

\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\varphi_{1}\bullet\varphi_{2})=\exists(\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\varphi_{1})\bullet\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\varphi_{2}))
for all
\bullet\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}
.

A direct proof by induction shows that the above definition satisfies the proposition below:

Proposition 46
.

Consider a set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
and
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
. We have:

•

for all
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas
\phi\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
,
|\phi|\geq|\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi)|
(the size may decrease for instance for the
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula
\phi=\exists\lX p\lor\forall\lX p
);

•

for all
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas
\varphi\in\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
,
|\varphi|=|\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\varphi)|
;

•

for all
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas
\varphi\in\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
, we have
\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\varphi))=\varphi
.

Interestingly for us, Definition
45
also satisfies the lemma below, which is slightly less direct to show that Proposition
46
above.

Lemma 47
.

Consider a set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
and
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
. Let
\phi\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
be a
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula. For all ultimately periodic words
w=u\cdot v^{\omega}\in 2^{\mathsf{Prop}}
, we have
K_{w}\models\phi
if and only if
w\models\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi)
.

Proof.

Let
k:=|u|
and
n:=|v|
. In the Kripke structure
K_{w}
, all states have exactly one successor. Hence, for all states
q\in Q
, we have
|\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q)|=1
and we let
\rho_{q}\in Q^{\omega}
be the infinite path such that
\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q)=\{\rho_{q}\}
.

Let
g:\mathbb{N}_{1}\rightarrow Q
be such that for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
we have:

\displaystyle g(i):=\begin{cases}q^{u}_{i}&\text{ if }i\leq k\\
q^{v}_{1+(i-k-1\mod n)}&\text{ if }i\geq k+1\end{cases}

For all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, we have
\mathsf{Succ}(g(i))=\{g(i+1)\}
. Indeed, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, we have:

•

if
i\leq k-1
, then
g(i)=q^{u}_{i}
and
g(i+1)=q^{u}_{i+1}
;

•

g(k)=q^{u}_{k}
and
g(k+1)=q^{v}_{1}
;

•

if
i\geq k+1
and
l:=(i-k-1)\mod n\leq n-2
, then
g(i)=q^{v}_{l+1}
and
g(i+1)=q^{v}_{l+2}
;

•

if
i\geq k+1
and
(i-k-1)\mod n=n-1
, then
g(i)=q^{v}_{n}
and
g(i+1)=q^{v}_{1}
.

It follows that, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, we have
w[i:]=\pi(\rho_{g(i)})\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})^{\omega}
.

Now, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, we denote by
K_{w}^{i}
the Kripke structure that is equal to the Kripke structure
K_{w}
except that the initial state is now
g(i)
, i.e.
I=\{g(i)\}
. Let us show by induction on
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas
\phi\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
the property
\mathcal{P}(\phi)
: for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, we have
K_{w}^{i}\models\phi
if and only if
w[i:]\models\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi)
. Consider some
\phi\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
. We have:

•

Assume that
\phi=x
for any
x\in\mathsf{Prop}
. In that case,
\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi)=x
. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
. We have

K_{w}^{i}\models\phi
iff
x\in\pi(q_{g(i)})=w[i]
iff
w[i:]\models\phi
. Hence,
\mathcal{P}(\phi)
holds;

•

Assume that
\phi=\neg\phi^{\prime}
for some
\phi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
. In that case, we have
\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi)=\neg\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi^{\prime})
. Hence,
\mathcal{P}(\phi)
is a straightforward consequence of
\mathcal{P}(\phi^{\prime})
.

•

For all
\bullet\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
, assume that
\phi=\diamond(\bullet\phi^{\prime})
for some
\diamond\in\{\exists,\forall\}
and that
\mathcal{P}(\phi^{\prime})
holds. In that case, we have
\varphi:=\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi)=\bullet\varphi^{\prime}
with
\varphi^{\prime}:=\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\phi^{\prime})
. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
. Since
|\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(g(i))|=1
, it follows that
K_{w}^{i}\models\exists(\bullet\phi^{\prime})
iff
K_{w}^{i}\models\forall(\bullet\phi^{\prime})
iff:

–

If
\bullet=\lX
:
K_{w}^{i+1}\models\phi^{\prime}
iff
w[i+1:]\models\varphi^{\prime}
(by
\mathcal{P}(\phi^{\prime})
) iff
w[i:]\models\varphi
;

–

If
\bullet=\lF
: there is some
j\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
K_{w}^{i+j}\models\phi^{\prime}
iff there is some
j\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
w[i+j:]\models\varphi^{\prime}
(by
\mathcal{P}(\phi^{\prime})
) iff
w[i:]\models\bullet\varphi
;

–

If
\bullet=\lG
: for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
K_{w}^{i+j}\models\phi^{\prime}
iff for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
w[i+j:]\models\varphi^{\prime}
(by
\mathcal{P}(\phi^{\prime})
) iff
w[i:]\models\bullet\varphi
.

Hence,
\mathcal{P}(\phi)
holds.

•

The case of binary operators is similar.

In fact,
\mathcal{P}(\phi)
holds for all
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas
\phi\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
. The lemma follows.
∎

We can now define the reduction that we consider.

Definition 48
.

Consider an instance
\mathsf{In}_{\mathsf{LTL}}=(\mathsf{Prop},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N},B)
of the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}
decision problem. We define the input
\mathsf{In}_{\mathsf{CTL}}=(\mathsf{Prop},\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{N}^{\prime},B)
with:

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}:=\{M_{w}\mid w\in\mathcal{P}\}

and

\mathcal{N}^{\prime}:=\{M_{w}\mid w\in\mathcal{N}\}

Clearly this reduction can be computed in logarithmic space. Let us now show that it satisfies the desired property.

Lemma 49
.

Consider a set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
, sets of operators
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{tp}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. Then, the input
\mathsf{In}_{\mathsf{LTL}}
is a positive instance of the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},k)
decision problem if and only if
\mathsf{In}_{\mathsf{CTL}}
is a positive instance of the
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},k)
decision problem.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathsf{In}_{\mathsf{LTL}}
is a positive instance of the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})
decision problem. Let
\varphi_{\mathsf{LTL}}
be an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula in
\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},k)
separating
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{N}
of size at most
B
. Consider the
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula
\varphi_{\mathsf{CTL}}:=\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{L}\text{-}\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\varphi_{\mathsf{LTL}})
. By Proposition
46
, we have
|\varphi_{\mathsf{CTL}}|=|\varphi_{\mathsf{LTL}}|\leq B
and
\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\varphi_{\mathsf{CTL}})=\varphi_{\mathsf{LTL}}
.
Consider now any
M_{w}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
with
w\in\mathcal{P}
. By Lemma
47
, we have
M_{w}\models\varphi_{\mathsf{CTL}}
if and only if
w\models\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\varphi_{\mathsf{CTL}})=\varphi_{\mathsf{LTL}}
. Since
w\in\mathcal{P}
, it follows that
w\models\varphi_{\mathsf{LTL}}
and
M_{w}\models\varphi_{\mathsf{CTL}}
. This is similar for any
M_{w}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
with
w\in\mathcal{N}
. Hence,
\mathsf{In}_{\mathsf{CTL}}
is a positive instance of the
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},k)
decision problem.

Assume now that
\mathsf{In}_{\mathsf{CTL}}
is a positive instance of the
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},k)
decision problem. Let
\varphi_{\mathsf{CTL}}
be a
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula separating
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
of size at most
B
. Consider the
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula
\varphi_{\mathsf{LTL}}:=\mathsf{f}_{\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}})}(\varphi_{\mathsf{CTL}})
. By Proposition
46
, we have
|\varphi_{\mathsf{LTL}}|\leq|\varphi_{\mathsf{CTL}}|\leq B
. Consider now any
w\in\mathcal{P}
. By Lemma
47
, we have
M_{w}\models\varphi_{\mathsf{CTL}}
if and only if
w\models\varphi_{\mathsf{LTL}}
. Since
M_{w}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, it follows that
M_{w}\models\varphi_{\mathsf{CTL}}
and
w\models\varphi_{\mathsf{LTL}}
. This is similar for any
w\in\mathcal{N}
. Hence,
\mathsf{In}_{\mathsf{LTL}}
is a positive instance of the
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},k)
decision problem.
∎

The proof of Theorem
43
is now direct.

Proof.

It is straightforward consequence of Lemma
49
and the fact that the reduction from Definition
48
can be computed in logarithmic space.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, one can straightforwardly simulate a Kripke structure by a concurrent game structure with
k
agents by making
k-1
agents having only one action. Hence,
\mathsf{ATL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}^{k}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
is at least as hard as
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{t}},\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
, under logarithmic space reduction.
∎

4
Learning formulas using only unary operators

We have seen that, regardless of the operators considered,
\mathsf{CTL}
learning (and therefore
\mathsf{ATL}
learning as well) is at least as hard as
\mathsf{LTL}
learning. Furthermore, we have also seen that
\mathsf{LTL}
learning is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard as soon as any non-unary operator is allowed. In this section, we focus on the learning problems without non-unary operators in order to be able to distinguish the complexity of
\mathsf{LTL}
,
\mathsf{CTL}
and different kinds of
\mathsf{ATL}
learning. In this setting, we show that:

•

\mathsf{LTL}
learning can now be decided in logarithmic space. To establish this result, we use simple results on equivalences of
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas. Some of these equivalences were already proven in
[
24
]
.

•

\mathsf{CTL}
learning remains
\mathsf{NP}
-complete, this is proved again via a reduction from the hitting set problem. The reduction relies heavily on the use of the next operator
\lX
. On the other hand,
\mathsf{CTL}
learning without the next operator
\lX
is equivalent to the
\mathsf{CTL}
learning with formulas of size at most 5, and is in
\mathsf{NL}
(i.e. it can be decided in non-deterministic logarithmic space).

•

On the other hand,
\mathsf{ATL}
learning with at least two agents and at least two operators in
\{\lF,\lG,\neg\}
is still
\mathsf{NP}
-complete. The reduction is an adaptation of the reduction for the
\mathsf{CTL}
case that makes use of the additional players to mimic the behavior of the next operator
\lX
with both the eventually and globally operators
\lF
and
\lG
. This is the most technical reduction of the paper. However, if one only allows the use of either
\lF
or
\lG
without negations, then the
\mathsf{ATL}
learning with at most two agents can be decided in polynomial time as, given a bound
k
and a set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
, the number of formulas to check is polynomial in
k
and
|\mathsf{Prop}|
.

•

Finally,
\mathsf{ATL}
learning with at least three agents remains
\mathsf{NP}
-complete even if only of the three operators
\lX,\lF,\lG
is allowed. The reduction is an adaptation of the previous one where the third player is used to replace one of the operators
\lF
or
\lG
. Hence, in this setting, we do not distinguish, complexity-wise, the
\mathsf{ATL}
learning problems with a fixed number of agents (at least 3) and a number of agents as part of the input. This will be done in the next section.

4.1
\mathsf{LTL}
learning

We first focus on the case of
\mathsf{LTL}
learning. The goal of this subsection is to show the proposition below.

Proposition 50
.

For all sets
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
, and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the decision problem
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
is in
\mathsf{L}
.

To establish this proposition, we first consider
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas that do not use any binary operators. First of all, since we consider ultimately periodic words, we have the (well-known, see for instance
[
24
, Proposition 8]
) equivalences below.

Observation 51
.

Consider a non-empty set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
and some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas
\varphi
on
\mathsf{Prop}
, we have:

1.

\lF\lX^{k}\varphi\equiv\lX^{k}\lF\varphi

2.

\lG\lX^{k}\varphi\equiv\lX^{k}\lG\varphi

3.

\lF\lF\varphi\equiv\lF\varphi

4.

\lG\lG\varphi\equiv\lG\varphi

5.

\lF\lG\lF\varphi\equiv\lG\lF\varphi

6.

\lG\lF\lG\varphi\equiv\lF\lG\varphi

Proof.

Consider an ultimately periodic word
w=u\cdot v^{\omega}\in(2^{\mathsf{Prop}})^{\omega}
. We prove the first, third and fifth items, the other ones are obtained by duality.

1.

We have
w\models\lF\lX^{k}\varphi
iff there is some
i\geq 1
such that
w[i:]\models\lX^{k}\varphi
iff there is some
i\geq 1
such that
w[i+k:]\models\varphi
iff there is some
j\geq k+1
such that
w[j:]\models\varphi
iff
w[k+1:]\models\lF\varphi
iff
w\models\lX^{k}\lF\varphi
.

3.

We have
w\models\lF\lF\varphi
iff there is some
i\geq 1
such that
w[i:]\models\lF\varphi
iff there is some
i\geq 1
and some
j\geq i
such that
w[i+j:]\models\varphi
iff there is some
j\geq 1
such that
w[j:]\models\varphi
iff
w\models\lF\varphi
.

5.

Straightforwardly, we have
\lG\lF\varphi\implies\lF\lG\lF\varphi
. On the other hand, assume that
w\models\lF\lG\lF\varphi
, i.e. that there is some
i\geq 1
such that
w[i:]\models\lG\lF\varphi
. Thus, for all
j\geq i
, we have
w[j:]\models\lF\varphi
, i.e. there is some
k_{j}\geq j
such that
w[k_{j}:]\models\varphi
. Let us show that
w\models\lG\lF\varphi
. Let
j\geq 1
and
j^{\prime}:=\max(i,j)\geq i
. We have
w[k_{j^{\prime}}:]\models\varphi
, hence
w[j:]\models\lF\varphi
since
k_{j^{\prime}}\geq j^{\prime}\geq j
. Since this holds for all
j\geq 1
, we have
w\models\lG\lF\varphi
.

∎

In turn, let us consider the definition below of sequences of
\mathsf{LTL}
-operators that we will consider.

Definition 52
.

Consider some
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\{\lX,\lF,\lG,\neg\}
.
We let:

\mathsf{Qt}_{\lX}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}):=\{\mathsf{Y}^{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N},\;\mathsf{Y}\in\{\epsilon\}\cup(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\cap\{\lX\})\}

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{Qt}_{\lF,\lG}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}):=\begin{cases}\{\lF,\lG,\lF\lG,\lG\lF\}&\text{ if }\lF,\lG\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\\
\{\lF\}&\text{ if }\lF\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\neg\notin\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\\
\{\lF,\neg\lF\neg,\lF\neg\lF,\neg\lF\neg\lF\}&\text{ if }\lF,\neg\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\\
\{\lG\}&\text{ if }\lG\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\neg\notin\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\\
\{\lG,\neg\lG\neg,\neg\lG\neg\lG,\lG\neg\lG\neg\}&\text{ if }\lG,\neg\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\\
\{\epsilon\}&\text{ if }\lF,\lG\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\end{cases}

and

\mathsf{Qt}_{\neg}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}):=\{\epsilon\}\cup\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\cap\{\neg\}

Then, we let:

\mathsf{SeqQt}_{\mathsf{LTL}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}):=\{\mathsf{Q}_{\lX}\cdot\mathsf{Q}_{\lF,\lG}\cdot\mathsf{Q}_{\neg}\mid\mathsf{Q}_{\lX}\in\mathsf{Qt}_{\lX}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}),\;\mathsf{Q}_{\lF,\lG}\in\mathsf{Qt}_{\lF,\lG}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}),\;\mathsf{Q}_{\neg}\in\mathsf{Qt}_{\neg}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}})\}

We deduce the corollary below.

Corollary 53
.

Consider a non-empty set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
, and some
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\{\lX,\lF,\lG,\neg\}
. For any
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula
\varphi=\mathsf{Qt}\cdot\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
, where
\mathsf{Qt}
is a sequence of operators and
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
is an
\mathsf{LTL}
-formula, there is a sequence of operators
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{SeqQt}_{\mathsf{LTL}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}})
such that, for
\varphi^{\prime\prime}:=\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
, we have
\varphi\equiv\varphi^{\prime\prime}
and
|\varphi^{\prime\prime}|\leq|\varphi|
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Observation
51
and the fact that, for all
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas
\varphi
, we have
\varphi\equiv\neg\neg\varphi
,
\lF\varphi\equiv\neg\lG\neg\varphi
and
\lG\varphi\equiv\neg\lF\neg\varphi
.
∎

Let us now consider
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas with (a bounded amount of occurrences of) binary operators.

Definition 54
.

Consider a non-empty set of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
, and some
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
. We define inductively the set
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)\subseteq\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
:

•

p\in\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
, for all
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
;

•

\mathsf{Qt}\cdot\varphi\in\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
, for all
\mathsf{Qt}\in\mathsf{SeqQt}_{\mathsf{LTL}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}})
and
\varphi\in\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
;

•

\varphi_{1}\bullet\varphi_{2}\in\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
, for all
\bullet\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
and
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)
.

Then, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the set of
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
is defined by
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n):=\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},\infty)\cap\mathsf{LTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
.

To conclude and prove Proposition
50
, we establish the three following facts, for sets
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
: 1) for all instances
I=(\mathsf{Prop},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N},k)
of the decision problem
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
,
I
is a positive instance if and only if there is an
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
-formula of size at most
k
accepting
\mathcal{P}
and rejecting
\mathcal{N}
. Furthermore, let us fix a bound
n\in\mathbb{N}
on the number of occurrences of binary operators, then: 2) the number of
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
-formulas of size at most
k
is polynomial in
k
and
|\mathsf{Prop}|
; and 3) there is a logarithmic-space algorithm that decides if an ultimately-periodic word satisfies
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
-formulas.

Before we argue that these facts hold, let us show that they directly imply Proposition
50
.

Proof.

Consider the decision problem
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
. With fact 1, deciding if an instance
(\mathsf{Prop},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N},k)
is positive amounts to deciding the existence of an
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
-formula of size at most
k
accepting
\mathcal{P}
and rejecting
\mathcal{N}
. With facts 2) and 3), we can design a logarithmic space algorithmic solving that problem: it suffices to have a counter with which we enumerate all the polynomially-many
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
-formulas to consider, and then check if one them does accept
\mathcal{P}
and reject
\mathcal{N}
.
∎

Let us now argue that these three facts hold. The first two facts are rather straightforward. Indeed, Fact 1) is direct consequence of Corollary
53
. In addition, Fact 2) is a direct consequence (which can be proved straightforwardly by induction on
n
) of the fact that the number of sequences of operators in
\mathsf{SeqQt}_{\mathsf{LTL}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}})
of size at most
k
is polynomial (in fact, linear) in
k
. As it is more involved, we state Fact 3 in a lemma below. Its proof concludes this subsection.

Lemma 55
.

Consider a bound
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The following decision problem can be decided in logarithmic space:

•

Input: an
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
-formula
\varphi
, and an ultimately periodic word
w=u\cdot v^{\omega}
;

•

Output: Yes iff
w\models\varphi
.

Proof.

The recursive algorithm depicted in Figure
1
(in which
\bullet
refers to a binary operator) straightforwardly solves the decision problem (by simply following the
\mathsf{LTL}
semantics). We have to argue that it can be implemented in logarithmic space. To execute the algorithmic, it is sufficient to keep a pointer to the current position in the word, plus additional pointers:

•

To keep track of the sub-formulas currently being evaluated, and the intermediary results already computed, which is necessary with binary operators. Since there are at most
n
occurrences of binary operators, the total number of intermediary results to keep track of is bounded by
2^{n}
.

•

To keep track of the indices being evaluated, which is necessary with the
\lF
and
\lG
operators. However, since we consider
\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
-formulas, between binary operators, there is at most two
\lF
operators and
\lG
operators. Thus, again, the amount of pointers sufficient is bounded by
2n
.

Overall, the total number of pointers sufficient to keep track of everything is bounded, independently of the input. Thus, Algorithm
\mathsf{DecideLTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}
can be implemented in logarithmic space.
∎

Figure 1:
Algorithm
\mathsf{DecideLTL}_{\mathsf{Unif}}(\varphi,w,i)
.

4.2
Abstract recipe for
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness proofs and binary operators

As can be seen in Table
1
, we are going to establish three
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness results in the following cases:
\mathsf{CTL}
-learning with the operator
\lX
,
\mathsf{ATL}
-learning with two agents and both operators
\lF
and
\lG
, and
\mathsf{ATL}
-learning with three agents and the operator
\lF
, or the operator
\lG
. Although these reductions differ, they all share a similar structure. The goal of this subsection is: first, to give the abstract recipe that we follow for all these reductions; and second, to state and prove the relevant lemmas to handle binary operators in our reductions.

4.2.1
Abstract recipe

Since the formulas to learn can only contain a bounded amount of binary operators, we cannot define a hitting set from a (small enough) separating formula by looking at the propositions that it uses, as we have done for LTL in Section
3
. Instead, our reduction focuses on formulas using only unary operators (and therefore a single proposition). In that context,we create a sample (and a bound
B
) such that all separating (unary) formulas have a specific shape, and there is bijection between subsets
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
and formulas
\phi(l,H)
of that specific shape. This correspondence allows us to
extract a hitting set. Note that, however, although the the formulas that we consider can only use a bounded amount of binary operators, they can still use some binary operators. Therefore, to be able to only focus on unary operators, we first need to control how the (separating) formulas use binary operators. More specifically, we follow the abstract recipe below, from an
(l,C,k)
of the hitting set problem:

a)

We first handle binary operators. That is, given any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and some binary operators
\bullet_{2}\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
, we consider
n
propositions
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
and several positive
\mathcal{P}_{n,\bullet}
and negative
\mathcal{N}_{n,\bullet}
structures such that a separating formula has to use binary operators to express properties with the
n
propositions
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
.

b)

We can now focus on unary formulas. We define the bound
B
and additional positive
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{Un}}
and negative
\mathcal{N}_{\mathsf{Un}}
structures (using only the proposition
p
) that “eliminate” certain (unary) operators or pattern of (unary) operators. This way we ensure that any unary formula separating
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{Un}}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\mathsf{Un}}
will be of the form
\phi(l,H)
, for some
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
.

c)

We can finally encode the hitting set problem itself. We define a negative structure (on
p
) that a unary formula
\phi(l,H)
accepts if and only if
|H|\geq k+1
.

d)

For all
1\leq i\leq n
, we define a positive structure (on
p
) that a unary formula
\phi(l,H)
accepts if and only if
H\cap C_{i}\neq\emptyset
.

By construction, the instance of the learning decision problem that we obtain is a positive instance if and only if
(l,C,k)
also is.

4.2.2
Handling binary operators

The statement of this section deal with
\mathsf{ATL}
-structures and formulas because they will be used for the next three
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness proof.

Notations and definitions

First of all, we will use notations akin to that of regular languages to describe the formulas that we will consider.

Notation 56
.

For any
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
and set of operators
O
, we denote by
O^{*}\;\phi
the set of
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas beginning with finitely many operators in
O
followed by the
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
. Furthermore, when
O
is not a singleton, its elements may be enumerated with commas.

For the
\mathsf{ATL}
-reductions, we will use turn-based game structures, where, at each state, only one agent is choosing the next state. Note that Kripke structure can be seen as turn-based structures, with only one player.

Definition 57
.

Given any coalition of agents
A
, an
A
-turn-based game structure
T
is defined by a tuple
T=\langle Q,I,\mathsf{Prop},\pi,\mathsf{AgSt},\mathsf{Succ}\rangle
where
\mathsf{AgSt}:Q\rightarrow A
maps every state to an agent in
A
and
\mathsf{Succ}:Q\rightarrow 2^{Q}
maps every state
q\in Q
its set of successor states where the agent
\mathsf{AgSt}(q)
can choose to go. Note that when a state has only one successor, i.e. one outgoing edge, the identity of the agent owning the state is irrelevant. The coalitions
A
of agents that we consider are always such that
A\subseteq\{1,2,3\}
.

Interestingly for us, when evaluated on turn-based structures,
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas satisfy the classical equivalences w.r.t. negations. Let us introduce below a notation that refers to the dual of the operators considered.

Definition 58
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
,
A\subseteq[1,\ldots,k]
, and
\mathsf{H}\in\{\lX,\lF,\lG\}
, we let:

\overline{\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\mathsf{H}}^{k}:=\langle\langle[1,\ldots,k]\setminus A\rangle\rangle\overline{\mathsf{H}}

where
\overline{\lX}:=\lX
,
\overline{\lF}:=\lG
and
\overline{\lG}:=\lF
.

This definition satisfies the proposition below.

Proposition 59
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
and
\mathsf{Ag}:=[1,\ldots,k]
. For all
A\subseteq\mathsf{Ag}
,
\mathsf{H}\in\{\lX,\lF,\lG\}
and any
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
, when evaluated on
\mathsf{Ag}
-turn-based game structures, for all coalitions of agents
A\subseteq[1,\ldots,k]
, we have the equivalence
\neg\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\mathsf{H}\phi\equiv\overline{\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\mathsf{H}}^{k}\neg\phi
.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the fact that two-player (with one player representing the coalition
A
and the other the coalition
\mathsf{Ag}\setminus A
) turn-based reachability/safety games (respectively for the
\lF/\lG
operator) are determined: from any starting state, either one of the players has winning (positional) strategies.
∎

We can use this property to define a way to remove negations from sequences of unary operators, while maintaining the semantics of the formula (and not increasing the size). This is done in the definition.

Definition 60
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, and
\mathsf{Ag}:=[1,\ldots,k]
. For
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
, we let
\mathsf{Op}(k,\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}):=\{\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\;\mathsf{H}\mid A\subseteq\mathsf{Ag},\;\mathsf{H}\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\}\cup\{\neg\}\cap\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
. We define inductively on
(\mathsf{Op}(k,\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}))^{+}
a function
\mathsf{UnNeg}:(\mathsf{Op}(k,\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}))^{*}\times\{0,1\}\rightarrow(\mathsf{Op}(k,\{\lX,\lF,\lG,\neg\}))^{*}\times\{0,1\}
as follows:

•

For all
x\in\{0,1\}
, we let
\mathsf{UnNeg}(\epsilon,x):=(\epsilon,x)
.

•

For all
\mathsf{O}\in\mathsf{Op}(k,\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}})
,
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\mathsf{Op}(k,\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}))^{*}
, and
x\in\{0,1\}
, we let:

\mathsf{UnNeg}(\mathsf{O}\cdot\mathsf{Qt},x):=\begin{cases}(\mathsf{O}\cdot\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime},x^{\prime})\text{ if }x=0\\
(\overline{\mathsf{O}}^{k}\cdot\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime},x^{\prime})\text{ if }x=1\end{cases}

for
(\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime},x^{\prime}):=\mathsf{UnNeg}(\mathsf{Qt},x)
.

•

For all
x\in\{0,1\}
and
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\mathsf{Op}(k,\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}))^{*}
, we let
\mathsf{UnNeg}(\neg\mathsf{Qt},x):=\mathsf{UnNeg}(\mathsf{Qt},1-x)
.

This definition satisfies the lemma below.

Lemma 61
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
,
\mathsf{Ag}:=[1,\ldots,k]
,
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
, and
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\mathsf{Op}(k,\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}))^{*}
. We have:

•

For all
x\in\{0,1\}
, letting
(\mathsf{Qt},y):=\mathsf{UnNeg}(\mathsf{Qt},x)
, we have
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\in(\mathsf{Op}(k,\{\lX,\lF,\lG\}))^{*}
and if
\lF\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
if and only if
\lG\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
, then
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\in(\mathsf{Op}(k,\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\}))^{*}
;

•

For all
x\in\{0,1\}
,
(\mathsf{Qt},y):=\mathsf{UnNeg}(\mathsf{Qt},x)
, we have
|\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}|\leq|\mathsf{Qt}|
;

•

Consider an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
. For all
i\in\{0,1\}
, we let
(\mathsf{Qt}_{i},x_{i}):=\mathsf{UnNeg}(\mathsf{Qt},i)
and
\psi_{i}:=\phi
if
x_{i}=0
, and
\psi_{i}:=\neg\phi
otherwise. Then, we have
\mathsf{Qt}\cdot\phi\equiv\mathsf{Qt}_{0}\cdot\psi_{0}
and
\neg\mathsf{Qt}\cdot\phi\equiv\mathsf{Qt}_{1}\cdot\psi_{1}
.

Proof.

The first item follows from Definition
58
. The second item follows from Definition
60
. The third item can be obtained by induction on
\mathsf{Qt}
, by successively applying Proposition
59
.
∎

Now, let us define the kind of turn-based game structures that we will consider. Note that there is a slight difference with the informal explanations of Section
4.2.1
: in addition to the
n
propositions
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
, we do not consider a single proposition
p
but rather two propositions
p
and
\bar{p}
. This is done to negate formulas without using negations (i.e. informally,
\bar{p}
will be equivalent to
\neg p
on the structures that we will consider).

Definition 62
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We let
\mathsf{Prop}_{n}:=\{p,\bar{p}\}\cup\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
where
\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}:=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}
be two sets of propositions. Then, for all
S\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
, a turn-based structure
T
is
(n,S)
-proper
if, for all states
q
in
T
, we have
\pi(q)\in\{S\cup\{p\},S\cup\{\bar{p}\}\}
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
and
\mathsf{Ag}:=[1,\ldots,k]
. Given any two
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
, we denote by
\phi\equiv_{k,n,S}\phi^{\prime}
the fact that
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
are equivalent on
(n,S)
-proper
\mathsf{Ag}
-turn-based structures, i.e. for all proper turn-based structures
T
, we have
T\models\phi
if and only if
T\models\phi^{\prime}
.

A structure
T
is trivial if it contains a single self-looping state. It can be seen as a turn-based structure with any number of agents. In addition, such a structure is entirely defined by the set
S
of propositions labeling the unique state of the structure. It is denoted
T(S)
.

We can now state the two lemmas that will let us properly handle binary operators in our
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness proofs. The first one, Lemma
63
, states that from an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
using
k
binary operators and featuring
k
propositions in
\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
(for some
k\leq n
), for all
S\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
, we can extract a unary
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi^{\prime}
equivalent to
\phi
on
S
-proper structures. This is stated below.

Lemma 63
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
. Consider a set of unary operators
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
such that
\lG\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
if and only if
\lF\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
, and some set of binary logical operator
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\leq n
. For all
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\phi\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},i)
such that
|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)\cap\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}|=i
and for all
S\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
, there are two
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\psi,\widehat{\psi}\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\{p,\bar{p}\},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},0)
such that:

•

|\psi|,|\widehat{\psi}|\leq|\phi|-2i
; and

•

\phi\equiv_{k,n,S}\psi
and
\neg\phi\equiv_{k,n,S}\widehat{\psi}
.

The second lemma, Lemma
64
, shows the existence, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and binary operators
\bullet\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
, of the two sets of structures
\mathcal{P}_{n,\bullet}
and
\mathcal{N}_{n,\bullet}
mentioned in Step
a)
in the abstract recipe described in Section
4.2.1
. It is stated below.

Lemma 64
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
. Consider a binary operator
\bullet\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
and some
n\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
. There is some
S_{n,\bullet}\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
and two sets trivial structures
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
and
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
such that:

•

If an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
distinguishes
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
and
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
, then
\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)
.

•

There is an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi_{n,\bullet}\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\mathsf{Prop}^{n},\emptyset,\emptyset,\{\bullet\},n)
of size
2n-1
and such that, for all positive and negative sets
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N}
of
(n,S_{n,\bullet})
-proper structures, there is
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{N}^{\prime})\in\{(\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet},\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}),(\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet},\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet})\}
such that, for all
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\phi\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\mathsf{Prop}^{n},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},0)
, we have:

\phi
accepts
\mathcal{P}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}
if and only if
\psi:=\phi\bullet\phi_{n,\bullet}
accepts
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}

With both of those lemmas, we are able to show the theorem below that allows to prove the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of the learning decision problems that we consider, with an arbitrary bound on the number of occurrences of binary operators, by showing the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of the learning problem without binary operator.

Theorem 65
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
,
\mathsf{Ag}:=[1,\ldots,k]
, and
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\in\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
. Assume that there is a function
f_{k}
computable in logarithmic space that takes an input an instance
(l,C,k^{\prime})
of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
and returns an instance of the learning decision problem
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{Un},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
such that for all instances
(l,C,k^{\prime})
:

•

The set of propositions in
f_{k}((l,C,k^{\prime}))
is
\mathsf{Prop}_{0}
;

•

All structures in
f_{k}((l,C,k^{\prime}))
are
(0,\emptyset)
-proper
\mathsf{Ag}
-turn-based structures;

•

The three statements below are equivalent:

–

(l,C,k^{\prime})
is a positive instance of
\mathsf{Hit}
;

–

f_{k}((l,C,k^{\prime}))
is a positive instance of
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
;

–

f_{k}((l,C,k^{\prime}))
is a positive instance of
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\cup\{\lF,\lG\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
.

Then, for all
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the decision problem
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. As mentioned in Proposition
6
, the decision problem
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
is in
\mathsf{NP}
. Let us now show that it in
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

If
n=0
or
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}=\emptyset
, the two problems
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
and
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
are the same. Furthermore, or all instances
(l,C,k^{\prime})
of
\mathsf{Hit}
, we have that if
f_{k}((l,C,k^{\prime}))
is a positive instance of
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
, then it is also a positive instance of
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
, and similarly if
f_{k}((l,C,k^{\prime}))
is a positive instance of
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
, then it is also a positive instance of
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\cup\{\lF,\lG\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
. Therefore,
f_{k}((l,C,k^{\prime}))
is a positive instance of
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
if and only if it is a positive instance
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
. We can conclude that the decision problem
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Assume now that we have
n\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\neq\emptyset
. We let
\bullet\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
and we consider the set of propositions
S_{n,\bullet}\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
and the two sets of trivial structures
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
and
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
from Lemma
64
, along with the formula
\phi_{n,\bullet}\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\mathsf{Prop}^{n},\emptyset,\emptyset,\{\bullet\},n)
of size at most
2n-1
. Now, consider an instance
(l,C,k^{\prime})
of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
. Let
\mathsf{In}_{0}:=f_{k}((l,C,k^{\prime}))=(\mathsf{Prop}_{0},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N},B)
. Note that all structures in
\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{N}
are
(0,\emptyset)
-proper
\mathsf{Ag}
-turn-based structures. We let
\widehat{\mathcal{P}},\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
be two sets of
\mathsf{Ag}
-turn-based structures equal to
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N}
respectively except that the labels of all states is changed from
x\in\mathsf{Prop}_{0}=\{p,\bar{p}\}
to
S_{n,\bullet}\cup\{x\}\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}_{n}
. That way, all the turn-based structures in
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
are
(n,S_{n,\bullet})
-proper structures. Then, we let
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{N}^{\prime})\in\{(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}\cup\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet},\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\cup\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}),(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\cup\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet},\widehat{\mathcal{P}}\cup\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet})\}
be as in the second point of Lemma
64
and we define the input
\mathsf{In}_{n}:=(\mathsf{Prop}_{n},\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{N}^{\prime},B+2n)
of the decision problem
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
. Note that, the structures in
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
and
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
are defined independently of the input
(l,C,k^{\prime})
and, by assumption, the input
\mathsf{In}_{0}
can be computed in logarithmic space from the instance
(l,C,k^{\prime})
. Therefore, the input
\mathsf{In}_{n}
can also be computed in logarithmic space from the instance
(l,C,k^{\prime})
.

Let us show that
\mathsf{In}_{n}
is a positive instance of the decision problem
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
if and only if
\mathsf{In}_{0}
is a positive instance decision problem
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
.

Assume that
\mathsf{In}_{n}
is a positive instance of the decision problem
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
. Consider a formula
\phi\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{Prop}_{n},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)\subseteq\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{Prop}_{n},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\cup\{\lF,\lG\},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
of size at most
B+2n
that accepts
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.
In that case, the formula
\phi
distinguishes
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
and
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
, and therefore, by Lemma
64
, we have
\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)
. Hence, by Lemma
63
, there are two formulas
\psi,\widehat{\psi}\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{Prop}_{0},(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\cup\{\lF,\lG\})\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
such that
|\psi|,|\widehat{\psi}|\leq|\phi|-2n\leq B
and
\phi\equiv_{k,n,S_{n,\bullet}}\psi
and
\neg\phi\equiv_{k,n,S_{n,\bullet}}\widehat{\psi}
. Let
\phi^{\prime}\in\{\psi,\widehat{\psi}\}
be such that
\phi^{\prime}=\psi
if and only if
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{N}^{\prime})=(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}\cup\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet},\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\cup\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet})
. That way, we have that
\phi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{Prop}_{n},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\cup\{\lF,\lG\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
accepts the set
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
and rejects the set
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
. Since
\mathsf{Prop}(\phi^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}_{0}=\{p,\bar{p}\}
, and by definition of
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
, it follows that
\phi^{\prime}
also accepts
\mathcal{P}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}
. Thus,
\mathsf{In}_{0}
is a positive instance of the decision problem
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\cup\{\lF,\lG\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
, and is therefore also a positive instance of the decision problem
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
.

Assume now that
\mathsf{In}_{0}
is a positive instance of the decision problem
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
. Consider an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{Prop}_{0},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
of size at most
B
that accepts
\mathcal{P}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}
. We consider the
\mathsf{ATL}
formula
\phi^{\prime}:=\phi\bullet\phi_{n,\bullet}\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{Prop}_{0},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
. We have
|\phi^{\prime}|\leq|\phi|+|\phi_{n,\bullet}|+1\leq B+2n-1+1=B+2n
. Furthermore, by definition of the sets
\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
, we have that
\phi^{\prime}
accepts
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
since
\phi
accepts
\mathcal{P}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}
4
4

4

Note that, to apply Lemma
64
, it is crucial that
\phi
does not feature negations.
. Thus,
\mathsf{In}_{n}
is a positive instance of the decision problem
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
.

Overall, we obtain that
\mathsf{In}_{n}
is a positive instance of the decision problem
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
if and only if
(l,C,k^{\prime})
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
. Hence, the decision problem
\mathsf{ATL}^{k}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.
∎

Let us now prove these two Lemmas
63
and
64
. We start with the proof of Lemma
63
, but before we proceed to it, we state and prove below a crucial lemma regarding the shape of formulas that use a bounded amount of binary operators and feature many propositions.

Lemma 66
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, for all
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas

\phi
, if
|\phi|_{\mathsf{Bin}}=k
, then
|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)|\leq k+1
. In addition, if

|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)|=k+1
, and
\phi=\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2}
for some binary operator
\bullet\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
, and
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
, then
\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{1})\cap\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{2})=\emptyset
.

Proof.

We prove this lemma by induction on
k
. It straightforwardly holds for
k=0
. Assume now that it holds for all
i\leq k
, for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, consider an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
for which
|\phi|_{\mathsf{Bin}}=k+1
. Let us first assume that
\phi=\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2}
, for some with binary operator
\bullet
, and
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
. We are going to modify the formula
\phi_{1}
. To do so, we consider a fresh proposition
x\notin\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)
. Then, for all
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\psi,\psi^{\prime}
, we let
\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\psi^{\prime}):=x
if
\psi\in\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})
, and

\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\psi^{\prime}):=\psi^{\prime}
otherwise. Now, we define inductively a function
\mathsf{tr}_{x}
on
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas as follows:

•

for all propositions
p
, we let
\mathsf{tr}_{x}(p):=\mathsf{ch}_{x}(p,x)
;

•

for all unary operators
\bullet_{1}
and
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\psi
, we let
\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\bullet_{1}\psi):=\bullet_{1}\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi))
;

•

for all binary operators
\bullet_{2}
, and
\psi,\psi^{\prime}
two
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas, we let
\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi\bullet_{2}\psi^{\prime}):=\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi))\bullet_{2}\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi^{\prime},\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi^{\prime}))
.

We are going to apply the function
\mathsf{tr}_{x}
to the formula
\phi_{1}
, and then use the properties satisfied by the obtained formula. Thus,
we show by induction on
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\psi
the property
\mathcal{P}(\psi)
:
\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi)))\cap\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})=\emptyset
and
\mathsf{SubBin}(\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi)))\subseteq\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\mathsf{SubBin}(\psi)\setminus\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{2}))
. First of all, for all
