\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas, if
\psi\in\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})
, then
\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi))=x
, in which case
\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi)))\cap\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})=\emptyset
, and
\mathsf{SubBin}(\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi)))=\emptyset
. Hence,
\mathcal{P}(\psi)
always holds in that case. Thus, we will focus on the cases where
\psi\notin\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})
and
\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi))=\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi)
.

Furthermore,
\mathcal{P}(p)
straightforwardly holds for all propositions
p
. Assume now that
\mathcal{P}(\psi)
holds for some
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\psi
. Consider any unary operator
\bullet_{1}
. We have
\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\bullet_{1}\;\psi)=\bullet_{1}\;\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi))
, hence
\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\bullet_{1}\;\psi))=\{\bullet_{1}\;\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi))\}\cup\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi)))
and
\mathsf{SubBin}(\bullet_{1}\;\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi))=\mathsf{SubBin}(\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi))
. Thus,
\mathcal{P}(\bullet_{1}\;\psi)
follows directly from
\mathcal{P}(\psi)
.

Assume now that
\mathcal{P}(\psi_{1}),\mathcal{P}(\psi_{2})
hold for some
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
. Consider any binary operator
\bullet_{2}
. We have
\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi\bullet_{2}\psi^{\prime}):=\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi))\bullet_{2}\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi^{\prime},\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi^{\prime}))
, hence:

\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi\bullet_{2}\psi^{\prime}))=\{\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi\bullet_{2}\psi^{\prime})\}\cup\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi)))\cup\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi^{\prime},\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi^{\prime})))

Since
\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi)))\cap\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})=\emptyset
(by
\mathcal{P}(\psi)
) and
\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi^{\prime},\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi^{\prime})))\cap\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})=\emptyset
(by
\mathcal{P}(\psi^{\prime})
), it follows that
\mathsf{SubF}(\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi\bullet_{2}\psi^{\prime}))\cap\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})=\emptyset
. Furthermore, we have:

\mathsf{SubBin}(\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi\bullet_{2}\psi^{\prime}))=\{\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi\bullet_{2}\psi^{\prime})\}\cup\mathsf{SubBin}(\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi,\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi)))\cup\mathsf{SubBin}(\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\psi^{\prime},\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi^{\prime})))

with
\{\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\psi\bullet_{2}\psi^{\prime})\}=\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\{\psi\bullet_{2}\psi^{\prime}\})
, and for any
\tilde{\psi}\in\{\psi,\psi^{\prime}\}
, we have
\mathsf{SubBin}(\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\tilde{\psi},\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\tilde{\psi})))\subseteq\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\mathsf{SubBin}(\tilde{\psi})\setminus\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{2}))
(by
\mathcal{P}(\tilde{\psi})
). Thus,
\mathcal{P}(\psi\bullet_{2}\psi^{\prime})
follows from the fact that, if
\psi\bullet_{2}\psi^{\prime}\notin\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})
, then
\mathsf{SubBin}(\psi\bullet_{2}\psi^{\prime})\setminus\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{2})=\{\psi\bullet_{2}\psi^{\prime}\}\cup(\mathsf{SubBin}(\psi)\setminus\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{2}))\cup(\mathsf{SubBin}(\psi^{\prime})\setminus\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{2}))
. In fact,
\mathcal{P}(\psi)
holds for all
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\psi
.

Now, let
\phi_{1}^{x}:=\mathsf{ch}_{x}(\phi_{1},\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\phi_{1}))
and
\phi^{x}:\phi_{1}^{x}\bullet\phi_{2}
. By
\mathcal{P}(\phi_{1}^{x})
, we have the following facts:

•

\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{1}^{x})\cap\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})=\emptyset
;

•

\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi^{x})=\{\phi^{x}\}\cup\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{1}^{x})\cup\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{2})
and
\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi)=\{\phi\}\cup\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{1})\cup\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{2})
. Since
\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{1}^{x})\subseteq\mathsf{tr}_{x}(\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{1})\setminus\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{2}))
, we have:

\displaystyle|\phi^{x}|_{\mathsf{Bin}}=|\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi^{x})|

\displaystyle=1+|\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{1}^{x})|+|\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq 1+|\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{1})\setminus\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{2})|+|\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi_{2})|

\displaystyle=|\mathsf{SubBin}(\phi)|=|\phi|_{\mathsf{Bin}}

That is,
|\phi^{x}|_{\mathsf{Bin}}\leq|\phi|_{\mathsf{Bin}}
;

•

If
\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{1})\cap\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})=\emptyset
, then
\phi^{x}=\phi
and
\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)=\mathsf{Prop}(\phi^{x})
. Otherwise, we have
\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)\cup\{x\}=\mathsf{Prop}(\phi^{x})
(it can be proved straightforwardly by induction).

Therefore, we have
|\phi^{x}|_{\mathsf{Bin}}=|\phi_{1}^{x}|_{\mathsf{Bin}}+|\phi_{2}|_{\mathsf{Bin}}+1\leq k+1
. Hence, we can apply our induction hypotheses to
|\phi_{1}^{x}|_{\mathsf{Bin}}\leq k
and
|\phi_{2}^{x}|_{\mathsf{Bin}}\leq k
to obtain that:
|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{1}^{x})|\leq|\phi_{1}^{x}|_{\mathsf{Bin}}+1
,
|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{2})|\leq|\phi_{2}|_{\mathsf{Bin}}+1
. Hence,
|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)|\leq|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi^{x})|=|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{1}^{x})|+|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{2})|\leq k+2
. In addition, if
\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{1})\cap\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})\neq\emptyset
, then
|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)|<|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi^{x})|
, and thus
|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)|<k+2
.

If instead there is an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi^{\prime}=\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2}
, for some
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
and binary operator
\bullet
, such that
\phi=\mathsf{Qt}\cdot\phi^{\prime}
where
\mathsf{Qt}
is a non-empty sequence of unary operators, we can apply the above arguments to the formula
\phi^{\prime}
. Thus, the property holds also for
k+1
. In fact, it holds for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The lemma follows.
∎

We can now proceed to the proof of Lemma
63
.

Proof.

First of all, note that for all
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\phi
such that
\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
, and for all
S\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
, there is
\psi\in\{\mathsf{True},\mathsf{False}\}
such that we have
\phi\equiv_{k,n,S}\psi
. This can be straightforwardly established by induction on
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\phi
.

Now, let us show by induction on
0\leq i\leq n
the property
\mathcal{P}(i)
: for all
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\phi\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},i)
such that
|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)\cap\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}|=i
, and for all
S\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
, there are two
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\psi,\widehat{\psi}\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\{p,\bar{p}\},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},0)
such that:
|\psi|,|\widehat{\psi}|\leq|\phi|-2i
,
\phi\equiv_{k,n,S}\psi
, and
\neg\phi\equiv_{k,n,S}\widehat{\psi}
.

Consider first the case
i=0
. Let
\phi\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},0)
. We have
\phi:=\mathsf{Qt}\cdot r
for some
r\in\{p,\bar{p}\}
and
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\mathsf{Op}(k,\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}))^{*}
. For
j\in\{0,1\}
, we let
(\mathsf{Qt}_{j},x_{j}):=\mathsf{UnNeg}(\mathsf{Qt},j)
and
r_{j}\in\{p,\bar{p}\}
be such that
r_{j}=r
if and only if
x_{j}=0
. Then, we let
\psi:=\mathsf{Qt}_{0}\cdot r_{0}
and
\widehat{\psi}:=\mathsf{Qt}_{1}\cdot r_{1}
. By Lemma
61
, we have:
\psi,\widehat{\psi}\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},0)
and
|\psi|,|\widehat{\psi}|\leq|\psi|
. Furthermore, we also have, for all
S\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
,
\phi\equiv_{k,n,S}\psi
, and
\neg\phi\equiv_{k,n,S}\widehat{\psi}
, again by Lemma
61
, and since
p\equiv_{k,n,S}\neg\bar{p}
(by definition of
(n,S)
-proper structures). Thus,
\mathcal{P}(0)
holds.

Assume now that it
\mathcal{P}(j)
holds for all
j\leq i
, for some
i\leq n-1\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},i+1)
such that
|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)\cap\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}|=i+1
. Let
S\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
. If
|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)|=i+1
, then we have
|\phi|\geq 2i+3
by Lemma
12
5
5

5

This lemma is established for
\mathsf{LTL}
-formulas, but its result can be applied to
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas as well
.
Furthermore, there is
\psi,\widehat{\psi}\in\{\mathsf{True},\mathsf{False}\}
of size 1 such that,
\phi\equiv_{k,n,S}\psi
and
\neg\phi\equiv_{k,n,S}\widehat{\psi}
. Assume now that
|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)|>i+1
. Since
\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)\leq|\phi|_{\mathsf{Bin}}+1\leq i+2
(by Lemma
66
), it follows that
|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)|=i+2
. Let
x\in\{p,\bar{p}\}
be such that
x\in\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)
. Then, there is some sequence of unary operators
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\mathsf{Op}(k,\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}))^{*}
, a binary operator
\bullet
and two
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},i)
such that
\phi=\mathsf{Qt}\cdot(\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2})
. Thus,
\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)=\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2})
. Since we have
\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2}\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},i+1)
and
|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2})|=i+2
, it follows that
\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{1})\cap\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{2})=\emptyset
, by Lemma
66
. Without loss of generality, let us assume that
x\in\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{1})
. In that case, we have
\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{2})\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
. Therefore, as mentioned at the beginning of this proof, there is some
\psi_{\mathsf{tr}}\in\{\mathsf{True},\mathsf{False}\}
such that
\phi_{2}\equiv_{k,S}\psi_{\mathsf{tr}}
. Hence,
\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2}\equiv_{k,S}\phi_{1}\bullet\psi_{\mathsf{tr}}
. It follows, since
\bullet
is a binary logical operator, that there is some
\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}\in\{\mathsf{True},\mathsf{False},\phi_{1},\neg\phi_{1}\}
such that
\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2}\equiv_{k,n,S}\Psi
and
\neg(\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2})\equiv_{k,n,S}\widehat{\Psi}
. Now, let
m_{1}:=|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{1})\cap\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}|\leq i
. We have
m_{2}:=|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{2})|
such that
m_{1}+m_{2}=|\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)|=i+1
. In addition, by
\mathcal{P}(m_{1})
,
\Psi
and
\widehat{\Psi}
can be chosen such that
\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\{p,\bar{p}\},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},0)
and
|\Psi|,|\widehat{\Psi}|\leq|\phi_{1}|-2m_{1}
. In addition, by Lemma
12
, we have
|\phi_{2}|\geq 2m_{2}-1
. Overall, we obtain that
\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}
can be chosen such that (note that since
\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{1})\cap\mathsf{Prop}(\phi_{2})=\emptyset
, we have
\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{1})\cap\mathsf{SubF}(\phi_{2})=\emptyset
):

\displaystyle|\Psi|,|\widehat{\Psi}|

\displaystyle\leq|\phi_{1}|-2m_{1}=|\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2}|-(|\phi_{2}|+1)-2m_{1}

\displaystyle\leq|\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2}|-2m_{2}-2m_{1}=|\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2}|-2(i+1)

Then, for
j\in\{0,1\}
, we let
(\mathsf{Qt}_{j},x_{j}):=\mathsf{UnNeg}(\mathsf{Qt},j)
and
\psi_{j}\in\{\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}\}
be such that
\psi_{j}\equiv_{k,n,S}\Psi
if and only if
x_{j}=0
. Then, we let
\psi:=\mathsf{Qt}_{0}\cdot\psi_{0}
and
\widehat{\psi}:=\mathsf{Qt}_{1}\cdot\psi_{1}
. By Lemma
61
, we have:
\psi,\widehat{\psi}\in\mathsf{ATL}^{k}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},0)
,
\phi\equiv_{k,n,S}\psi
and
\neg\phi\equiv_{k,n,S}\widehat{\psi}
, and
|\mathsf{Qt}_{0}|,|\mathsf{Qt}_{1}|\leq|\mathsf{Qt}|
. Thus, we have
|\psi|=|\mathsf{Qt}_{0}|+|\psi_{0}|\leq|\mathsf{Qt}|+|\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2}|-2(i+1)=|\phi|-2(i+1)
, and similarly for
\widehat{\psi}
. Thus,
\mathcal{P}(i+1)
follows. In fact,
\mathcal{P}(i)
holds for all
0\leq i\leq n
. The lemma is then given by
\mathcal{P}(n)
.
∎

Let us consider Lemma
64
. Before we proceed to its proof, we state below a useful lemma analogous to Lemma
11
.

Lemma 67
.

For all sets of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
, for all
Y\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}
, and
S,S^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}
, if, for all
x\in Y
, we have
x\in S
if and only if
x\in S^{\prime}
, then an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
such that
\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)\subseteq Y
cannot distinguish the trivial structures
T(S)
and
T(S^{\prime})
.

Proof.

A straightforward proof by induction on
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas establishes the lemma.
∎

We can now proceed to the proof of Lemma
64
.

Proof.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

Assume that
\bullet=\lor
. The cases
\bullet=\Rightarrow
and
\bullet=\Leftarrow
are analogous.
We let
S_{n,\bullet}:=\emptyset
and, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we let
S_{i}:=\{p_{i}\}
. Then, we define:
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}:=\{T(S_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
,
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}:=\{T(S_{n,\bullet})\}
, and
\phi_{n,\bullet}:=p_{1}\lor\ldots\lor p_{n}
.

That way, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, to distinguish
T(S_{i})
and
T(S)
, an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
needs to be such that
p_{i}\in\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)
, by Lemma
67
. Hence, if an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
separates
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
and
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
, we have
\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)
. Consider now any two positive and negative sets
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N}
of
(n,S_{n,\bullet})
-proper structures. Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
. Consider any formula
\phi\in\mathsf{ATL}(\mathsf{Prop}^{n},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},0)
and
\psi:=\phi\lor\phi_{n,\bullet}
. For all
1\leq i\leq n
, we have
T(S_{i})\models\phi_{n,\bullet}
and
T(S_{n,\bullet})\not\models\phi_{n,\bullet}
. Furthermore, since
\phi
does not use negations, we also have
T(S_{n,\bullet})\not\models\phi
. In addition, by definition of
S_{n,\bullet}
, for all
(n,S_{n,\bullet})
-proper structures
T
, we have
T\not\models\phi_{n,\bullet}
. Thus, we have that
\phi
accepts
\mathcal{P}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}
if and only if
\psi
accepts
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.

•

Assume now that
\bullet=\wedge
. The cases
\bullet=\prescript{\neg}{}{\Rightarrow}
and
\bullet=\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftarrow}
are analogous.
We let
S_{n,\bullet}:=\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
,
S:=\mathsf{Prop}_{n}
and, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we let
S_{i}:=\mathsf{Prop}_{n}\setminus\{p_{i}\}
. Then, we define:
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}:=\{T(S)\}
,
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}:=\{T(S_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
, and
\phi_{n,\bullet}:=p_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge p_{n}
.

That way, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, to distinguish
T(S_{i})
and
T(S)
, an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
needs to be such that
p_{i}\in\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)
, by Lemma
67
. Hence, if an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
separates
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
and
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
, we have
\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)
. Consider now any two positive and negative sets
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N}
of
(n,S_{n,\bullet})
-proper structures. Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
. Consider any formula
\phi\in\mathsf{ATL}(\mathsf{Prop}^{n},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},0)
and
\psi:=\phi\wedge\phi_{n,\bullet}
. We have
T(S)\models\phi_{n,\bullet}
and for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we have
T(S_{i})\not\models\phi_{n,\bullet}
. Furthermore, since
\phi
does not use negations, we also have
T(S)\models\phi
. In addition, by definition of
S_{n,\bullet}
, for all
(n,S_{n,\bullet})
-proper structures
T
, we have
T\models\phi_{n,\bullet}
. Overall, we obtain that
\phi
accepts
\mathcal{P}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}
if and only if
\psi
accepts
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.

•

Assume now that
\bullet=\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}
, the case
\bullet=\prescript{\neg}{}{\lor}
is analogous. We let
\phi^{1}:=p_{1}
and, for all
2\leq i\leq n
, we let
\phi^{i}:=\phi^{i-1}\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;p_{i}
. Let us first prove by induction on
1\leq i\leq n
that, for all
S\subseteq\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{i}\}
, letting
\tilde{S}^{i}:=S\cup\{p_{i+1},\ldots,p_{n}\}
, we have that
T(S)\models\phi^{i}
and
T(\tilde{S}^{i})\models\phi^{n}
have the same truth value if and only if
i
and
n
have the same parity. This obviously holds for
i=n
. Assume now that it holds for some
2\leq i\leq n
. Let
S\subseteq\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{i-1}\}
. We have
\phi^{i}:=\phi^{i-1}\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;p_{i}\equiv\neg\phi^{i-1}\lor\neg p_{i}
. Since
p_{i}\in\tilde{S}^{i-1}
, and
\mathsf{Prop}(\phi^{i-1})\subseteq\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{i-1}\}
we have
T(\tilde{S}^{i-1})\models\phi^{i}
if and only if
T(S\cup\{p_{i}\})\models\phi^{i}
if and only if
T(S)\not\models\phi^{i-1}
. By our induction hypothesis, we have that
T(S\cup\{p_{i}\})\models\phi^{i}
and
T(\widetilde{S\cup\{p_{i}\}}^{i})\models\phi^{n}
have the same truth value if and only if
i
and
n
have the same parity. We deduce that
T(S)\models\phi^{i-1}
and
T(\widetilde{S}^{i-1})\models\phi^{n}
have the same truth value if and only if
i-1
and
n
have the same parity, since
\widetilde{S\cup\{p_{i}\}}^{i}=\tilde{S}^{i-1}
. In fact, the property holds for all
1\leq i\leq n
.

Let us now show by induction on
1\leq i\leq n
that there is some
S_{i},S_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{i}\}
such that
S_{i}\cup\{p_{i}\}=S_{i}^{\prime}\cup\{p_{i}\}
, and
T(\tilde{S_{i}}^{i})\models\phi^{n}
while
T(\tilde{S_{i}^{\prime}}^{i})\not\models\phi^{n}
. We have
T(\{p_{1}\})\models\phi^{1}
and
T(\emptyset)\not\models\phi^{1}
. Hence, we can conclude that this property holds for
i=1
with what we have proved above. Assume now that it holds for some
1\leq i-1\leq n-1
. We have
\phi^{i}:=\phi^{i-1}\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;p_{i}
. We let
S:=S_{i-1}
if
n
and
i-1
have the same parity, and
S:=S_{i-1}^{\prime}
otherwise. That way, with our above result and the induction hypothesis, we know that
T(S)\models\phi^{i-1}
. Then, for all
S_{i},S_{i}^{\prime}\in\{S\cup\{p_{i}\},S\}
such that
S_{i},\neq S_{i}^{\prime}
, we have that the truth value of
T(S_{i})\models\phi^{i}
and
T(S_{i}^{\prime})\models\phi^{i}
are different. We can then conclude with our above result. In fact, this property holds for all
1\leq i\leq n
.

We can now finally define the formula and structures that we consider. We let
\phi_{n,\bullet}:=\phi^{n}
and
S_{n,\bullet}:=S_{n}\subseteq\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}
. Furthermore, we let
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}:=\{T(\tilde{S_{i}}^{i}\cup\{p,\bar{p}\})\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
, and
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}:=\{T(\tilde{S_{i}^{\prime}}^{i}\cup\{p,\bar{p}\})\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
.

That way, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, to distinguish
T(\tilde{S_{i}}^{i}\cup\{p,\bar{p}\})
and
T(\tilde{S_{i}^{\prime}}^{i}\cup\{p,\bar{p}\})
, an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
needs to be such that
p_{i}\in\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)
, since
S_{i}\cup\{p_{i}\}=S_{i}^{\prime}\cup\{p_{i}\}
and by Lemma
67
. Hence, if an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
separates
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
and
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
, we have
\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)
. Consider now any two positive and negative sets
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N}
of
(n,S_{n,\bullet})
-proper structures. Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
. Consider any formula
\phi\in\mathsf{ATL}(\mathsf{Prop}^{n},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},0)
and
\psi:=\phi\prescript{\neg}{}{\wedge}\;\phi_{n,\bullet}\equiv\neg\phi\lor\neg\phi_{n,\bullet}
. Let
1\leq l\leq n
. We have
\phi_{n,\bullet}\models T(\tilde{S_{i}}^{i})
and
\phi\models T(\tilde{S_{i}}^{i})
. Therefore,
\psi\not\models T(\tilde{S_{i}}^{i})
. However,
\phi_{n,\bullet}\not\models T(\tilde{S_{i}^{\prime}}^{i})
. Therefore,
\psi\models T(\tilde{S_{i}^{\prime}}^{i})
. Hence,
\psi
accepts
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
and rejects
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
. In addition, by definition of
S_{n,\bullet}
, for all
(n,S_{n,\bullet})
-proper structures
T
, we have
T\models\phi_{n,\bullet}
. Overall, we obtain that
\phi
accepts
\mathcal{P}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}
if and only if
\psi
accepts
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.

•

Assume now that
\bullet=\;\Leftrightarrow
. The case
\bullet=\prescript{\neg}{}{\Leftrightarrow}
is analogous.
We let
S_{n,\bullet}:=\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}
,
S:=\mathsf{Prop}_{n}
and, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we let
S_{i}:=\mathsf{Prop}_{n}\setminus\{p_{i}\}
. Then, we define:
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}:=\{T(S)\}
,
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}:=\{T(S_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
, and
\phi_{n,\bullet}:=p_{1}\Leftrightarrow\ldots\Leftrightarrow p_{n}
.

That way, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, to distinguish
T(S_{i})
and
T(S)
, an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
needs to be such that
p_{i}\in\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)
, by Lemma
67
. Hence, if an
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula
\phi
separates
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
and
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
, we have
\mathsf{Prop}_{n}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{Prop}(\phi)
. Consider now any two positive and negative sets
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N}
of
(n,S_{n,\bullet})
-proper structures. Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
. Consider any formula
\phi\in\mathsf{ATL}(\mathsf{Prop}^{n},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},0)
and
\psi:=\phi\Leftrightarrow\phi_{n,\bullet}
. We have
T(S)\models\phi_{n,\bullet}
and for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we have
T(S_{i})\not\models\phi_{n,\bullet}
. Furthermore, since
\phi
does not use negations, we also have
T(S)\models\phi
, and
T(S_{i})\models\phi
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Thus,
\psi
accepts
\mathcal{A}_{n,\bullet}
and rejects
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bullet}
. In addition, by definition of
S_{n,\bullet}
, for all
(n,S_{n,\bullet})
-proper structures
T
, we have
T\models\phi_{n,\bullet}
. Thus, we have that
\phi
accepts
\mathcal{P}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}
if and only if
\psi
accepts
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.

∎

4.3
\mathsf{CTL}
learning

We start with
\mathsf{CTL}
learning. We consider two cases: with and without the next operator
\lX
. In the former case, the learning problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete, in the latter it is
\mathsf{NL}
-complete.

4.3.1
With the next operator
\lX

The goal of this subsection is to show the theorem below.

Theorem 68
.

Consider a set
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
of unary temporal operators and assume that
\lX\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
. Then, for all sets
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the decision problem
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.

For the remainder of this subsection, we consider a set
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Un}}^{\mathsf{}}
of unary temporal operators and assume that
\lX\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
.

Overview of the reduction.

We follow the steps described in Section
4.2.1
. However, Step
a)
was already taken care of in the previous section. This lets us focus on
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas using only unary operators (and a single proposition). First of all, we define Kripke structures that prevent the use of the
\lF
and
\lG
operators, as well as negations. Hence, the only operators remaining are
\forall\lX
and
\exists\lX
. Our idea is that now a
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula of size
l+1
is entirely defined by a subset
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
: such a subset defines the
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula
\phi(l,H)
in which, for all
i\in[1,\ldots,l]
, the
i
-th operator of
\phi
is
\exists\lX
if and only if
i\in H
.

On the other hand, a subset
C\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
defines a positive Kripke structure
K^{l,C}
that is defined as a sequence of
l+1
states

\{q_{1}^{\mathsf{lose}},\ldots,q_{l+1}^{\mathsf{lose}}\}
such that no
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula of the shape
\phi(l,H)
can satisfy the state
q_{l+1}^{\mathsf{lose}}
. Furthermore, all states
q_{i}^{\mathsf{lose}}
are such that
q_{i+1}^{\mathsf{lose}}\in\mathsf{Succ}(q_{i}^{\mathsf{lose}})
. However, some states
q_{i}^{\mathsf{lose}}
can branch out to the winning state
q^{\mathsf{win}}
(that all
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas of the shape
\phi(l,H)
satisfy). This occurs for those indices
i\in[1\ldots,l]
such that
i\in C
. In fact, with such a definition, we obtain that
K^{l,C}\models\phi(l,H)
if and only if
H\cap C\neq\emptyset
.

The final step that we take is to define, for
k\leq l
, a negative Kripke structure
K_{\exists>k}^{l}
that is satisfied by the
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula
\phi(l,H)
if and only if
\phi(l,H)
uses at least
k+1
times the operator
\exists\lX
, which is equivalent to
|H|>k
. The Kripke structure
K_{\exists>k}^{l}
has
k+2
levels, from the bottom up:

•

with a single starting state at the bottommost level;

•

where the formula
p
is satisfied in no state of the bottom
k+1
levels, but it is satisfied in the only (self-looping) state
q^{\mathsf{win}}
of the topmost level;

•

where every state of the bottom
k+1
levels has a successor one level higher.

Formal definitions and proofs.

Let us now formally define the Kripke structures that we will use in the reduction. We first define the reduction for
n=0
(i.e. when no binary operator is allowed) and prove its correctness. We then use it to exhibit a reduction for arbitrary
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

We start with the Kripke structures that ensure that the candidate formulas are of a specific shape.

Definition 69
.

Given
l\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
we define the two Kripke structures
K^{\mathcal{P}}_{l}
and
K^{\mathcal{N}}_{l}
that can be seen in Figures
2
and
3
.

Figure 2:
The Kripke structure
K^{\mathcal{P}}_{l}
.

Figure 3:
The Kripke structure
K^{\mathcal{N}}_{l}
.

Let us now consider the Kripke structures
K^{l,C}
that encode a subset of
C\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
.

Definition 70
.

Given some
l\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
and
C\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
, we define the Kripke structure
K_{(l,C)}=\langle Q,I,\{p,\bar{p}\},\pi,\mathsf{Succ}\rangle
where:

•

Q:=\{q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{1},\ldots,q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{l+1},q^{\mathsf{win}}\}
;

•

I:=\{q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{1}\}
;

•

for all
1\leq i\leq l
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Succ}(q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{i}):=\begin{cases}\{q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{i+1},q^{\mathsf{win}}\}&\text{ if }i\in C\\
\{q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{i+1}\}&\text{ if }i\notin C\end{cases}

Furthermore,
\mathsf{Succ}(q^{\mathsf{win}}):=\{q^{\mathsf{win}}\}
and
\mathsf{Succ}(q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{l+1}):=\{q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{l+1}\}
.

•

\pi(q^{\mathsf{win}}):=\{p\}
and, for all
q\in Q\setminus\{q^{\mathsf{win}}\}
, we have
\pi(q):=\{\bar{p}\}
.

An example of such a construction is depicted in Figure
4
.

Figure 4:
The Kripke structure
K_{(5,\{2,5\})}
.

Finally, we also consider a Kripke structure that prevents from using too many
\exists
operators.

Definition 71
.

Given some
l\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
and
k\leq l
, we define the Kripke structure
K_{\exists>k}^{l}=\langle Q,I,\{p,\bar{p}\},\pi,\mathsf{Succ}\rangle
where:

•

Q:=\{q^{\mathsf{win}}\}\cup\{q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{i,j}\mid 0\leq i\leq k,\;i+1\leq j\leq l+1\}
;

•

I:=\{q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{0,1}\}
;

•

For all
0\leq i\leq k,\;i+1\leq j\leq l
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Succ}(q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{i,j}):=\begin{cases}\{q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{i,j+1},q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{i+1,j+1}\}&\text{ if }i\leq k-1\\
\{q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{i,j+1},q^{\mathsf{win}}\}&\text{ if }i=k\end{cases}

For all
0\leq i\leq k
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Succ}(q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{i,l+1}):=\{q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{i,l+1}\}

and
\mathsf{Succ}(q^{\mathsf{win}}):=\{q^{\mathsf{win}}\}
.

•

\pi(q^{\mathsf{win}}):=\{p\}
and, for all
q\in Q\setminus\{q^{\mathsf{win}}\}
, we have
\pi(q):=\bar{p}
.

An example of such a Kripke structure is depicted in Figure
5
.

Figure 5:
The Kripke structure
K^{5}_{\exists>2}
.

We can finally define the reduction from the hitting set problem that we consider.

Definition 72
.

Consider an instance
(l,C,k)
of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
. We consider:

•

\mathsf{Prop}_{0}=\{p,\bar{p}\}
as set of propositions;

•

\mathcal{P}:=\{K_{(l,C_{i})}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}\cup\{K^{\mathcal{P}}_{l}\}
;

•

\mathcal{N}:=\{K^{l}_{\exists>k},K_{l}^{\mathcal{N}}\}
;

•

B:=l+1
.

Then, we define the input
\mathsf{In}^{\mathsf{CTL}(\lX),0}_{(l,C,k)}:=(\mathsf{Prop},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N},B)
.

This reduction satisfies the lemma below.

Lemma 73
.

The input
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
if and only if
\mathsf{In}^{\mathsf{CTL}(\lX),0}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
if and only if
\mathsf{In}^{\mathsf{CTL}(\lX),0}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\cup\{\lF,\lG\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
.

We start with the formal definition below
\phi(l,H)
-formulas.

Definition 74
.

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
. A
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula
\phi\in\mathsf{CTL}(\{p,\bar{p}\},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\cup\{\lF,\lG\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
is an
\lX^{l}
-formula if
\phi\in\mathsf{CTL}(\{p,\bar{p}\},\{\lX\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
and
|\phi|=l+1
.

The
\lX^{l}
-formulas differ by the indices where
\exists
and
\forall
quantifiers appear. For all
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
, we let
\phi(l,H)
denote the
\lX^{l}
-formula:

\phi(l,H):=Q_{1}\lX\ldots Q_{l}\lX p

where, for all
i\in[1,\ldots,l]
, we have:

\displaystyle Q_{i}:=\begin{cases}\exists&\text{ if }i\in H\\
\forall&\text{ if }i\in[1,\ldots,l]\setminus H\end{cases}

Let us show that we can restrict ourselves to
\lX^{l}
-formulas by considering the Kripke structures
K_{l}^{\mathcal{P}},K_{l}^{\mathcal{N}}
.

Lemma 75
.

Consider some
l\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
and a
\mathsf{CTL}
formula
\phi\in\mathsf{CTL}(\{p,\bar{p}\},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
of size at most
l+1
. The formula
\phi
accepts
K_{l}^{\mathcal{P}}
and rejects
K_{l}^{\mathcal{N}}
if and only if
\phi
is an
\lX^{l}
-formula.

Proof.

First of all, note that, for all
1\leq i\leq l+1
, the states
q_{i}^{\mathcal{P}}
and
q_{i+1}^{\mathcal{N}}
satisfy exactly the same
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas. Then, we show by induction on
0\leq i\leq l
the property
\mathcal{P}(i)
:

•

for all
1\leq j\leq l-i
, any
\mathsf{CTL}(\{p,\bar{p}\},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
-formula of size at most
i+1
cannot distinguish the states
q_{j}^{\mathcal{P}}
and
q_{j}^{\mathcal{N}}
;

•

a
\mathsf{CTL}(\{p,\bar{p}\},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
-formula of size at most
i+1
distinguishes the states
q_{l+1-i}^{\mathcal{P}}
and
q_{l+1-i}^{\mathcal{N}}
if and only if it is an
\lX^{i}
-formula (in which case, it accepts
q_{l+1-i}^{\mathcal{P}}
and rejects
q_{l+1-i}^{\mathcal{N}}
).

The property
\mathcal{P}(0)
straightforwardly holds.

Assume now that the property
\mathcal{P}(i)
holds for some
0\leq i\leq l-1
. Consider a
\mathsf{CTL}(\{p,\bar{p}\},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
-formula
\phi
of size at most
i+2
. First of all, if
\phi
is of size at most
i+1
, then
\mathcal{P}(i)
gives that for all
1\leq j\leq l+1-(i+1)=l-i
, the formula
\phi
does not distinguish the states
q_{j}^{\mathcal{P}}
and
q_{j}^{\mathcal{N}}
. Assume now that
|\phi|=i+2
. Consider any
1\leq j\leq l-i
. There are several cases.

•

Assume that
\phi=\neg\phi^{\prime}
, in which case
|\phi^{\prime}|=i+1
. By
\mathcal{P}(i)
,
\phi^{\prime}
does not distinguish the states
q_{j}^{\mathcal{P}}
and
q_{j}^{\mathcal{N}}
, thus
\phi
does not either.

•

Assume that
\phi=\mathsf{Q}\;\lF\phi^{\prime}
, with
\mathsf{Q}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
, in which case
|\phi^{\prime}|=i+1
. Assume that
q_{j}^{\mathcal{P}}\models\phi
. Then, there is some
j\leq r\leq l+1
such that
q_{r}^{\mathcal{P}}\models\phi^{\prime}
, which is equivalent to
q_{r+1}^{\mathcal{N}}\models\phi^{\prime}
, and thus
q_{j}^{\mathcal{N}}\models\phi
. On the other hand, assume that
q_{j}^{\mathcal{N}}\models\phi
, in which case there is some
j\leq r\leq l+2
such that
q_{r}^{\mathcal{N}}\models\phi^{\prime}
. If
r\geq j+1
, then we have
r-1\geq j
and
q_{r-1}^{\mathcal{P}}\models\phi^{\prime}
, and thus
q_{j}^{\mathcal{P}}\models\phi
. Otherwise,
r=j
. Since, by
\mathcal{P}(i)
, we have that
\phi^{\prime}
does not distinguish
q_{j}^{\mathcal{P}}
and
q_{j}^{\mathcal{N}}
, then it follows that we also have
q_{j}^{\mathcal{P}}\models\phi^{\prime}
, and thus
q_{j}^{\mathcal{P}}\models\phi
. Hence, in any case the formula
\phi
does not distinguish
q_{j}^{\mathcal{P}}
and
q_{j}^{\mathcal{N}}
.

•

Assume that
\phi=\mathsf{Q}\;\lG\phi^{\prime}
, with
\mathsf{Q}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
. This case is analogous to the previous one.

•

Assume finally that
\phi=\mathsf{Q}\;\lX\phi^{\prime}
, with
\mathsf{Q}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
, in which case
|\phi^{\prime}|=i+1
. If
j<l-i
, then
j+1\leq l-i
, and thus, by
\mathcal{P}(i)
,
\phi^{\prime}
does not distinguish the two states
q_{j+1}^{\mathcal{P}}
and
q_{j+1}^{\mathcal{N}}
. Hence,
\phi
does not distinguish the two states
q_{j}^{\mathcal{P}}
and
q_{j}^{\mathcal{N}}
. If
j=l-i
, then
j+1=l+1-i
, hence
\phi^{\prime}
distinguishes the states
q_{j+1}^{\mathcal{P}}
and
q_{j+1}^{\mathcal{N}}
if and only if
\phi^{\prime}
is an
\lX^{i}
-formula (in which case it accepts
q_{j+1}^{\mathcal{P}}
and rejects
q_{j+1}^{\mathcal{N}}
), which is equivalent to
\phi
being an
\lX^{i+1}
-formula (in which case it accepts
q_{j}^{\mathcal{P}}
and rejects
q_{j}^{\mathcal{N}}
).

Therefore, the property
\mathcal{P}(i+1)
holds. In fact, it holds for all
0\leq i\leq l
. The lemma is then given by
\mathcal{P}(l)
.
∎

We deduce as a corollary.

Corollary 76
.

The input
\mathsf{In}^{\mathsf{CTL}(\lX),0}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
if and only if
\mathsf{In}^{\mathsf{CTL}(\lX),0}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\cup\{\lF,\lG\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
.

Proof.

This is straightforward consequence of Lemma
73
: if a formula
\phi
accepts
\mathcal{P}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}
, then it accepts
K_{l}^{\mathcal{P}}
and rejects
K_{l}^{\mathcal{N}}
. Thus, if its size is at most
l+1
, it only uses the operator
\lX\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
.
∎

Let us now consider at which conditions the
\mathsf{CTL}

\lX^{l}
-formulas
\phi(l,H)
accepts the Kripke structure
K_{(l,C)}
.

Lemma 77
.

Consider some
l\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
and
H,C\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
. The
\mathsf{CTL}

\lX^{l}
-formula
\phi(l,H)
accepts the Kripke structure
K_{(l,C)}
if and only if
H\cap C\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Let
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
. We have
\phi(l,H)=Q_{1}\lX\ldots Q_{l}\lX p
. For all
1\leq i\leq l+1
, we let
H_{i}:=H\cap[i,\ldots,l]
,
\phi_{i}(H):=Q_{i}\lX\ldots Q_{l}\lX p
. In particular,
\phi_{l+1}(H)=p
and
\phi_{1}(H)=\phi(l,H)
.

Let us show by induction on
l+1\geq i\geq 1
the property
\mathcal{P}(i)
:
q_{i}^{\mathsf{lose}}\models\phi_{i}(H)
if and only if
H_{i}\cap C\neq\emptyset
. The property
\mathcal{P}(l+1)
straightforwardly holds since
\pi(q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{l+1})=\emptyset
and
H_{l+1}=\emptyset
.

Assume now that
\mathcal{P}(i)
holds for some
l+1\geq i\geq 2
. We have
\phi_{i-1}(H):=Q_{i-1}\lX\phi_{i}(H)
with
Q_{i-1}=\exists
if and only if
i-1\in H
. Then:

•

Assume that
H_{i-1}\cap C\neq\emptyset
. If we have
H_{i}\cap C\neq\emptyset
, then by
\mathcal{P}(i)
,
q_{i}^{\mathsf{lose}}\models\phi_{i}(H)
. Since
q^{\mathsf{win}}\models\phi_{i}(H)
and
\mathsf{Succ}(q_{i-1}^{\mathsf{lose}})\subseteq\{q_{i}^{\mathsf{lose}},q^{\mathsf{win}}\}
, it follows that
q_{i-1}^{\mathsf{lose}}\models\phi_{i-1}(H)
(regardless of whether
Q_{i-1}=\exists
or
Q_{i-1}=\forall
). Otherwise, it must be that
i-1\in H\cap C
. Thus, we have
Q_{i-1}=\exists
and
q^{\mathsf{win}}\in\mathsf{Succ}(q_{i-1}^{\mathsf{lose}})
. Since
q^{\mathsf{win}}\models\phi_{i}(H)
, it follows that
q_{i-1}^{\mathsf{lose}}\models\phi_{i-1}(H)
.

•

Assume now that
H_{i-1}\cap C=\emptyset
. It follows that
q_{i}^{\mathsf{lose}}\not\models\phi_{i}(H)
by
\mathcal{P}(i)
, since
H_{i}\cap C=\emptyset
. There are two cases:

–

Assume that
i-1\notin H
. In that case,
Q_{i-1}=\forall
. Furthermore,
q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{i}\in\mathsf{Succ}(q_{i-1}^{\mathsf{lose}})
and
q_{i}^{\mathsf{lose}}\not\models\phi_{i}(H)
. Hence,
q_{i-1}^{\mathsf{lose}}\not\models\phi_{i-1}(H)
.

–

Assume that
i-1\notin C
. In that case,
\mathsf{Succ}(q_{i-1}^{\mathsf{lose}})=\{q^{\mathsf{lose}}_{i}\}
and
q_{i}^{\mathsf{lose}}\not\models\phi_{i}(H)
. Hence,
q_{i-1}^{\mathsf{lose}}\not\models\phi_{i-1}(H)
.

Therefore, the property
\mathcal{P}(i-1)
holds. In fact,
\mathcal{P}(i)
holds for all
1\leq i\leq l+1
. The lemma is then given by
\mathcal{P}(1)
.
∎

In addition, for a
\mathsf{CTL}

\lX^{l}
-formula
\phi(l,H)
not to accept the Kripke structure
K^{l}_{\exists>k}
, it must have not too much existential quantifiers, as stated below.

Lemma 78
.

Consider some
l\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
,
k\leq l
and
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
. The
\mathsf{CTL}

\lX^{l}
-formula
\phi(l,H)
accepts the Kripke structure
K^{l}_{\exists>k}
if and only if
|H|>k
.

Proof.

Let
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
. As in the proof of Lemma
77
, we have
\phi(l,H)=Q_{1}\lX\ldots Q_{l}\lX p
and, for all
1\leq i\leq l+1
, we let
H_{i}:=H\cap[i,\ldots,l]
and
\phi_{i}(H):=Q_{i}\lX\ldots Q_{l}\lX p
. Thus,
\phi_{l+1}(H)=p
and
\phi_{1}(H)=\phi(H)
. Furthermore, for all
k+1\geq i\geq 0
,
l+1\geq j\geq i+1
, we let:

\displaystyle q(i,j):=\begin{cases}q_{(i,j)}^{\mathsf{lose}}&\text{ if }i<k+1\\
q^{\mathsf{win}}&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

Note that, for all
k\geq i\geq 0
,
l\geq j\geq i+1
, we have
\mathsf{Succ}(q(i,j))=\{q(i,j+1),q(i+1,j+1)\}
.

Let us show by induction on
k+1\geq i\geq 0
the property
\mathcal{P}(i)
: for all
l+1\geq j\geq i+1
,
q(i,j)\models\phi_{j}(H)
if and only if
|H_{j}|>k-i
.

The property
\mathcal{P}(k+1)
states that for all
k+2\leq j\leq l+1
,
q(k+1,j)=q^{\mathsf{win}}\models\phi_{j}(H)
if and only if
|\emptyset|>-1
. This straightforwardly holds since
\phi_{j}(H)
is a
\neg
-free formula.

Assume now that the property
\mathcal{P}(i)
holds for some
k+1\geq i\geq 1
. Let us show by induction on
l+1\geq j\geq i
the property
\mathcal{Q}(j)
:
q(i-1,j)\models\phi_{j}(H)
if and only if
|H_{j}|>k-i+1
. The property
\mathcal{Q}(l+1)
straightforwardly holds since
q(i-1,l+1)=q_{i-1,l+1}^{\mathsf{lose}}\not\models\phi_{l+1}(H)
and
H_{l+1}=\emptyset
. Assume now that the property
\mathcal{Q}(j)
holds for some
l+1\geq j\geq i+1
. We have
\phi_{j-1}(H):=Q_{j-1}\lX\phi_{j}(H)
with
Q_{j-1}=\exists
if and only if
j-1\in H
.

•

Assume that
|H_{j-1}|>k-i+1
. If we have
j-1\in H
, then
Q_{j-1}=\exists
and
|H_{j}|>k-i
. Therefore, by
\mathcal{P}(i)
, we have
q(i,j)\models\phi_{j}(H)
. Since
q(i,j)\in\mathsf{Succ}(q(i-1,j-1))
, it follows that
q(i-1,j-1)\models\phi_{j-1}(H)
.

On the other hand, if
j-1\notin H
, then
|H_{j}|>k-i+1>k-i
. Hence, by
\mathcal{P}(i)
, we have
q(i,j)\models\phi_{j}(H)
and by
\mathcal{Q}(j)
, we have
q(i-1,j)\models\phi_{j}(H)
. Since
\mathsf{Succ}(q(i-1,j-1))=\{q(i,j),q(i-1,j)\}
, it follows that
q(i-1,j-1)\models\phi_{j-1}(H)
.

•

Assume now that
|H_{j-1}|\leq k-i+1
. If we have
j-1\in H
, then
|H_{j}|\leq k-i<k-i+1
. Hence, by
\mathcal{P}(i)
, we have
q(i,j)\not\models\phi_{j}(H)
and by
\mathcal{Q}(j)
, we have
q(i-1,j)\not\models\phi_{j}(H)
. Since
\mathsf{Succ}(q(i-1,j-1))=\{q(i,j),q(i-1,j)\}
, it follows that
q(i-1,j-1)\not\models\phi_{j-1}(H)
.

On the other hand, if
j-1\notin H
, then
Q_{j-1}=\forall
and
|H_{j}|\leq k-i+1
. Therefore, by
\mathcal{Q}(j)
, we have
q(i-1,j)\not\models\phi_{j}(H)
. Since
q(i-1,j)\in\mathsf{Succ}(q(i-1,j-1))
, it follows that
q(i-1,j-1)\not\models\phi_{j-1}(H)
.

Hence,
\mathcal{Q}(j-1)
holds. In fact, for all
l+1\geq j\geq i
,
\mathcal{Q}(j-1)
holds and therefore
\mathcal{P}(i-1)
holds. In fact,
\mathcal{P}(i)
holds for all
0\leq i\leq k+1
. The lemma is then given by
\mathcal{P}(0)
applied with
j=1
.
∎

The proof of Lemma
73
is now direct.

Proof.

Assume that
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
. Consider a hitting set
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
with
|H|\leq k
. We let
\phi:=\phi(l,H)\in\mathsf{CTL}(\{p,\bar{p}\},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
since
\lX\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
. We have
|\phi|=l+1
. By Lemma
75
,
\varphi
accepts
K_{l}^{\mathcal{P}}
and rejects both
K_{l}^{\mathcal{N}}
. By Lemma
78
,
\phi
rejects
K^{l}_{\exists>k}
. Consider now some
1\leq i\leq n
. Since
H
is a hitting set, we have
H\cap C_{i}\neq\emptyset
. Hence, by Lemma
77
,
\phi(l,H)
accepts the Kripke structure
K_{(l,C_{i})}
. In follows that the
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula
\phi
accepts
\mathcal{P}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}
. Hence,
\mathsf{In}^{\mathsf{CTL}(\lX),0}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of the
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,n)
decision problem.

Assume now that
\mathsf{In}^{\mathsf{CTL}(\lX),0}_{(l,C,k)}
is a positive instance of the decision problem
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
. Consider a
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula
\phi\in\mathsf{CTL}(\{p,\bar{p}\},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\setminus\{\neg\},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
of size at most
l+1
that accepts
\mathcal{P}
and rejects
\mathcal{N}
. Since
\phi
accepts
K_{l}^{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{P}
and rejects
K_{l}^{\mathcal{N}}\in\mathcal{N}
, it follows by Lemma
75
that
\phi
is an
\lX^{l}
-formula. Let
H\subseteq[1,\ldots,l]
be such that
\phi=\phi(l,H)
. Since
\phi
rejects
K^{l}_{\exists>k}\in\mathcal{N}
, it follows, by Lemma
78
, that
|H|\leq k
. Consider some
1\leq i\leq n
. Since
\phi
accepts
K_{(l,C_{i})}\in\mathcal{P}
, it follows, by Lemma
77
, that
H\cap C_{i}\neq\emptyset
. Therefore,
H
is a hitting set and
(l,C,k)
is a positive instance of the hitting set problem
\mathsf{Hit}
.

We conclude with Corollary
76
.
∎

Theorem
68
follows.

Proof.

This is direct consequence of Lemma
73
, Corollary
76
, of the fact that the instance
\mathsf{In}^{\mathsf{CTL}(\lX),0}_{(l,C,k)}
can be computed in logarithmic space from
(l,C,k)
, and of Theorem
65
.
∎

4.3.2
Without the next operator
\lX

In the previous subsection, we have seen that the
\mathsf{CTL}
learning problem with a bounded amount of binary operator is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete. However, as can be seen, the proof of
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness heavily relies on the use of the operator
\lX
. In this subsection, we focus on the
\mathsf{CTL}
learning problem where the operator
\lX
is not allowed anymore. We show that this decision problem in
\mathsf{NL}
, as stated in the theorem below.

Theorem 79
.

For all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\{\lF,\lG,\neg\}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
decision problem is in
\mathsf{NL}
. If
\lF\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
or
\lG\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
, then the
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
decision problem is
\mathsf{NL}
-complete.

Proving that the decision problem is
\mathsf{NL}
-hard is rather straightforward and will handled as a second step. Let us first show that this decision problem is in
\mathsf{NL}
.

To prove this, we are going to proceed similarly to the
\mathsf{LTL}
case (except that this case is more involved), i.e. we first consider
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas that do not use binary operators at all, and then consider the case of
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas using binary operators.

Let us first tackle the case of
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas using no binary operators. Our goal is to successively restrict the set of
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas that is sufficient to consider. More precisely, we show that sequentially using several operators in a row is useless. We start by showing that using twice in a row either of the operators
\lF
or
\lG
is useless. The formal statement that we give below is in the context of
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas, more general than
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas, because we will use this statement later on in this paper.

Lemma 80
.

Consider some
k\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
,
A\subseteq A^{\prime}\subseteq[1,\ldots,k]
. Let
\phi
be any
\mathsf{ATL}
-formula with
k
agents. We have:

\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lG\phi\equiv\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lG\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi\equiv\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi

and dually

\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lF\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi\equiv\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lF\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lF\phi\equiv\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi

Proof.

We start with the operator
\lG
.

•

By definition of the globally operator
\lG
, we have
\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lG\phi\implies\phi
, hence
\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lG\phi\implies\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi
. Also, we have
\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lG\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi\implies\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi
.

•

Let us show that
\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi\implies\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi
. Consider a concurrent game structure
C
and any state
q\in Q
. Assume that
q\models\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi
. Consider a strategy profile
s^{A}\in\mathsf{S}_{A}
for the coalition of agents
A
such that, for all
\rho\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q,s^{A})
, we have
\rho\models\lG\phi
. We claim that we have for all
\rho\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q,s^{A})
,
\rho\models\lG\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi
. Indeed, consider any
\rho\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q,s^{A})
and
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
. Let
\tilde{s}^{A}
be a strategy profile for the coalition
A
that coincides with
s^{A}
after
\rho[:i-1]
. That is, for all
a\in A
and
\theta\in Q^{+}
, we have
\tilde{s}_{a}^{A}(\theta):=s_{a}^{A}(\rho[:i-1]\cdot\theta)
. Consider then any
\theta\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(\rho[i],\tilde{s}^{A})
. By definition of the strategy profiles
\tilde{s}^{A}
and
s^{A}
, we have that
\rho[:i-1]\cdot\theta\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q,s^{A})
. Therefore, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, we have
(\rho[:i-1]\cdot\theta)[j]\models\phi
. Thus, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, we have
\theta[j]\models\phi
. That is,
\theta\models\lG\phi
. In fact,
\rho[i]\models\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi
, which holds for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
. Thus,
\rho\models\lG\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi
. We obtain that
\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi\implies\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi
.

Since
A\subseteq A^{\prime}
, we have, for all
\mathsf{ATL}
-formulas
\phi^{\prime}
,
\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi^{\prime}\implies\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lG\phi
. We can conclude that
\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi\implies\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lG\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi
and
\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\phi\implies\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lG\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lG\phi
.

We now turn to the operator
\lF
, with dual arguments.

•

By definition of the eventually operator
\lF
, we have
\phi\implies\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lF\phi
, hence
\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi\implies\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lF\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lF\phi
. Also, we have
\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi\implies\langle\langle A\rangle\rangle\lF\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi
.

•

Consider now two coalitions
A_{1},A_{2}\subseteq A^{\prime}
, a concurrent game structure
C
and any state
q\in Q
. Assume that
q\models\langle\langle A_{1}\rangle\rangle\lF\langle\langle A_{2}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi
. Let us show that
q\models\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi
. Consider a strategy profile
s^{A_{1}}\in\mathsf{S}_{A_{1}}
for the coalition of agents
A_{1}
such that, for all
\rho\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q,s^{A_{1}})
, we have
\rho\models\lF\langle\langle A_{2}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi
. We let
\mathsf{Already}(\langle\langle A_{2}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi):=\{\rho\in Q^{+}\cup Q^{\omega}\mid\exists i_{\rho}\in\mathbb{N}_{1},\;i_{\rho}\leq|\rho|,\;\rho[i_{\rho}]\models\langle\langle A_{2}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi\}
. By definition of the strategy
s^{A_{1}}
, we have
\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q,s^{A_{1}})\subseteq\mathsf{Already}(\langle\langle A_{2}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi)
. Then, for all such

\rho\in\mathsf{Already}(\langle\langle A_{2}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi)
, we let
i_{\rho}\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
be the least index such that
\rho[i_{\rho}]\models\langle\langle A_{2}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi
and
\theta_{\rho}\in Q^{*}\cup Q^{\omega}
be such that
\rho=\rho[:i_{\rho}-1]\cdot\theta_{\rho}
. We also let
s^{\rho,A_{2}}\in\mathsf{S}_{A_{2}}
be a strategy profile for the coalition
A_{2}
such that for all
\theta\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(\rho[i_{\rho}],s^{\rho,A_{2}})
, we have
\theta\models\lF\phi
.

We now define the strategy profile
s^{A^{\prime}}
such that, for all
\rho\in Q^{+}
:

\displaystyle s^{A^{\prime}}(\rho)\text{ coincides with }\begin{cases}s^{A_{1}}(\rho)\text{ on }A_{1}&\text{ if }\rho\notin\mathsf{Already}(\langle\langle A_{2}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi)\\
s^{\rho,A_{2}}(\theta_{\rho})\text{ on }A_{2}&\text{ if }\rho\in\mathsf{Already}(\langle\langle A_{2}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi)\end{cases}

where, for all coalitions of agents
X,X_{1},X_{2}
, we say that a strategy
s\in\mathsf{S}_{X_{1}}
coincides with another strategy
s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{S}_{X_{2}}
on
X\subseteq X_{1}\cap X_{2}
if, for all
a\in X
, we have
s_{a}=s^{\prime}_{a}
.

We claim that for all
\rho\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q,s^{A^{\prime}})
,
\rho\models\lF\phi
. Indeed, consider any
\rho\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q,s^{A^{\prime}})
. By definition, on finite paths not in
\mathsf{Already}(\langle\langle A_{2}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi)
, the strategy
s^{A^{\prime}}
coincides with the strategy
s^{A_{1}}
on
A_{1}
. Since
\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q,s^{A_{1}})\subseteq\mathsf{Already}(\langle\langle A_{2}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi)
, it follows that
\rho\in\mathsf{Already}(\langle\langle A_{2}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi)
. Therefore, we have
\rho=\rho[:i_{\rho}-1]\cdot\theta_{\rho}
. In addition, by definition of the strategy
s^{A^{\prime}}
, we have
\theta_{\rho}\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(\rho[i_{\rho}],s^{\rho,A_{2}})
. By choice of the strategy
s^{\rho,A_{2}}
, this implies
\theta_{\rho}\models\lF\phi
. It follows that
\rho\models\lF\phi
. Therefore, for all
\rho\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q,s^{A^{\prime}})
, we have
\rho\models\lF\phi
. Thus,
q\models\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi
. Hence, we have proved that
\langle\langle A_{1}\rangle\rangle\lF\langle\langle A_{2}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi\implies\langle\langle A^{\prime}\rangle\rangle\lF\phi
.

We obtain the desired implications since
A,A^{\prime}\subseteq A^{\prime}
.

We obtain the equivalences for both operators
\lF
and
\lG
.
∎

Let us now come back to the more restrictive context of
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas where there are only two different strategic quantifiers:
\langle\langle\emptyset\rangle\rangle
(i.e.
\forall
) and
\langle\langle\{1\}\rangle\rangle
(i.e.
\exists
). To properly express the above lemma in this context, we define below the notion of dominating quantifiers (between
\exists
and
\forall
) used with the operators
\lF
and
\lG
.

Definition 81
.

For all
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
, we define
\mathsf{Dom}_{\lF}(Q_{1},Q_{2})\in\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}
and
\mathsf{Dom}_{\lG}(Q_{1},Q_{2})\in\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Dom}_{\lF}(Q_{1},Q_{2}):=\begin{cases}\exists&\text{ if }Q_{1}=\exists\text{ or }Q_{2}=\exists\\
\forall&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{Dom}_{\lG}(Q_{1},Q_{2}):=\begin{cases}\forall&\text{ if }Q_{1}=\forall\text{ or }Q_{2}=\forall\\
\exists&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

Note that
\mathsf{Dom}_{\lG}
is the dual of the operator
\mathsf{Dom}_{\lF}
, as stated in the observation below.

Observation 82
.

For all
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula
\phi
and
Q,Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
, we have:

\displaystyle\neg\mathsf{Dom}_{\lF}(Q_{1},Q_{2})\lF

\displaystyle\phi\equiv\mathsf{Dom}_{\lG}(\neg Q_{1},\neg Q_{2})\lG\neg\phi

(1)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\implies Q\lF\phi

(2)

\displaystyle Q\lG\phi

\displaystyle\implies\phi

(3)

where
\neg\forall:=\exists
and
\neg\exists:=\forall
.

Proof.

The equivalence
(\ref{eqn:equiv_neg_F_G})
is a straightforward consequence of the following equivalences, for
\phi^{\prime}
any
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula:
\neg\forall\lG\phi^{\prime}\equiv\exists\lF\neg\phi^{\prime}
,
\neg\exists\lF\phi^{\prime}\equiv\forall\lG\neg\phi^{\prime}
,
\neg\exists\lG\phi^{\prime}\equiv\forall\lF\neg\phi^{\prime}
and
\neg\forall\lF\phi^{\prime}\equiv\exists\lG\neg\phi^{\prime}
.
The implications
(\ref{eqn:simple_F})
and
(\ref{eqn:simple_G})
come from the definition of the operators
\lF
and
\lG
and the fact that, in all Kripke structures
K
, for all states
q\in Q
and for all
\rho\in\mathsf{Out}^{Q}(q)
, we have
\rho[1]=q
.
∎

We can now state below the corollary of Lemma
80
with
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas. (Which justifies the terminology defined above of
dominating
quantifiers.)

Corollary 83
.

Let
\phi
be any
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula and
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
. We have:

Q_{1}\lG Q_{2}\lG\phi\equiv Q_{2}\lG Q_{1}\lG\phi\equiv\mathsf{Dom}_{\lG}(Q_{1},Q_{2})\lG\phi

and dually

Q_{1}\lF Q_{2}\lF\phi\equiv Q_{2}\lF Q_{1}\lF\phi\equiv\mathsf{Dom}_{\lF}(Q_{1},Q_{2})\lF\phi

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Lemma
80
and the fact that
\forall
stands for
\langle\langle\emptyset\rangle\rangle
and
\exists
stands for
\langle\langle\{1\}\rangle\rangle
.
∎

We deduce the corollary below stating equivalences over
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas alternating the
\lF
and
\lG
operators.

Corollary 84
.

Let
\phi
be any
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula and
Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3},Q_{4}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
. Let
Q_{\lF}:=\mathsf{Dom}_{\lF}(Q_{1},Q_{3})
and
Q_{\lG}:=\mathsf{Dom}_{\lG}(Q_{2},Q_{4})
. Assume that
Q_{1}=Q_{\lF}
and
Q_{4}=Q_{\lG}
. Then:

Q_{1}\lF Q_{2}\lG Q_{3}\lF Q_{4}\lG\phi\equiv Q_{\lF}\lF Q_{\lG}\lG\phi

Dually, letting
Q_{\lG}:=\mathsf{Dom}_{\lG}(Q_{1},Q_{3})
and
Q_{\lF}:=\mathsf{Dom}_{\lF}(Q_{2},Q_{4})
, if
Q_{1}=Q_{\lG}
and
Q_{4}=Q_{\lF}
, then:

Q_{1}\lG Q_{2}\lF Q_{3}\lG Q_{4}\lF\phi\equiv Q_{\lG}\lG Q_{\lF}\lF\phi

Proof.

We prove the first equivalence, the second is then given by Observation
82
(Eq. (
1
)).

By Corollary
83
and Observation
82
(Eq. (
2
)), we have:

\displaystyle Q_{\lG}\lG\phi\equiv Q_{2}\lG Q_{4}\lG\phi\implies Q_{2}\lG Q_{3}\lF Q_{4}\lG\phi

Since
Q_{\lF}=Q_{1}
, we have:

Q_{\lF}\lF Q_{\lG}\lG\phi\implies Q_{1}\lF Q_{2}\lG Q_{3}\lF Q_{4}\lG\phi

On the other hand, we have by Observation
82
(Eq. (
3
)):

Q_{2}\lG Q_{3}\lF Q_{4}\lG\phi\implies Q_{3}\lF Q_{4}\lG\phi

Therefore, by Corollary
83
and since
Q_{\lG}=Q_{4}
:

Q_{1}\lF Q_{2}\lG Q_{3}\lF Q_{4}\lG\phi\implies Q_{1}\lF Q_{3}\lF Q_{4}\lG\phi\equiv Q_{\lF}\lF Q_{4}\lG\phi\implies Q_{\lF}\lF Q_{\lG}\lG\phi

We obtain the desired equivalence.
∎

We deduce that it is useless to use quantifiers between
\exists\lF
and
\forall\lG
.

Lemma 85
.

Let
\phi
be any
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula and
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG)^{*}
be a sequence of quantifiers. We have:

\exists\lF\;\mathsf{Qt}\;\forall\lG\phi\equiv\exists\lF\forall\lG\phi

and

\forall\lG\;\mathsf{Qt}\;\exists\lF\phi\equiv\forall\lG\exists\lF\phi

Proof.

We prove the result by induction on the size of
\mathsf{Qt}
. If
\mathsf{Qt}
if the empty sequence, both equivalences are straightforward. Assume now that both equivalences hold for all sequences
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG)^{*}
of size at most
k
, for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider a sequence of quantifiers
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG)^{k+1}
. Let us consider the sequence
\exists\lF\;\mathsf{Qt}\;\forall\lG
, the arguments are similar for the other one. If
k+1
is odd, then the sequences
\exists\lF\;\mathsf{Qt}\;\forall\lG
features an operator
\lF
or
\lG
used twice in a row. By Corollary
83
, there is some
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG)^{*}
with
|\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}|\leq k
such that
\exists\lF\;\mathsf{Qt}\;\forall\lG\phi\equiv\exists\lF\;\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\;\forall\lG\phi
. We can then apply our induction hypothesis.

Assume now that
k+1
is even and that the sequence
\exists\lF\;\mathsf{Qt}\;\forall\lG
does not feature an operator
\lF
or
\lG
used twice in a row. If
|\mathsf{Qt}|=2
, we can apply Corollary
84
. Assume now that
|\mathsf{Qt}|\geq 4
. Let us us write
\mathsf{Qt}
as
\mathsf{Qt}=\mathsf{T}_{1}\cdots\mathsf{T}_{2n}
where
\mathsf{T}_{i}\in\{\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG\}
for all
1\leq i\leq 2n
. By assumption, for all even
1\leq i\leq 2n
,
\mathsf{T}_{i}
is an
\lF
operator, whereas for all odd
1\leq i\leq 2n
,
\mathsf{T}_{i}
is an
\lG
operator. We denote by
Q_{i}

the
\exists
or
\forall
quantifier associated with
\mathsf{T}_{i}
. There are three cases:

•

Assume that
Q_{1}=\exists
. Then, we necessarily have
\mathsf{Dom}_{\lG}(Q_{1},Q_{3})=Q_{3}
, and
\mathsf{Dom}_{\lF}(\exists,Q_{2})=\exists
. Therefore, by Corollary
84
, we have

\exists\lF\;\mathsf{Qt}\;\forall\lG\phi\equiv\exists\lF\;\mathsf{T}_{3}\cdots\mathsf{T}_{2n}\;\forall\lG\phi
. We can then apply our induction hypothesis.

•

Similarly, assume that
Q_{2n}=\forall
. Then, we necessarily have
\mathsf{Dom}_{\lF}(Q_{2(n-1)},Q_{2n})=Q_{2(n-1)}
, and
\mathsf{Dom}_{\lG}(Q_{2n-1},\forall)=\forall
. Therefore, by Corollary
84
, we have

\exists\lF\;\mathsf{Qt}\;\forall\lG\phi\equiv\exists\lF\;\mathsf{T}_{1}\cdots\mathsf{T}_{2(n-1)}\;\forall\lG\phi
. We can then apply our induction hypothesis.

•

Otherwise, we have
\mathsf{T}_{1}=\forall\lG
and
\mathsf{T}_{2n}=\exists\lF
. Therefore, by our induction hypothesis and Corollary
84
,
\exists\lF\;\mathsf{Qt}\;\forall\lG\phi=\exists\lF\;\mathsf{T}_{1}\cdots\mathsf{T}_{2n}\;\forall\lG\phi\equiv\exists\lF\;\forall\lG\;\exists\lF\;\forall\lG\phi\equiv\exists\lF\forall\lG\phi
.

Thus the equivalence holds for all sequences of operators
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG)^{*}
of size
k+1
. The lemma follows.
∎

We also deduce that it is useless to use too long sequences of operators next to
\exists\lF\;\forall\lG
.

Lemma 86
.

Let
\phi
be any
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula. For all
\mathsf{Qt},\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG)^{*}
, there is
\mathsf{Qt}_{s},\mathsf{Qt}_{s}^{\prime}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG)^{*}
of size at most
3
such that:

\mathsf{Qt}\;\exists\lF\forall\lG\;\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\phi\equiv\mathsf{Qt}_{s}\;\exists\lF\forall\lG\;\mathsf{Qt}_{s}^{\prime}\;\phi

Similarly, for all
\mathsf{Qt},\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG)^{*}
, there is
\mathsf{Qt}_{s},\mathsf{Qt}_{s}^{\prime}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG)^{*}
of size at most 3 such that:

\mathsf{Qt}\;\forall\lG\exists\lF\;\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\phi\equiv\mathsf{Qt}_{s}\;\forall\lG\exists\lF\;\mathsf{Qt}_{s}^{\prime}\;\phi

Proof.

We prove the result for
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG)^{*}
with the sequence
\exists\lF\;\forall\lG
, the three other cases are analogous.

We prove by induction on the size of
\mathsf{Qt}
that there is
\mathsf{Qt}_{s}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG)^{*}
of size at most
3
such that:
\mathsf{Qt}\;\exists\lF\forall\lG\;\phi\equiv\mathsf{Qt}_{s}\;\exists\lF\forall\lG\;\;\phi
. This obviously holds if
\mathsf{Qt}
is of size at most 3. Assume now that it holds for all sizes
i\leq k
for some
k\geq 3
. Consider a sequence
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG)^{k+1}
. We let
\mathsf{Qt}
be equal to
\mathsf{Qt}=\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{2}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{3}\cdot\mathsf{T_{4}}
where, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we have
\mathsf{T}_{i}\in\{\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG\}
. We assume that in the sequence
\mathsf{Qt}\;\exists\lF\forall\lG
, no
\lF
or
\lG
operator appears twice in a row, otherwise we can apply Corollary
83
and our induction hypothesis. For
1\leq i\leq 4
, we let
Q_{i}
denote the
\exists
or
\forall
quantifier associated with the operator
\mathsf{T}_{i}
. There are two cases:

•

If
\mathsf{T}_{2}=\forall\lG
or
\mathsf{T}_{3}=\exists\lF
, Corollary
84
gives that
\mathsf{Qt}\cdot\exists\lF\forall\lG\phi\equiv\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{2}\cdot\exists\lF\forall\lG\phi
. We can then apply our induction hypothesis to
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{2}\cdot\exists\lF\forall\lG\phi
since
|\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{2}|\leq k
.

•

Otherwise, we have both
\mathsf{Dom}_{\lF}(Q_{1},Q_{3})=Q_{1}
and
\mathsf{Dom}_{\lG}(Q_{2},Q_{4})=Q_{4}
. Therefore, by Corollary
84
, we have

\mathsf{Qt}\cdot\exists\lF\forall\lG\phi\equiv\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{4}\cdot\exists\lF\forall\lG\phi
. We can then apply our induction hypothesis to
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{4}\exists\lF\forall\lG\phi
since
|\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{4}|\leq k
.

Overall, the equivalences also holds for all sequences
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG)^{k+1}
. The lemma follows.
∎

Finally, let us consider the case where at most one of the two operators
\exists\lF
or
\forall\lG
is used.

Lemma 87
.

Let
\phi
be any
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula. For all
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG)^{*}
, there is
\mathsf{Qt}_{s}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG)^{*}
of size at most
5
such that
\mathsf{Qt}\;\phi\equiv\mathsf{Qt}_{s}\;\phi
.

Similarly, for all
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\exists\lG,\forall\lG,\exists\lF)^{*}
, there is
\mathsf{Qt}_{s}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG)^{*}
of size at most
5
such that
\mathsf{Qt}\;\phi\equiv\mathsf{Qt}_{s}\;\phi
.

Proof.

We prove the result for the first case, the second one is analogous. We prove the result by induction on the size of
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG)^{*}
that there is
\mathsf{Qt}_{s}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG)^{*}
of size at most
5
such that
\mathsf{Qt}\;\phi\equiv\mathsf{Qt}_{s}\;\phi
. This obviously holds if
\mathsf{Qt}
is of size at most 5. Assume now that it holds for all sizes
i\leq k
of
\mathsf{Qt}
for some
k\geq 5
. Consider a sequence
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG)^{k+1}
. We denote
\mathsf{Qt}
as follows
\mathsf{Qt}=\mathsf{T}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{2}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{3}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{4}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{5}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{6}\cdot\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}
. We assume that in
\mathsf{Qt}
, no
\lF
or
\lG
operator appears twice in a row, otherwise we can apply Corollary
83
and our induction hypothesis. For
1\leq i\leq 6
, we let
Q_{i}
denote the
\exists
or
\forall
quantifier associated with the operator
\mathsf{T}_{i}
. There are two cases.

•

Assume that there is some
1\leq i\leq 6
such that
\mathsf{T}_{i}=\exists\lF
. If
i\leq 3
,
then we have
\mathsf{Dom}_{\lF}(Q_{i},Q_{i+2})=Q_{i}
and
\mathsf{Dom}_{\lG}(Q_{i+1},Q_{i+3})=Q_{i+3}
since
Q_{i+1}=Q_{i+3}=\exists
. Thus, by Corollary
84
, for all CTL-formulas
\phi^{\prime}
, we have
\mathsf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{i+1}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{i+2}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{i+3}\cdot\phi^{\prime}\equiv\mathsf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{i+3}\cdot\phi^{\prime}
. We can then apply our induction hypothesis to conclude. If
i\geq 4
, then we have
\mathsf{Dom}_{\lF}(Q_{i-2},Q_{i})=Q_{i}
and
\mathsf{Dom}_{\lG}(Q_{i-3},Q_{i-1})=Q_{i-3}
since
Q_{i-3}=Q_{i-1}=\exists
. Thus, by Corollary
84
, for all CTL-formulas
\phi^{\prime}
, we have
\mathsf{T}_{i-3}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{i-2}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{i-1}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{i}\cdot\phi^{\prime}\equiv\mathsf{T}_{i-3}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{i}\cdot\phi^{\prime}
. We can then apply our induction hypothesis to conclude.

•

Otherwise, we have
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\forall\lF,\exists\lG)^{k+1}
and thus we can apply Corollary
84
and our induction hypothesis to conclude.

Therefore, the result holds for sequences
\mathsf{Qt}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG)^{k+1}
. The lemma follows.
∎

Overall, we obtain the lemma below, bounding the size of CTL-formulas (using only unary operators) that is sufficient to consider.

Lemma 88
.

There is a bound
B\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\{\neg,\lF,\lG\}
, for all CTL-formulas
\phi=\mathsf{Qt}\cdot\phi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
, there is a sequence of
\mathsf{CTL}
-operators
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}
such that
\phi^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot\phi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
,
|\mathsf{Qt}|\leq B
, and
\phi\equiv\phi^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

Let us first consider any
\phi=\mathsf{Qt}\cdot\phi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset,0)
with
\mathsf{Qt}
as follows
\mathsf{Qt}=\mathsf{T}_{1}\cdots\mathsf{T}_{n}
where, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we have
\mathsf{T}_{i}\in\{\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG\}
. There are two cases:

•

Assume that
\phi
uses both operators
\exists\lF
and
\forall\lG
. Consider
1\leq i<j\leq n
such that
\mathsf{T}_{i},\mathsf{T}_{j}\in\{\exists\lF,\forall\lG\}
and
\mathsf{T}_{i}\neq\mathsf{T}_{j}
. By Lemma
85
, we have
\phi\equiv\mathsf{T}_{1}\cdots\mathsf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{j}\cdots\mathsf{T}_{n}\;\phi^{\prime}
. Furthermore, by Lemma
86
, there is
\mathsf{Qt}_{s},\mathsf{Qt}_{s}^{\prime}\in(\exists\lF,\forall\lF,\exists\lG,\forall\lG)^{*}
of size at most
3
such that:
\mathsf{T}_{1}\cdots\mathsf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{j}\cdots\mathsf{T}_{n}\;\phi^{\prime}\equiv\mathsf{Qt}_{s}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{j}\cdot\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}_{s}\;\phi
. We let
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}:=\mathsf{Qt}_{s}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathsf{T}_{j}\cdot\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}_{s}\;\phi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\emptyset)
. We have
|\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}|\leq 8
and
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot\phi^{\prime}\equiv\mathsf{Qt}\cdot\phi^{\prime}
.

•

Assume
\phi
does not use both operators
\exists\lF
or
\forall\lG
. Then, by Lemma
87
, there is
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}
of size at most 5 such that
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot\phi^{\prime}\equiv\mathsf{Qt}\cdot\phi^{\prime}
.

Thus, in both cases, there is a sequence of
\mathsf{CTL}
-operators
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}
of size at most 8such that
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot\phi^{\prime}\equiv\mathsf{Qt}\cdot\phi^{\prime}
with at most 8 quantifiers.

Then, it is straightforward to handle the cases where the sequence of quantifiers
\mathsf{Qt}
uses negations, since
we have the equivalence, for all
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas
\phi
,
\lG\phi\equiv\neg\lF\neg\phi
and
\lF\phi\equiv\neg\lG\neg\phi
.
∎

We obtain a similar statement with formulas that can use (a bounded amount of) binary operators.

Lemma 89
.

For all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\{\neg,\lF,\lG\}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a bound
B_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all CTL-formulas
\phi\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
, there is a
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula
\phi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
such that
|\phi^{\prime}|\leq B_{n}
and
\phi^{\prime}\equiv\phi
.

Proof.

Let
B
denote the bound from Lemma
88
.
We proceed by induction on
n
(note that the bound
B_{n}
depends on
n
). For the case
n=0
, it suffices to consider
B_{0}:=B+1
. Assume now that it holds for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We let
B_{n+1}:=2B_{n}+B+1\geq B_{n}
. Consider a formula
\phi\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n+1)\setminus\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
. This formula can be written as
\phi=\mathsf{Qt}\cdot(\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2})
where
\mathsf{Qt}
is a sequence of unary operators,
\bullet\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
is a binary operator and
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
. By our induction hypothesis, there are formulas
\phi_{1}^{\prime},\phi_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
such that
|\phi_{1}^{\prime}|,|\phi_{2}^{\prime}|\leq B_{n}
and
\phi_{1}\equiv\phi_{1}^{\prime}
,
\phi_{2}\equiv\phi_{2}^{\prime}
. In addition, by Lemma
88
, there is a sequence of operators
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}
of size at most
B
such that, for all
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas
\phi^{\prime}
, we have
\mathsf{Qt}\cdot\phi^{\prime}\equiv\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot\phi^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot(\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2})\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n+1)
. Overall, we have
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot(\phi_{1}^{\prime}\bullet\phi_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n+1)
with
|\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot(\phi_{1}^{\prime}\bullet\phi_{2}^{\prime})|\leq B_{n+1}
and
\mathsf{Qt}^{\prime}\cdot(\phi_{1}^{\prime}\bullet\phi_{2}^{\prime})\equiv\mathsf{Qt}\cdot(\phi_{1}\bullet\phi_{2})
. Hence, our inductive property holds also for
n+1
. The lemmas follows.
∎

We can establish that deciding the learning
\mathsf{CTL}
decision problem without the operator
\lX
can be done in non-deterministic logarithmic space.

Lemma 90
.

For all
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\{\lF,\lG,\neg\}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the problem
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
can be decided in non-deterministic logarithmic space.

Proof.

By Immerman-Szelepcsényi’s theorem
[
20
]
, we have
\mathsf{NL}=\mathsf{coNL}
. In other words, any problem that can decided by a logarithmic-space Turing machine using only existential (TM) states can also be decided by a logarithmic-space Turing machine using only universal (TM) states.

Now, consider the bound
B_{n}
from Lemma
89
. We let
\widetilde{\mathsf{CTL}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
denote a set of
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula structures, i.e.
\mathsf{CTL}
-formulas where the propositions are left unspecified. Each one of these formula structure can be seen, for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, as functions
\mathsf{Prop}^{k}\rightarrow\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
, for all sets of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
. The set
\widetilde{\mathsf{CTL}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
corresponds to the set of all
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula structures such that, for all non-empty sets of propositions
\mathsf{Prop}
, there are propositions in
\mathsf{Prop}
specifying them such that the obtained formula is in
\mathsf{CTL}(\mathsf{Prop},\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
and of size at most
B_{n}
. Note that the set
\widetilde{\mathsf{CTL}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
is finite. For all
\widetilde{\phi}\in\widetilde{\mathsf{CTL}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
, let us exhibit an
\mathsf{NL}
-algorithm
\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\widetilde{\phi}}
that decides, given the propositions specifying the formula structure
\widetilde{\phi}
and a state in a Kripke structure, whether that state satisfies the obtained
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula.

First, checking that a state satisfies a proposition is straightforward. Assume now that we have designed an
\mathsf{NL}
-algorithm
\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\widetilde{\phi}}
for a
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula structure
\widetilde{\phi}
. The case of the formula structure
\widetilde{\psi}=\neg\widetilde{\phi}
is straightforward since
\mathsf{NL}=\mathsf{coNL}
. Consider now the formula structure
\widetilde{\psi}=\exists\lF\widetilde{\phi}
. Consider any propositions specifying
\widetilde{\psi}
into a
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula
\psi
(and therefore
\widetilde{\phi}
into a
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula
\phi
). Checking that a state
q
satisfies
\psi
amounts to guessing a path from
q
of size at most
|Q|
and checking, with a logarithmic space Turing machine using only existential (TM) states, that it satisfies
\phi
(by calling the algorithm
\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\widetilde{\phi}}
). This induces an
\mathsf{NL}
-algorithm. Consider now the formula
\widetilde{\psi}=\forall\lF\widetilde{\phi}
. Since
\mathsf{NL}=\mathsf{coNL}
, there is a
\mathsf{coNL}
-algorithm
\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\widetilde{\phi}}^{\prime}
for the formula structure
\widetilde{\phi}
. As above, consider any propositions specifying
\widetilde{\psi}
into a
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula
\psi
(and therefore
\widetilde{\phi}
into a
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula
\phi
). Then, checking that a state
q
satisfies the formula
\psi
amounts to exploring, with universal (TM) states, paths of lengths at most
|Q|
from
q
and check that we encounter a state that satisfies
\varphi
(by calling
\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\widetilde{\phi}}^{\prime}
). This induces a
\mathsf{coNL}
-algorithm, and therefore an
\mathsf{NL}
-algorithm as well. The arguments are similar for the operators
\exists\lG
and
\forall\lG
. Furthermore, consider a formula structure
\widetilde{\psi}=\widetilde{\phi_{1}}\bullet\widetilde{\phi_{2}}
for some binary operator
\bullet\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}
, and assume that we have designed
\mathsf{NL}
-algorithms
\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\widetilde{\phi_{i}}},\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\neg\widetilde{\phi_{i}}}
for the
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula structures
\widetilde{\phi_{i}},\neg\widetilde{\phi_{i}}
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
. In that case, we may rewrite
\bullet
in disjunctive normal form (for instance
x_{1}\Leftrightarrow x_{2}\equiv(x_{1}\wedge x_{2})\lor(\neg x_{1}\wedge\neg x_{2})
). Then, an
\mathsf{NL}
-algorithm
\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\widetilde{\psi}}
could guess which clause to satisfy and run at most two of the algorithms
\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\widetilde{\phi_{1}}},\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\widetilde{\phi_{2}}},\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\neg\widetilde{\phi_{1}}},\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\neg\widetilde{\phi_{2}}}
. That way, we obtain an
\mathsf{NL}
-algorithm
\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\widetilde{\psi}}
.

Overall, we have an
\mathsf{NL}
-algorithm for all
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula structures (and their negations since
\mathsf{NL}=\mathsf{coNL}
) in
\widetilde{\mathsf{CTL}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
. Let us now design an
\mathsf{NL}
-algorithm for the
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
decision problem. That algorithm could do the following, on an input
(\mathsf{Prop},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N},B)
:

1.

Loop over all
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula structures
\widetilde{\phi}
in
\widetilde{\mathsf{CTL}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)

2.

Letting
k
be the number of unspecified propositions in
\widetilde{\phi}
, loop over all tuples
t
in
\mathsf{Prop}^{k}
for which the
\mathsf{CTL}
-formula obtained from
\widetilde{\phi}
with
t
has size at most
\min(B_{n},B)

3.

Loop over:

•

all starting states
q
of all positive structures and run the
\mathsf{NL}
-algorithm
\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\widetilde{\phi}}
on
t
and
q

•

all negative structures
K\in\mathcal{N}
, guess a starting state
q
in
K
and run the
\mathsf{NL}
-algorithm
\mathsf{SatKripke}_{\neg\widetilde{\phi}}
on
t
and
q

Accept if all calls return positive answers

If the algorithm does not accept the input, then it rejects it. The first loop is entered a bounded number of times (independent of the input). Since of formula structures in
\widetilde{\mathsf{CTL}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
use at most
n
occurrences of binary operators, the number
k
of unspecified variables in
\widetilde{\phi}
is at most
2^{n}
, thus the second loop is entered at most
|\mathsf{Prop}|^{2^{n}}
times, which is polynomial in
|\mathsf{Prop}|
. Therefore, the algorithm that we have designed above runs in non-deterministic logarithmic space. Furthermore, it decides the problem
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
by Lemma
89
.

∎

\mathsf{NL}
-hardness

To establish
\mathsf{NL}
-hardness, we are going to exhibit a reduction from the problem of reachability in a graph. We introduce in the definition below that decision problem as a sub-case of a more general decision problem of reachability in a two-player game, that we will use in the next section.

Definition 91

(Reachability Game)
.

We denote by
\mathsf{Reach}
the following decision problem:

•

Input: two propositions
p,\bar{p}
and a
\{1,2\}
-game structure
G
on
\{p,\bar{p}\}
, such that all state in
G
satisfy exactly one of the two propositions
p
and
\bar{p}
, and with a single starting state;

•

Output: yes iff
q\models\langle\langle\{1\}\rangle\rangle\lF p
.

Similarly, we consider the decision problem 1-
\mathsf{Reach}
, where the game structure taken as input is in fact a
\{1\}
-game structure, i.e. it is a Kripke structure.

Theorem 92

(
[
19
]
,
[
27
]
)
.

The decision problem
\mathsf{Reach}
is
\mathsf{P}
-complete and the decision problem 1-
\mathsf{Reach}
is
\mathsf{NL}
-complete under logarithmic space reductions.

Proof.

The decision problem
\mathsf{Reach}
is equivalent to solving two-player reachability games, which is
\mathsf{P}
-complete
[
19
]
. The decision problem 1-
\mathsf{Reach}
is equivalent to solving reachability in a graph (“s-t connectivity”), which is
\mathsf{NL}
-complete
[
27
, Theorem 16.2]
.
∎

Let us define the reduction that we consider.

Definition 93
.

Consider any input
p,\bar{p},K
of the decision problem 1-
\mathsf{Reach}
. We let
K_{\bar{p}}
be a single-state Kripke structure whose only state is labeled by
\{\bar{p}\}
.

We define the inputs
\mathsf{In}^{\mathsf{CTL},\lF}_{(p,\bar{p},K)}:=(\{p,\bar{p}\},\{K\},\{K_{\bar{p}}\},2)
and
\mathsf{In}^{\mathsf{CTL},\lG}_{(p,\bar{p},K)}:=(\{p,\bar{p}\},\{K_{\bar{p}}\},\{K\},2)
of a
\mathsf{CTL}
learning problem.

The definition above satisfies the lemma below.

Lemma 94
.

Consider some set of unary operators
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq\{\lX,\lF,\lG,\neg\}
,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}_{\mathsf{Bin}}^{\mathsf{lg}}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and an input
(p,\bar{p},K)
of the 1-
\mathsf{Reach}
decision problem. Let
\mathsf{H}\in\{\lF,\lG\}
. If
\mathsf{H}\in\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}}
, then the input
(p,\bar{p},K)
is a positive instance of the 1-
\mathsf{Reach}
decision problem if and only if
\mathsf{In}^{\mathsf{CTL},\mathsf{H}}_{(p,\bar{p},K)}
is a positive instance of the
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
decision problem.

Proof.

Let us first consider the case
\mathsf{H}=\lF
. Assume that
(p,\bar{p},K)
is a positive instance of 1-
\mathsf{Reach}
. We let
\varphi:=\exists\lF p
. We have
|\varphi|=2
. Furthermore,
K_{\bar{p}}\not\models\varphi
and, by assumption,
K\models\varphi
. Hence,
\mathsf{In}^{\mathsf{CTL}^{,}\mathsf{F}}_{(p,\bar{p},K)}
is a positive instance of the
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}^{2}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
decision problem.

On the other hand, assume that
\mathsf{In}^{\mathsf{CTL},\mathsf{F}}_{(p,\bar{p},K)}
is a positive instance of the
\mathsf{CTL}_{\mathsf{Learn}}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{t}},\emptyset,\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{l}},n)
decision problem. Consider a separating formula
\varphi
of size at most 2 that accepts the Kripke structure
K
and rejects the Kripke structure
K_{\bar{p}}
. The structures
K
and
K_{\bar{p}}
satisfy the following property: for all states
q
,
q\models p
if and only if
q\models\neg\bar{p}
. Let us show that on these structures, we have
\varphi\implies\exists\lF p
. If
\varphi
uses a negation, then
\varphi=\neg\bar{p}
and
\varphi\equiv p\implies\exists\lF p
on such structures. If
\varphi
